Francis de Sales's controversial sheets for the Calvinists of Thonon (Chablais mission, mid-1590s), published posthumously in 1672; H. B. Mackey's translation, *The Catholic Controversy* (1886; third edition 1909).

**A method better than its era's (1.3).** The Author's General Introduction disowns "a thousand impieties which are attributed to Catholics" and asks readers who have heard one party to "have yet patience to hear the other". It still says "pretended church". **Weight:** a Doctor writing as an author, **no magisterial level**.

**Boss 1 (outline only).** Boss 1 reads Part II, Article III, ch. 12, where de Sales quotes Edmund Campion's *Decem Rationes* 7, a "when did Rome change?" argument: the argument is Campion's, quoted by de Sales, so never attribute Campion's words to de Sales. Its reconstruction, the abuses it commits or avoids, its loci and its ceiling are Boss 1(a)-(b)'s, worked with 1.3's four abuses and the rule in [loci and historical premises](#loci-and-historical-premises).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md)

### Jewel challenge

John Jewel's challenge from Paul's Cross (26 November 1559; repeated, enlarged, in March 1560): prove any of a list of Roman doctrines and practices from the first six hundred years, and he would "give over and subscribe".

**The articles (Coxe's text, ANF 3, Elucidation V to Tertullian's *Prescription*)** include private Masses, communion in one kind, prayer in a tongue the people do not understand, the bishop of Rome as universal bishop, a corporal presence, the elevation and adoration of the sacrament, "only the accidents and shews" remaining, and images set up to be worshipped. The bar: "any one clear or plain clause or sentence" of scripture, an old doctor, an old general council or an example of the primitive Church.

**At full strength (1.3).** The faith and its worship were delivered whole; the age of the Fathers and great councils, which Rome also reveres, wrote enough that anything apostolic would leave one clear sentence; so an article with none is a later addition, and whoever holds it must prove it. One sentence is a deliberately low bar: Tertullian's prescription turned on Rome. An argument from expected record, not bare [silence](#argument-from-silence).

**Standing.** A bishop's sermon, not an Anglican formulary (those are the Thirty-Nine Articles, the Prayer Book and the Ordinal); **no Catholic level**. Thomas Harding answered it in 1564; Jewel replied in 1565. Coxe's claim that it went unanswered is confessional history.

**Where it lands (1.3 P3).** On private Masses and one-kind communion the record bears Jewel out: Trent XXI ch. 2 itself says the use of both species was "not unfrequent" from the beginning. Silence shows those practices not apostolic in that form, not unlawful: communion in one kind **law, no rung** (XXI ch. 2); no divine precept binding all to both kinds (XXI can. 1) and private Masses not unlawful (XXII can. 8), **rung 1 as worded**. The "accidents" article asks for a word Trent avoids. Success yields a probable historical result (locus 10); "therefore false" needs the premise that nothing apostolic can become explicit after 600, the [development](#development-of-doctrine) question. **Trap:** stating it as *sola scriptura* or "Protestants reject the Fathers": Jewel admits doctors, councils and practice as witnesses.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md)

### Encyclical of the Eastern Patriarchs 1848

The synodal reply (May 1848) of the patriarchs of Constantinople, Alexandria, Antioch and Jerusalem, with their synods, to Pius IX's letter to the Easterns.

**Standing.** A synodal letter of four patriarchates: honoured and weighty, not an ecumenical council's act, and not "Orthodoxy defined" (1.1, 1.3); **no Catholic level**. Its circulating English has no recorded translator, so the lessons paraphrase it and quote nothing.

**What the lessons use.** *§17,* the root of the Orthodox account of [reception](#reception): neither patriarchs nor councils could bring in novelties, because the whole body of the Church, the people itself, guards the faith (1.1; 5.3). *§§13-15,* the Orthodox historical case against Rome on the principle that the Fathers and the seven councils are received, not developed (1.3): §13, Chalcedon's canon 28 grounds Rome's rank in the city and is silent about Peter, where a record would be expected ([argument from silence](#argument-from-silence) used properly; 4.2 weighs it); §15, Chalcedon acclaimed "Peter has spoken through Leo" only after examining Leo's letter against Nicaea and Cyril ([cherry-picked Father](#cherry-picked-father)). *§5:* the [filioque](#filioque) has no ground in the Fathers, and the Latin witnesses are adulterations ([manufactured consensus](#manufactured-consensus)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md)

### Loci and historical premises

Where a historical premise sits among Cano's loci, and the ceiling it puts on a conclusion.

**The rule (1.3 P5, from [`fundamental-theology` 5.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-05-the-loci-theologici.md)).** A historical premise is **locus 10** (human history, extrinsic) unless it reports the Fathers teaching (**locus 6**) or the Church's own practice (**locus 3**). A conclusion's note is capped by its weakest load-bearing premise, so an argument standing on an archive claim reaches **probable**, on either side: Jewel's "not apostolic" and a Catholic's "certainly apostolic" alike. The archive answers "who held what, when", never "is it revealed?" (1.3 P1).

**The third corollary: the cap falls on the argument, not on the claim.** If the relic argument fails, the Assumption is still **rung 1** (1950); invocation of the saints stands on Trent XXV and LG 49-50, not on a silence argument. The reverse error promotes a historical conclusion because its doctrine is defined; both confuse the cap on an argument with a claim's level. **Applied (1.3 P1):** a letter arguing from no protest before 500 reaches it only through a locus-10 premise, so it is capped at probable; had it *shown* the Church's universal practice, that would be locus 3. Applying the rule to the de Sales-Campion argument is Boss 1(b)'s work.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Invocation of saints

Asking the saints in glory to pray for us, as one asks a living Christian's prayer.

**Level.** **At least rung 3:** Trent XXV's decree (3-4 December 1563) that it is good and useful to invoke them, and that those who deny it think impiously, has no canon; LG 49-50 restate it. It is presupposed by Trent XXII can. 5 (Masses for obtaining the saints' intercession), **rung 1 as worded**. How the saints hear our prayers: **rung 5** (6.2).

**1.3.** The doctrine stands on those acts, not on a silence argument. Historical datum: Vigilantius, a presbyter from Gaul, wrote that while we live we can pray for one another but after death no one's prayer for another is heard, and Jerome answered him (*Against Vigilantius* 6, 406): an early objector, and positive testimony that the practice was defended, and contested, around 400; not testimony for the apostolic age.

**The opposing texts (6.2), which are not one objection.** *Reformed:* Calvin, *Institutes* III.20.19-27, a theologian's book: the living rightly pray for one another, but of the departed interceding there is "not a word on the subject in Scripture"; the Heidelberg Catechism lists invocation of saints beside idolatry under the first commandment (Q.94); Westminster XXI.2 gives religious worship to God alone, "not to angels, saints, or any other creature", and on the Reformed reading of XXI.3 a request made in prayer to the unseen is itself prayer. *Anglican:* Article XXII calls the "Romish Doctrine" of invoking saints "a fond thing, vainly invented". *Lutheran, and different:* Augsburg XXI keeps the saints' memory for imitation, but "the Scripture teaches not the invocation of saints"; the Apology (XXI.8-10) grants that the saints in heaven pray "for the Church in general" and denies only that invoking them follows, for want of "a command, nor a promise, nor an example"; the Smalcald Articles (II.2) reject it outright. **6.1:** what divides Lutheran and Catholic on Mary is whether one may *ask* the prayer the Apology grants she offers. **6.2's crux:** is invoking a saint an act of religion of a different kind from asking a friend's prayer, or the same kind toward a friend in heaven?

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Transubstantiation

The conversion of the whole substance of the bread and wine into Christ's body and blood, only the species remaining, and the Church's name for that change.

**Level.** **Rung 1** for the change and the term (Trent XIII cans. 1-2, 1551), **not for Aristotle**. Trent says "species", never "accidents", and calls the name suitably and properly, and most aptly, given (Waterworth). Whether "substance" in can. 2 must be read in the *Categories*' sense is **rung 5** (6.4's concession, with [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)).

**In 1.3.** Jewel's article asks the Fathers for "only the accidents and shews", later vocabulary Trent itself avoids: an [anachronism](#anachronism) trap. What would have to be found is the thing, a change of the bread into Christ's body, and that is 6.4's question. **The confessions (6.4),** all of which reject it: the Formula's "under the bread, with the bread, in the bread" marks a union of the bread's *unchanged* substance with the body (SD VII.35), which can. 2's first clause condemns ([sacramental union](#sacramental-union)); Westminster XXIX.7, a [spiritual presence](#spiritual-presence); Article XXVIII calls transubstantiation "repugnant to the plain words of Scripture". **Trap:** making the doctrine sound Aristotelian concedes ground the Church has not conceded.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Pseudo-Isidorian decretals

The Pseudo-Isidorean Decretals: forged papal letters compiled in the Frankish province of Reims, probably in the 840s, routing bishops' cases to Rome. (The heading spells the anchor "Isidorian"; the lessons write "Isidorean".)

**A written-in concession (1.3; 4.2).** Catholic polemic and the Catholic case have leaned on forged documents, and these were used in the service of Roman claims ([`church-history` 3.3](../church-history/lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md); [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-02-the-development-of-papal-claims-read-honestly.md)). **Their limit:** the claims were older than the forgeries, which served them; appeals to Rome are some five centuries older (Sardica, 343 or 344), so "no appeal before the forgeries" is false (4.2 P1). **No authority.**

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Donation of Constantine

A forged grant in which Constantine gives the pope supremacy and rule; exposed by Lorenzo Valla in 1440.

One of the written-in concessions of 1.3 and 4.2: forgeries served the papal claims ([`church-history` 3.3](../church-history/lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md), which dates and sets it). Concede it flatly; it carries **no authority** and is evidence for no rung. A forgery is excellent evidence of what its forger wanted (`church-history` 3.3's rule, 1.3's anachronism check from the historian's side).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Securus judicat orbis terrarum

Augustine, *Against the Letter of Parmenianus* III.4.24: the whole world judges securely that those who divide themselves from the whole world, in whatever part, are not good.

**What it is about (1.3 Ex. 2; text read in [`patristics` 4.4](../patristics/lessons/04-04-augustine-against-the-donatists.md)).** Schism: anyone can see who has separated, though not who is guilty. **Newman's use (*Apologia*, ch. 3, of 1839):** he heard a rule that "decided ecclesiastical questions on a simpler rule than that of Antiquity": the whole Church's settled judgment is "a final sentence against such portions of it as protest and secede".

**1.3's verdict.** Legitimate on schism, inside Augustine's argument. Beyond the text as a rule outranking antiquity in *doctrinal* disputes. And it decides nothing until one has settled *which* communion is the *orbis*: Augustine's world was one empire-wide communion against one province, but in 1839 Christendom was divided three ways, and the Orthodox can turn the sentence on Rome. That is the seat-of-authority question ([two axes](#two-axes)), argued in Module 4. In 5.3, Newman's *Letter to the Duke of Norfolk* invokes the same dictum for a definition the whole body receives, and the same question returns: whose *orbis* judges.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md)

**Against sola scriptura (Module 2)**

### Sola scriptura

Scripture the only infallible rule of faith, read in and with the Church, with creeds, confessions, councils and pastors as real but fallible authorities beneath it.

**Stated as the confessions state it (2.1).** *Lutheran:* the Formula of Concord makes the prophetic and apostolic scriptures "the sole rule and standard" by which all dogmas and teachers are judged, other writings being received only "as witnesses" ([Rule and Norm](#rule-and-norm)). *Reformed:* Westminster I.4-10, with synods that determine controversies "ministerially" (XXXI.3) but "may err" and so are "a help", not the rule (XXXI.4; XXXI.3 in the American numbering); Calvin, *Institutes* I.7; the [Second Helvetic](#second-helvetic-confession) II. *Anglican:* nothing that scripture neither contains nor proves may be required as of faith (Article VI), and the Church has authority in controversies under it (Article XX). The phrase *sola scriptura* occurs in none of these texts. 2.1's analogy: one master standard and rulers calibrated against it, binding in their place and fallible; discarding the rulers is [solo scriptura](#solo-scriptura). On Mathison's map it is Tradition I ([Tradition I and Tradition II](#tradition-i-and-tradition-ii)).

**Levels.** **No act condemns *sola scriptura* by name.** The denial of formal sufficiency: **at least rung 3** (Trent IV; DV 9-10). Whether a named formulation (Westminster I.6, Mathison) falls under Trent IV's anathema: **rung 5**. Scripture and the traditions received with equal reverence: **rung 1 as worded**. The other traditions' texts: **no Catholic level**.

**What the reply must answer (2.1).** (1) Authority's source is shared: Westminster I.4, Calvin I.7.2 and *Dei Filius* ch. 2 all deny the Church confers it. (2) The [canon](#canon-of-scripture) (2.2). (3) [Formal sufficiency](#material-and-formal-sufficiency), not material (2.3). (4) [Clarity](#perspicuity) as Westminster I.7 states it, qualifiers included (2.4). (5) Real, fallible authority: show why it is not enough, never pretend it absent (4.4).

**Concession (2.1):** as the confessions state it, it is not individualism. **Cruxes.** 2.1: whether a rule that fallible bodies must identify and read can be the *sole infallible* rule, its certainty given by the Spirit's witness through it. 2.4: whether a [rule of faith](#rule-of-faith) that needs an authority to apply it is still a rule of faith *alone*. **Later:** on warrant the Marian dogmas are a test case of *sola scriptura*, since their warrant is scripture read in Tradition by the Church, the authority Article VI denies (6.1, 6.3); the saints-and-images dispute returns to it through the rule that God is worshipped only as he commands (Heidelberg Q.96; 6.2).

**What a Catholic may not say (2.1).** Anything against *solo scriptura* under the name *sola*. "Protestants reject tradition" (they reject unwritten additions as a second rule). "Luther invented it" (Tradition I is medieval). "The inward testimony is condemned fideism" ([inward testimony](#inward-testimony-of-the-spirit)). "Trent settled it" (an external critique; and Trent never names *sola scriptura*).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Tradition I and Tradition II

Heiko Oberman's names for two medieval conceptions: Tradition I, single-source, tradition as the Church's handing on and interpreting of scripture; Tradition II, two-source, unwritten apostolic traditions supplying what scripture lacks.

**Oberman** (*The Harvest of Medieval Theology*, 1963; a historian's book): the Reformers took one side of an existing medieval disagreement, so "Luther invented *sola scriptura*" fails. **Mathison's map** (*The Shape of Sola Scriptura*, 2001; a theologian's book, no confessional standing): Tradition 0, [solo scriptura](#solo-scriptura), common among modern evangelicals, never the Reformers'; Tradition I, *sola scriptura*, scripture the sole infallible authority read in the Church under the ancient [rule of faith](#rule-of-faith); Tradition II, which he finds dogmatized at Trent; Tradition III, the magisterium as itself the norm, which he finds in modern Rome. The confessions sit at Tradition I.

**The Catholic placement is contested (2.3).** Trent wrote "and" where its draft had "partly ... partly", so it did not dogmatize Tradition II ([partim-partim](#partim-partim)); a Catholic who holds material sufficiency holds a single-source view of the content and still denies formal sufficiency ([material and formal sufficiency](#material-and-formal-sufficiency)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)

### Rule and Norm

The Formula of Concord's opening section (Epitome, "Of the Summary Content, Rule, and Standard"): scripture the sole rule and only judge; the creeds and the Lutheran confessions witnesses, not judges.

**The text (Triglot, 1921, 2.1's Source).** The prophetic and apostolic scriptures "alone" are "the sole rule and standard according to which all dogmas together with [all] teachers should be estimated and judged"; other writings are received only "as witnesses". The section's last paragraph: the scriptures "the only judge, rule, and standard ... the only test-stone"; the other symbols "are not judges, as are the Holy Scriptures, but only a testimony and declaration of the faith". §2 pledges the Apostles', Nicene and Athanasian creeds as "the unanimous, universal Christian faith"; §3 the Augsburg Confession, its Apology, the Smalcald Articles and Luther's Catechisms.

**Three moves (2.1).** Exclusion ("sole", "alone", "only"; scripture judges teachers as well as doctrines); subordination without discarding; judge set apart from witness: the creeds bind whoever confesses them but never judge beside scripture. **Example (2.1 Ex. 1):** a Lutheran pastor confesses the non-scriptural *homoousios* because the creed is a witness that scripture was rightly read against Arius, binding the pastor who subscribed it; Westminster's route is "good and necessary consequence" (I.6).

**Standing.** Lutheran confession in the Book of Concord, a *norma normata* under scripture; **no Catholic level**. **Traps.** Neither *sola scriptura* nor *norma normans/normata* is in the text (the Triglot's editor F. Bente uses *norma normans* in his introduction). Schaff renders the phrase "the only rule and norm"; the Triglot "the sole rule and standard". The section names its rule without listing the books (2.2). Westminster I.10's "the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scripture" is not the Formula's phrase (2.1 P2).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md)

### Inward testimony of the Spirit

The Reformed teaching that full persuasion of scripture's divine authority comes from the Holy Spirit witnessing by and with the word in the believer's heart.

**The texts.** Westminster I.5: the Church's testimony may move us to "an high and reverent esteem", and scripture's excellencies evidence it, yet our full persuasion of its infallible truth and divine authority "is from the inward work of the Holy Spirit, bearing witness by and with the Word in our hearts". Calvin, *Institutes* I.7.4 (a theologian's book): conviction rests on "the secret testimony of the Spirit". Belgic V: the books are received "not so much because the Church receives and approves them as such, but more especially because the Holy Ghost witnesseth in our hearts" that they are from God. Standing: Presbyterian and continental Reformed standards; **no Catholic level**.

**At the canon (2.2).** The Reformed answer to the crux: certainty does not pass through the recognizer; reception is subordinate, not zero ("not so much"). **The Catholic reply (2.2 P3):** believers who had the witness disagreed about the edge of the list for centuries (Hebrews, James, Revelation; the deuterocanon; Luther's 1522 ranking against Belgic IV), so the witness, given believer by believer, did not deliver one list. The mirror circle: the Reformed believer's certainty also outruns his evidence ([circularity objection](#circularity-objection)); its philosophical twin is Plantinga's warranted belief ([`philosophy-of-religion` 5.2](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/05-02-reformed-epistemology.md)).

**Trap (2.1).** It is not the fideism *Dei Filius* condemns: canons on faith, 3, condemns denying that outward signs make revelation credible, and Westminster I.5 cites evidences; Catholic faith too needs the Spirit's interior help ([`fundamental-theology` 2.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-01-the-act-of-faith.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Self-authenticating canon

The Reformed model on which the canonical books bear the marks of their divine origin and are recognized by believers through the Spirit, rather than authorized by a church's decision.

**Source and standing.** Michael J. Kruger, a Reformed New Testament scholar, *Canon Revisited* (Crossway, 2012), presented as a development of the historic Reformed view: **a scholar's book**, no confessional standing. It is a study of the New Testament books, while the live Catholic-Reformed dispute is the Old Testament deuterocanon. **Ancestors:** Calvin I.7.5 calls scripture *autopistos*, credible of itself (Beveridge: "carrying its own evidence along with it"); Belgic V's books "carry the evidence in themselves"; Westminster I.5. "Self-authenticating" is later wording (a later translator's and Kruger's), never Calvin's in quotation marks.

**Boss 2(b) asks for the model as its defenders state it: outline only.** 2.2 teaches its parts: the rival models it rejects (community-determined, the Roman Catholic among them; historically determined); three attributes of canonical books (divine qualities, corporate reception, apostolic origins); the Spirit's work in recognition; reception's standing as evidence rather than ground; and what it claims to explain that a bare appeal to the Church does not. Read them in 2.2's "The modern restatement".

**Where the Catholic presses (2.2).** At the disputed edge: where reception settled the edge, whose reception? Kruger's corporate reception lets a Reformed reply grant that the confessions sided with the churches' long reception of Hebrews against Luther (2.2 Ex. 1). The slogan "a fallible collection of infallible books", current among Reformed writers, states the price.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Canon of Scripture

The list of books received as inspired scripture and as the rule of faith; on both sides a catalogue that recognizes, not a commission that confers.

**Common ground (2.2 P1).** *Dei Filius* ch. 2: the books are canonical "not because, having been carefully composed by mere human industry, they were afterwards approved by her authority", but because, written by inspiration, they have God for their author (Schaff); Westminster I.4; Calvin I.7.2 (the Church "does not make that authentic which was otherwise doubtful or controverted"). So "who made the list?" is not the disagreement. No book of scripture lists the books of scripture (P2), so every side owes an account of how the edge is known.

**The two disputes.** *Which books:* the twenty-seven New Testament books are common to Catholics, Orthodox and the Reformation confessions; the Old Testament [deuterocanon](#deuterocanon) is disputed. *How certain the recognition is:* the Catholic says the Church's recognition cannot fail when she defines; the Reformed say the books bear their marks, which the Spirit teaches believers to see.

**Each side's list.** *Catholic:* Trent IV, "entire with all their parts", **rung 1** (with *Dei Filius* can. II.4); the full canon known through Tradition, DV 8, **rung 3**; recognition not conferral, *Dei Filius* ch. 2, **at least rung 3**. *Reformed:* Westminster I.2-3 (sixty-six books; the Apocrypha no part of the canon); [Belgic](#belgic-confession) IV-VI. *Anglican:* Article VI. *Lutheran:* **no confessional list**; the Formula names "the prophetic and apostolic Scriptures" without enumerating them; [Luther's 1522 prefaces](#luthers-1522-prefaces) are a theologian's.

**The argument and where it stops (2.2).** Believers with the Spirit's witness disagreed at the edge; where the edge was settled, reception settled it, and reception is a church's act; so an edge held with the certainty of faith needs a recognizer that cannot fail when it defines. On the Wisdom of Solomon the Church's own reception splits (Jerome and Athanasius against Carthage, Augustine and Florence), and history alone yields Calvin's "undecided": Trent answers the shorter list with authority, not with a better history, and that authority needs its own warrant ([circularity objection](#circularity-objection); Module 4). **Crux:** can a recognition carry more certainty than the body that recognizes?

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Material and formal sufficiency

Material: every revealed truth is in scripture, at least implicitly. Formal: scripture is also its own sufficient rule of interpretation, so after the apostles it is the only infallible norm, and every other authority is fallible and answerable to it.

**The analogy (2.3).** A constitution may contain every law (material) without settling its own disputes (formal); yes to the first fits no to the second. [Partim-partim](#partim-partim) denies material sufficiency; both Catholic options deny formal sufficiency.

**The Catholic positions.** Material sufficiency is a legitimate and widely held option, **rung 5** against partim-partim ([`fundamental-theology` 3.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-04-tradition.md)): Yves Congar (*Tradition and Traditions*, 1960-63) held that Catholics may admit it; J. R. Geiselmann argued in the 1950s that Trent's drafting left room for it; Newman called the Catholic-Anglican difference over whether the whole faith is in scripture "merely one of words", Catholics denying only that every article may be "logically proved, independently of the teaching and authority of the Tradition" (*Letter to Pusey*, 1865, §2). The denial of formal sufficiency: **at least rung 3** (Trent IV; *Dei Filius* ch. 2; DV 9-10).

**The confessions' claim is formal, never "me and my Bible" (2.1, 2.3).** Westminster I.6 (the whole counsel "either expressly set down in Scripture, or by good and necessary consequence may be deduced from Scripture"), I.9 ("The infallible rule of interpretation of Scripture is the Scripture itself"), I.10; the Second Helvetic II; the Formula's Rule and Norm; Articles VI and XX. [Perspicuity](#perspicuity) is its working claim (2.4).

**2.3's case in one line:** the New Testament hands on teaching in two modes and as a deposit with keepers who teach; 2 Timothy 3:16-17 says "profitable", not "alone"; the second-century Church settled readings by the [rule of faith](#rule-of-faith), and Vincent joins material sufficiency with formal insufficiency; nothing early says only the text is infallible after the apostles. Each text is weak alone and the case is cumulative; it rests on no claim about Pauline authorship, and it shows formal sufficiency unsupported, not which body holds the interpreting office (Module 4). **Crux (2.3):** can a post-apostolic act of interpretation share the gospel's infallibility without being inspired? The Catholic answer is assistance, not inspiration (*Pastor Aeternus* ch. 4, chapter text, **at least rung 3**; DV 10); the Reformed answer, every synod "may err" (Westminster XXXI.4).

**Traps.** "Catholics say the Bible is incomplete" attacks a view no Catholic is bound to hold. The mirror: "material sufficiency is *the* Catholic view" (it is an option; Vatican II left the question open). Aiming the Catholic reply at material sufficiency instead of formal (2.1).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)

### Perspicuity

The Reformation thesis that scripture is clear enough, in what is necessary for salvation, for the unlearned to understand through the ordinary means: the working claim behind formal sufficiency.

**Westminster I.7 (Schaff).** It opens with a concession, "All things in Scripture are not alike plain in themselves, nor alike clear unto all", whose proof-text in Schaff's footnote is 2 Peter 3:16. Then three qualifiers: **scope**, "those things which are necessary to be known, believed, and observed, for salvation"; **means**, set out "in some place of Scripture or other", for learned and unlearned alike, "in a due use of the [ordinary means](#ordinary-means)"; **measure**, "a sufficient understanding of them". I.6: the Spirit's inward illumination is needed for saving understanding; I.9: a disputed sense is "searched and known by other places that speak more clearly". The Particular Baptists' confession (1677, reissued 1689) repeats the article almost word for word (ch. 1 §7). Standing: Presbyterian subordinate standard; **no Catholic level**. Luther's wider version: [external and internal clarity](#external-and-internal-clarity).

**The Catholic argument from the record (2.4; an argument, no magisterial level).** By its own scope the thesis is tested only by disputes the parties held necessary. They disagreed about what is necessary: Augsburg IX teaches baptism "is necessary to salvation" and condemns the Anabaptists, while Westminster XXVIII.5 denies that grace and salvation are "so inseparably annexed unto it". They disagreed about which texts are plain: the Formula (Epitome VII) condemns treating the words of institution as "obscure expressions, whose meaning must be sought first in other passages of Scripture", the method Westminster I.9 later generalized. They used every ordinary means; inside each tradition a subscribed confession settled the dispute, and between traditions nothing did. Conclusion: not that scripture is obscure, but that a clear text still needs a judge.

**The three replies, granted where true (2.4).** *Never claimed:* the presence and baptism's subjects are not "necessary" on the Reformed and Baptist reading, and Marburg (1529) agreed fourteen articles and most of a fifteenth, disagreeing only on whether the body and blood "are corporally present in the bread and wine" (Schaff, *History* VII §108). *Ordinary means are not "me and my Bible"*. *Not like for like:* Rome agrees by authority, not clarity, and divides where it has not decided (the *de auxiliis* dispute, ended in 1607 by deciding nothing: the schools **rung 5**, the formula **law, no rung**; [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.5](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)). The Preface to the Book of Concord aims its condemnations at doctrines and obstinate teachers, not at those who err "from a certain simplicity of mind" or at whole churches, so condemning a doctrine is not declaring it necessary to salvation (2.4 Ex. 2).

**Levels.** That scripture is obscure: **not taught** (no act says so; DV 25 urges all the faithful to read it). The [rule of interpretation](#rule-of-interpretation): **at least rung 3**. Whether Westminster I.7 falls under a Tridentine anathema: **rung 5**. **Traps.** "The thesis says every verse is plain to every reader" (I.7 denies it, so quoting 2 Peter 3:16 against Westminster quotes its own proof-text; Acts 8:31 too is granted, as the ordinary means at work). "The Church teaches scripture is obscure" (Luther's charge). "Marburg agreed on nothing" or "split on the Eucharist". **Crux (2.4):** is a rule of faith that needs an authority to apply it still a rule of faith *alone*?

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)

### Rule of faith

The norm by which scripture is read and teaching measured: in the second century, the public summary of apostolic teaching held in the apostolic churches; in 2.4's sense, whatever settles, for those who hold it, what must be believed.

**The *regula fidei* (2.1, 2.3).** When heretics argued from the same books, the second-century Church settled readings by the rule of faith of the apostolic churches ([`patristics` 2.1](../patristics/lessons/02-01-irenaeus-against-the-gnostics.md), [2.3](../patristics/lessons/02-03-tertullian.md)). Irenaeus, *Against Heresies* 3.4.1 (ANF): in "a dispute relative to some important question", "have recourse to the most ancient Churches"; had the apostles left no writings, we would follow the tradition they handed on; barbarian believers keep the faith "without paper or ink" (3.4.2); only then (3.5.1) comes "the Scriptural proof". 2.3's verdict: *materially* sufficient (the rule holds the same content scripture holds, not extra content; concede that in full), *formally* not (in a dispute the apostolic churches' teaching rules the reading). On the Reformation side, Mathison reads *sola scriptura* under the ancient rule of faith, and the Second Helvetic (II) admits only readings in accord "with the rule of faith and charity".

**Trap (2.3).** Irenaeus and Tertullian argued against those who rejected the rule; Lutheran and Reformed churches confess the creeds. What transfers is the structure, the rule as a norm of reading, not the verdict; Tertullian's prescription bars outsiders, not creed-confessing Christians.

**The working rule (2.4).** Westminster's supreme judge is the one "in whose sentence we are to rest" (I.10), and Luther's external clarity judges doctrine "for others also". **Crux:** is a rule of faith that needs an authority to apply it still a rule of faith *alone*? Reformed: yes, since ministers and synods apply it ministerially, are judged by it and may err, "alone" means alone infallible and supreme, not alone present. Catholic: the working rule then includes the one who applies it, so "Scripture alone" names the norm, not the rule.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)

### Second Helvetic Confession

Heinrich Bullinger's Swiss Reformed confession (1566), widely adopted or approved among the Reformed churches: a Reformed standard, not a magisterium; **no Catholic level**. Schaff vol. 3 prints it in Latin in the body; the English is the third edition's appendix.

**Ch. II, on interpretation (2.1, 2.3, 2.4).** Since scripture is "not of any private interpretation" (2 Pet 1:20), "we do not allow all kinds of exposition"; it rejects the Roman Church's meaning as the true sense; it admits only interpretation from scripture, by its language, circumstances and comparison of places, that accords "with the rule of faith and charity"; it does not despise the Greek and Latin Fathers where they agree with scripture; it admits no judge in controversies but God speaking by scripture; it rejects traditions which, "being compared with the Scriptures, disagree with them". The clearest confessional clause against [solo scriptura](#solo-scriptura), and the proof that Reformed formal sufficiency is not private reading.

**Ch. XI (1.1; 6.1).** It professes what the creeds and the first four councils "defined out of the Holy Scriptures" (the qualifier matters); XI.4 has Christ born *ex Maria semper virgine*, of Mary ever virgin: a Reformed confession keeping the perpetual virginity.

**Chs. XVII-XIX (4.1; 4.3).** Christ needs no vicar, "for he is said to have a substitute, who is absent; but Christ is present with his Church" (XVII.8); the [key of David](#key-of-david) is Christ's own, and ministers hold a steward's "ministerial power", "the same and alike in all" (XVIII.13-16). The ministry is "a most ancient ordinance of God himself"; ministers are lawfully elected and "ordained by the elders with public prayer, and laying on of hands"; ministry and priesthood are "things far different"; bishops rank over elders by custom; churches without the true marks gain nothing when they "brag of the succession of bishops" (XVII); ordination is "very profitable, but no sacrament" (XIX). This is [succession in doctrine](#succession-in-doctrine), a theory of what the apostles handed on, not an evasion.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Trent Session IV

The session of 8 April 1546: a first decree receiving scripture and the apostolic traditions and defining the canon, and a second on the Vulgate and on the interpretation of scripture.

**First decree (Waterworth, 1848).** The gospel's truth and discipline are "contained in the written books, and the unwritten traditions"; both are received "with an equal affection of piety, and reverence". The Synod "receives and venerates" all the books of both Testaments, inserts the list "lest a doubt may arise in any one's mind", and anathematizes whoever does not receive them "entire with all their parts, as they have been used to be read in the Catholic Church", or "knowingly and deliberately" contemns the traditions. It identifies the books by the Church's reading and does not argue from history (2.2).

**Levels.** The canon and inspiration: **rung 1**. Equal reverence: **rung 1 as worded**, equal in reverence, never in extent; the draft's "partly ... partly" became "and", so the extent question is **rung 5** ([partim-partim](#partim-partim)). The anathema's object is refusing the canon and knowingly contemning the traditions, not the belief that scripture contains all things necessary; whether it reaches Westminster I.6, Article VI or Chemnitz: **rung 5**, since no act applies it. **Second decree:** the [rule of interpretation](#rule-of-interpretation), penalties by law, not anathema: **at least rung 3**; the Vulgate declared authentic: **law, no rung** ([`fundamental-theology` 3.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-03-the-canon-as-defined.md)).

**Traps.** Trent never names *sola scriptura*. Reading "equal" as equal content. Saying the anathema certainly reaches Westminster. Florence's "receives and venerates" is echoed, but Florence attached no anathema to its list ([Council of Florence](#council-of-florence)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)

### Dei Filius

Vatican I's Dogmatic Constitution on the Catholic Faith (24 April 1870); quoted from Schaff vol. 2.

**Ch. 2.** The books are sacred and canonical "not because, having been carefully composed by mere human industry, they were afterwards approved by her authority", but because, written by the Spirit's inspiration, they have God for their author and were delivered as such to the Church: chapter text, **at least rung 3**, its substance following from can. II.4 (canon and inspiration, **rung 1**). It agrees with Westminster I.4 and Calvin I.7.2 that the Church does not confer scripture's authority (2.1, 2.2). Ch. 2 also renews Trent IV's [rule of interpretation](#rule-of-interpretation) ("renewing the said decree"): **at least rung 3**.

**Ch. 3.** Faith believes revealed things because of the authority of God revealing; the Church herself is a great and perpetual [motive of credibility](#motive-of-credibility): chapter text no canon repeats, **at least rung 3**. Canons on faith, 3 (revelation can be made credible by outward signs; condemns saying men are moved to faith solely by inner experience or private inspiration): **rung 1** ([`fundamental-theology` 2.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-02-credibility-and-the-preambles-of-faith.md)).

**Ch. 4 with can. IV.3.** The sense of dogmas once declared is perpetually retained, never departed from under pretext of deeper understanding; growth "in one and the same doctrine, one and the same sense, one and the same judgment": **rung 1** ([development of doctrine](#development-of-doctrine); 6.6). 7.2 sets ch. 4 beside Westminster I.6.

**Traps.** Saying Vatican I *defined* what a chapter only expounds. Reading canons on faith, 3 as condemning the Reformed inward testimony, which affirms outward evidences (2.1).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Deuterocanon

The seven Old Testament books (Tobit, Judith, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch, 1-2 Maccabees) and the Greek parts of Esther and Daniel, received in the Catholic canon and absent from the Hebrew canon.

**Level.** Canonical, **rung 1** (Trent IV, "entire with all their parts"; *Dei Filius* can. II.4): Trent's single list is rung 1 for all seventy-three books. "Deuterocanonical" is a historians' label for slower reception: **no rank**. Florence had listed the books in 1442 without anathema and without the word "canonical" ([Council of Florence](#council-of-florence)).

**Each tradition's handling: name the text, never "Protestants" (2.2).** *Reformed:* Westminster I.3, the Apocrypha, "not being of divine inspiration, are no part of the Canon", and have "no authority in the Church of God" beyond "other human writings"; Belgic VI, the Church may read and take instruction from them "so far as they agree with the canonical books", never to confirm a point of faith. *Anglican:* Article VI (1571), citing Jerome, the Church reads them "for example of lyfe and instruction of manners", but does not apply them "to establishe any doctrene". *Lutheran:* the Book of Concord has no list; Luther's 1534 Bible printed the books apart ([`old-testament` 6.1](../old-testament/lessons/06-01-how-the-old-testament-canon-formed.md)). Calvin's polemical case: [Calvin Antidote](#calvin-antidote).

**The concession (2.2).** The Reformers did not invent their Old Testament: the Reformed and Anglican list is the Hebrew canon Jerome defended, and Article VI cites him. Jerome doubted the seven; Rufinus, as Calvin reports, says the Fathers called several of them ecclesiastical, not canonical; Cardinal Cajetan, Luther's examiner in 1518, still ranked Judith, Tobit and Maccabees with Jerome in his commentary on the historical books. The canon was not defined under anathema before 1546.

**Later (6.3).** 2 Maccabees 12:46 on prayer for the dead is scripture only for a reader who receives the deuterocanon, so that premise runs through 2.2; for anyone else it attests Jewish practice. **Traps.** "Protestants made up their own Old Testament" or "removed seven books" (every book of the Protestant Old Testament is in the Catholic one). "Nobody doubted them before Luther" ([manufactured consensus](#manufactured-consensus)). "Deuterocanonical means second-class" (understates rung 1). Reading Article VI's "commonly received" test for the New Testament as Anglican acceptance of the deuterocanon (2.2 P1).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Belgic Confession

Guido de Bres's Reformed confession (1561; revised 1619) for the churches of the Low Countries: a confessional standard of the continental (Dutch) Reformed churches that their officers subscribe. Not a magisterium, never Lutheran; **no Catholic level**. Quoted from Schaff vol. 3's English.

**On the canon (2.2).** Art. IV lists the books, Hebrews among Paul's fourteen epistles. Art. V: the books are received "not so much because the Church receives and approves them as such", but more especially because the Holy Ghost witnesses in believers' hearts that they are from God, "whereof they carry the evidence in themselves": reception subordinate, not zero ([inward testimony of the Spirit](#inward-testimony-of-the-spirit)). Art. VI: the apocryphal books may be read for instruction "so far as they agree with the canonical books", never to confirm a point of faith. Art. IX receives the three creeds (1.1).

**On intercession (6.1).** Art. XXVI: no creature loves us more than Christ or has more power, so seeking another intercessor is distrust.

**On the Church and ministry (4.3, 4.4, 7.3).** Arts. 28-29 give the visible Church real authority in discipline (4.4 P3). Art. 29 names three [marks of the Church](#marks-of-the-church) where Augsburg names two: pure preaching, pure sacraments, and "if church discipline is exercised in punishing of sin" ([discipline as a mark](#discipline-as-a-mark)); the same article grants that hypocrites are mixed with the good in the Church, so the Reformed argument from scandal built on it is not Donatism (7.3). Art. XXXI agrees with the Second Helvetic on the ministry (4.3): [succession in doctrine](#succession-in-doctrine).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Calvin Antidote

Calvin's *Acts of the Council of Trent with the Antidote* (1547), his point-by-point reply to Trent's Sessions I-VII (Beveridge's translation, *Tracts* vol. 3, 1851): a theologian's polemical tract, not a confession; **no magisterial level**.

**On Session IV (2.2).** Trent marks "all the books with the same chalk", against "the consent of the primitive Church": Jerome reports the earlier common opinion, and Rufinus, with Jerome, says the Fathers called Ecclesiasticus, Wisdom, Tobit, Judith and Maccabees "not canonical but ecclesiastical books", read to the people, not used to establish doctrine. He grants that Carthage and Augustine held Trent's view, but since not all agreed, "let us assume that the point was then undecided". Internal evidence: 2 Maccabees' writer begs pardon if he wrote poorly (15:38; DR 15:39), which is "alien ... from the majesty of the Holy Spirit". He suspects a motive (2 Maccabees will "prove Purgatory") but does not "entirely disapprove the reading of those books".

**On Session VI (3.2).** "Faith alone" never meant a faith devoid of charity: "It is therefore faith alone which justifies, and yet the faith which justifies is not alone"; can. 10 trades on an equivocation, since the adverb "formally" is used in two senses. **Text trap:** the *Antidote* numbers Trent VI's chapters one higher (Trent's ch. 7 is his "VIII").

**Traps.** Calling it "the Reformed position" (the Reformed confessional texts are Westminster I.3 and Belgic VI). "Calvin threw the books out" or "forbade reading them" (2.2 P3). **The Catholic reply's shape (2.2 P3):** the record alone splits Father against council, so a Catholic must add that the Church's later definition settles what earlier reception left open, an authority claim needing its own warrant; and inspired authors write as true authors, so a modest apology is no evidence against inspiration ([`fundamental-theology` 3.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-01-inspiration.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Luthers 1522 prefaces

The prefaces of Luther's September Testament (Wittenberg, 1522), which rank the New Testament books.

**What they do (2.2).** The table of contents numbers twenty-three books and sets Hebrews, James, Jude and Revelation after them, unnumbered. In the lesson's translation of the German, these four "have in times past had a different standing" (Hebrews preface). Hebrews is denied to Paul or any apostle (2:3) and praised for its learning. James is not "in the number of the true chief books" (*hewbtbucher*), yet Luther will not "forbid anyone to place and raise him as he pleases". The "strawy epistle" remark is in the general preface ([`new-testament` 6.3](../new-testament/lessons/06-03-james-faith-and-works.md)); sources date its later omission differently, so name the source if you date it.

**Standing.** A theologian's prefaces, not confessional: Lutherans subscribe the confessions, which never adopted his rankings, and the Book of Concord has no canon list; **no magisterial level**.

**In the argument (2.2).** Luther's ranking against Belgic IV's list is evidence that the Spirit's witness, given believer by believer, did not fix the edge (P3); on Hebrews the Reformed confessions sided with the churches' long reception against Luther (Ex. 1). **Trap:** "Luther cut Hebrews, James, Jude and Revelation from his Bible": he printed all four, last, with prefaces that rank them; a prefatory judgement, not an excision.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Council of Florence

The council of union (Ferrara 1438, Florence 1439, later Rome) whose decrees include *Laetentur caeli* for the Greeks (6 July 1439) and *Cantate Domino* for the Copts (4 February 1442).

**The canon (2.2).** The decree for the Copts professes one God author of both Testaments and "receives and venerates" (*suscipit et veneratur*) their books, listing the deuterocanon among them (DS 1334-1335), without the word "canonical" and with no anathema on the list: **at least rung 3**, not manifestly a definition (can. 749 §3). Trent's wording echoes it; the definition, **rung 1**, is Trent's. *Cantate Domino*'s Latin wording of the monarchy of the Father is **rung 1** (5.1).

**The procession (5.1).** *Laetentur caeli* defines procession from Father and Son as from one principle (**rung 1**); glosses "through the Son" (the Son has from the Father that the Spirit proceeds from him, DS 1301), inside the definition for the doctrine but, as a claim about what the Greek Fathers meant, a reading, not defined; and defines the addition lawful (DS 1302, **rung 2**, floor **at least rung 3**; [lawfulness of the addition](#lawfulness-of-the-addition)).

**Primacy (1.3).** It defined Roman primacy over the whole world (1439): **rung 1**, with *Pastor Aeternus*; Coxe's dating of supremacy as dogma to Pius IX overlooks it.

**The dead (5.5).** Its purgatory clause (DS 1304-1305) defines purgation "by purgatorial pains", helped by suffrages, naming no fire, place or treasury: **rung 1** (with Trent VI can. 30); fire, place, duration and mechanism **rung 5**. At Ferrara (5 June 1438) Cardinal Cesarini gave the Greeks the formula, arguing for a set place and a fire, and [Mark of Ephesus](#mark-of-ephesus) answered; the Latins left the fire out of the decree.

**Reception.** The union was rejected in the East, so citing Florence against the Orthodox begs the question ([reception](#reception)).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Circularity objection

The charge that appealing to the Church to certify scripture, and to scripture to certify the Church, is circular; it applies in mirror form to Reformed self-attestation.

**The strongest form against the Catholic (2.3).** From Calvin, *Institutes* I.7.2: the Church is built on the apostles' doctrine (Eph 2:20), so that doctrine "must have had its certainty" first. Pushed a step: that *this* Church is scripture's interpreter is argued from disputed texts (Matt 16:18; 1 Tim 3:15) and disputed history, and its definitions must themselves be read. Either the Church certifies the texts that certify the Church, a circle, or the Catholic chose Rome by his own fallible judgment, the private judgment he will not trust with scripture.

**The Catholic answer (2.3 Ex. 2, from [`fundamental-theology` 2.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-02-credibility-and-the-preambles-of-faith.md)).** Two steps: the New Testament books, read first as historical sources, with the Church's visible marks as a [motive of credibility](#motive-of-credibility), ground a reasonable judgment that Christ founded a teaching Church; that Church, believed on God's authority, then teaches which books are inspired and what they mean. A spiral, not a circle.

**Cost and limit.** The first step is the inquirer's own fallible judgment, and his certainty of faith outruns it, as the Reformed believer's does when it rests on the Spirit's witness (Westminster I.5; Kruger's self-attestation, 2.2: the mirror circle). So the Catholic is shown no worse off than the Reformed on circularity, and not shown better off; the choice returns to the crux. In 2.2 the canon crux ends here, and the [burden](#burden-of-the-catholic-claim) is the Catholic's: a warrant for the recognizer that does not presuppose it. Philosophical twin: the son-of-Great-Pumpkin objection to Plantinga and its parity reply ([`philosophy-of-religion` 5.2](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/05-02-reformed-epistemology.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)

### Partim-partim

The view that revealed truth is divided, some in scripture and some in unwritten tradition alone ("partly ... partly").

**The drafting.** An earlier draft of Trent IV read "partly ... partly"; the promulgated decree reads "and" ([`fundamental-theology` 3.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-04-tradition.md)). So the extent question, material sufficiency against partim-partim, is **rung 5**, freely disputed. Both are Catholic options, and both deny formal sufficiency ([material and formal sufficiency](#material-and-formal-sufficiency)). **Consequence (2.3):** Trent did not dogmatize [Tradition II](#tradition-i-and-tradition-ii), as Mathison holds.

**Traps.** "Trent defined that some revealed truth is in tradition alone" (overstated). Answering Chemnitz's reading of Basil with partim-partim ("so some doctrines are in tradition alone") concedes his frame and argues the wrong question (2.3 P2).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)

### Unwritten tradition

Apostolic teaching or practice handed on in the Church without written record; Basil, *On the Holy Spirit* 27 (375), is the classic patristic text.

**Basil 27.66 (NPNF, Jackson).** Of the Church's beliefs and practices, some come from written teaching, others are "delivered to us 'in a mystery' by the tradition of the apostles; and both of these in relation to true religion have the same force". The items: the sign of the cross, facing east, the words at the displaying of the eucharistic bread, blessing the font and the oil, triple immersion, the renunciation of Satan. In 27.67 Basil asks his opponents for the written source of the baptismal confession itself. **Weight:** a Father writing as an author, **no magisterial level**.

**Readings.** *Lutheran:* [Chemnitz](#chemnitz-examen) reads the items as rites and "the same force" as a hyperbole. *Catholic (2.3 P2):* the chapter defends a doctrine, the Spirit's rank, by a rite, the doxology "with the Spirit" ([`patristics` 3.2](../patristics/lessons/03-02-basil-the-great.md)); so it is weak evidence for extra doctrines and stronger evidence that received worship worked as a norm of reading scripture: against formal sufficiency, not for [partim-partim](#partim-partim). It does not show that norm infallible. Most items are traditions with a small *t* ([`fundamental-theology` 3.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-04-tradition.md)).

**Text notes.** Erasmus doubted the chapter is Basil's; NPNF's note reports Casaubon's defence and early Syriac evidence, so the doubt is not decisive. Basil's Letter 189.3, "let God-inspired Scripture decide between us", sends conflicting local customs to scripture's judgment, which a Catholic grants; it is also transmitted among Gregory of Nyssa's works. The New Testament's own tradition texts are 2.3's P1: "hold the traditions, which you have learned, whether by word or by our epistle" (2 Thess 2:15; DR 2:14), and 1 Cor 11:2, where the DR, after the Vulgate, has "ordinances".

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)

### Chemnitz Examen

Martin Chemnitz's *Examen Concilii Tridentini* (*Examination of the Council of Trent*, 1565-73), the classic Lutheran reply to Trent: a theologian's book by one of the Formula of Concord's six authors, weighty, not a confession; **no Catholic level**. Paraphrased from the Latin, never quoted.

**On traditions (2.3; Part I).** He sorts the Fathers' uses of "tradition" into eight kinds and finds seven compatible with scripture's sole authority, among them the handing on of the canon, the rule of faith, the Fathers' consensus and ancient rites. He rejects the eighth, Trent's: doctrines of faith and morals that no testimony of scripture proves, received with equal reverence. **On Basil 27:** the Fathers' unwritten traditions are rites; what scripture's sense supports (the baptismal confession, the renunciation of Satan, the doxology) he receives gladly; the rest are customs credited to the apostles without proof, some dropped by Rome itself; "the same force" is a hyperbole, since baptism without triple immersion is still baptism; after Erasmus he doubts the chapter is Basil's. **Turned (2.3 P2):** he admits the doxology because scripture's sense supports it, but that was exactly what Basil's opponents denied; received practice settled it.

**On justification (3.2).** Trent's decrees imply that Lutherans teach forgiveness without renewal; they teach that renewal comes with reconciliation, distinguished so that faith rests on the Mediator. He refuses to call it a war over the word "justify": the real question is what a sinner can set against God's judgment.

**Traps.** "Chemnitz rejects all tradition" (he accepts seven kinds). Treating the *Examen* as the Lutheran position or as part of the Book of Concord: Lutherans confess the Formula's [Rule and Norm](#rule-and-norm).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Motive of credibility

A public sign that makes it reasonable to believe God has spoken, as distinct from the motive of faith, which is the authority of God revealing.

**The text.** *Dei Filius* ch. 3 (Schaff vol. 2): the Church, by her wonderful extension, eminent holiness and inexhaustible fruitfulness, her catholic unity and invincible stability, is "a great and perpetual motive of credibility": chapter text no canon repeats, **at least rung 3**; its canon (revelation can be made credible by outward signs) **rung 1**. The doctrine is [`fundamental-theology` 2.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-02-credibility-and-the-preambles-of-faith.md)'s.

**In 2.3.** The Church's visible marks enter the first, historical step of the Catholic answer to the [circularity objection](#circularity-objection): read as a historical source, the record grounds a reasonable judgment that Christ founded a teaching Church. It is the warrant 2.2 says the Catholic owes for an infallible recognizer.

**In 7.3.** The abuse record counts as real evidence against one motive of credibility the Church herself offered. The crux: is the Church's claim about her holiness of conduct, or about the deposit and sacraments she is given? The critic presses that a Church that offered her holiness as a sign cannot retreat to the deposit when the sign fails; the Catholic, that the sign was always the whole across centuries, which one era can weaken but not falsify.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### External and internal clarity

Luther's distinction (*The Bondage of the Will*, 1525) between scripture's clarity as the word publicly proclaimed (external) and its clarity to a heart given the Spirit (internal).

**The text (Henry Cole's translation, 1823).** Sect. IV: "The one is external, placed in the ministry of the word; the other internal, placed in the understanding of the heart." Internal: "no man sees one iota in the scriptures, but he that hath the Spirit of God." External: "nothing whatever is left obscure or ambiguous". Sect. XXXIV ties external clarity to the external judgment, "peculiar to the public ministry of the word and the external office", which judges doctrine "not for ourselves only, but for others also". End of Sect. III: the *things* of scripture are all in the light, though some places are obscure because their words are not understood, and what is dark in one place is plain in another; scripture "simply confesses the trinity of God, the humanity of Christ, and the unpardonable sin", and how these things are it does not say. Aimed at enthusiasts who subject scripture to their own spirit, and at a saying Luther met among the papal party, that scripture is obscure and its interpreter must be sought from Rome. Sect. XXXV offers Deuteronomy 17:8-12 as his first proof of external clarity (2.4 P3).

**Standing.** A theologian's polemic, not a Lutheran confession; the Formula commends it on free will (SD II.44); **no Catholic level**. Cite Cole's translation, not the separate 1823 version by Vaughan that circulates more widely.

**Traps.** Merging Luther and Westminster: Luther's claim covers all the *things* of scripture (not every word) and places external clarity in the public ministry, while Westminster I.7 promises clarity only for what is necessary. Reading external clarity as grammar any private reader can parse. Answering a public dispute between two preachers with "the text is unclear", the one answer the passage rules out: Luther must lay the fault on one of them (2.4 P1).

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)

### Ordinary means

Westminster's term of art for the regular helps through which Christ works (the word read and especially preached, the sacraments, prayer), through which the unlearned reach a sufficient understanding of what is necessary.

**The text.** Westminster I.7 promises clarity "in a due use of the ordinary means"; chapter I does not define the phrase, and the lesson glosses it from Westminster's own vocabulary: the Shorter Catechism names Christ's "ordinances, especially the word, sacraments, and prayer", with preaching foremost (Q.88-89); also XVIII.3. The Second Helvetic (II) adds reading by language, circumstances and comparison of places, under "the rule of faith and charity", with the Fathers consulted. Standing: Presbyterian subordinate standards; **no Catholic level**.

**The reply at full strength (2.4).** The clarity thesis was never "me and my Bible": preaching, pastors, catechisms, creeds and synods are the means, and the Ethiopian understood through Philip (Acts 8:31). **The Catholic rejoinder:** grant it; the parties at Marburg and after used every ordinary means (the original languages, preaching, the Fathers, a face-to-face colloquy) and still divided over what is necessary, and inside each tradition a subscribed confession settled the dispute ([perspicuity](#perspicuity)).

**Traps.** Reading "ordinary means" as private reading. Using Acts 8:31 as proof of an infallible interpreter: Westminster grants it, and neither it nor 2 Peter 3:16 decides what kind of guide is needed.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)

### Rule of interpretation

The Catholic rule that no one may interpret scripture, in faith and morals, against the sense the Church holds, hers being to judge the true sense, or against the unanimous consent of the Fathers.

**The text.** Trent IV, second decree (8 April 1546; Waterworth): "to restrain petulant spirits", no one "relying on his own skill" may interpret scripture against the sense "which holy mother Church,--whose it is to judge of the true sense and interpretation of the holy Scriptures,--hath held and doth hold", or against "the unanimous consent of the Fathers"; the penalty is "the penalties by law established", no anathema. Renewed in *Dei Filius* ch. 2; restated in DV 10, which entrusts authentic interpretation exclusively to the living teaching office (CCC 85, 100, 119).

**Level.** **At least rung 3** (1.1, 1.3, 2.1, 2.3, 2.4); DV 10's "exclusively", **at least rung 3**. Its "whose it is to judge" clause is the Catholic counterpart to Westminster I.10's supreme judge (2.4 P6): both sides apply the text through a judge, and only one claims its judge cannot fail.

**Distinctions.** A restraint, not a method ([`patristics` 1.1](../patristics/lessons/01-01-how-to-read-a-church-father.md)). Not an obscurity thesis: that scripture is obscure is **not taught**, and DV 25 urges all the faithful to read scripture often. **Traps:** calling it defined dogma (it carries penalties, not an anathema); repeating Luther's charge that Rome teaches scripture is obscure.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Osiander controversy

The 1550s Lutheran dispute over Andreas Osiander's teaching that our justifying righteousness is God's essential righteousness dwelling in us, settled for subscribing Lutherans by the Formula of Concord, art. III.

**In 2.4.** The test case Module 3 inherits: justification, which every party called necessary, divided Lutherans among themselves until a subscribed confession settled it, so it bears on whether fallible authority settles necessary disputes ([perspicuity](#perspicuity)).

**In 3.1.** The Formula names no one: "one side" made our righteousness God's essential righteousness dwelling in us, "others" Christ's human nature alone; F. Bente's introduction to the Triglot names Osiander and Stancaro (Stancarus). What the Formula affirms against both is the core of the Lutheran statement Boss 3(a) asks for, and whether Newman's *Lectures on Justification* restate the Osiandrian position is Boss 3(c)'s question: outline only here (read 3.1). **Trap:** writing that the Formula names Osiander or Stancaro.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)



**Against sola fide (Module 3)**

### Sola fide

Justification by faith alone: the sinner is accepted by God for Christ's sake, through faith, without works or merit.

**Standing, by confession.** Lutheran: Augsburg IV (1530, Triglot), men "cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake", through faith. Anglican: Article XI (1571), justification "by Faith only, is a most wholesome Doctrine, and very full of comfort". Reformed: faith "the alone instrument of justification" (Westminster XI.2). None means faith without works: Augsburg VI (faith "is bound to bring forth good fruits"), Westminster XI.2 ("not alone in the person justified"), Formula of Concord Epitome III aff. 8 ("it never is alone"). Confessional standards in their traditions; no Catholic level.

**Catholic side.** Trent VI can. 9 condemns "faith alone" only "in such wise as to mean" that nothing else is required to co-operate: **rung 1** as worded. Ch. 8 says we are "justified by faith", faith being "the beginning ... the foundation, and the root of all Justification" (wording **at least rung 3**). In 1999 the Annex the Catholic Church signed says justification is by faith alone, apart from works, with co-operation in the Spirit's power (2C; **no rung**; see [Official Common Statement and Annex](#official-common-statement-and-annex)).

**Distinctions.** As a noun phrase, "faith alone" occurs in the New Testament only at James 2:24, negated (*ouk ek pisteōs monon*); "only believe" (Mark 5:36; Luke 8:50) is a verb in healing stories. The word count refutes nothing: James denies that bare assent justifies (2:19), and so does the Apology. **Traps:** "Trent condemned justification by faith" (3.1 P3); "faith alone is bare assent" (Augsburg XX says otherwise); "faith alone justifies, but the faith that justifies is never alone" quoted as Luther's words: the tag's source is Calvin's *Antidote* on can. 11 ("It is therefore faith alone which justifies, and yet the faith which justifies is not alone"), and the Formula has its own "it never is alone" ([3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)). The idea is older than the tag: the Formula ascribes to Luther that faith alone lays hold of the blessing "and yet it is never and at no time alone" (SD III.41, Triglot).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Imputation

Crediting to someone what is not his own doing: in the confessions, God counts Christ's obedience to the believer as the righteousness for which he is accepted.

**Standing, by text.** Lutheran: Formula of Concord Epitome III aff. 2, God "imputes to us the righteousness of Christ's obedience". Reformed: Westminster XI.1, "by imputing the obedience and satisfaction of Christ unto them"; Heidelberg Q.60, Christ's satisfaction, righteousness and holiness imputed "as if I had never committed nor had any sin". Calvin, *Institutes* III.11.2 (a theologian's book): justification consists "in the forgiveness of sins and the imputation of the righteousness of Christ".

**Lutheran and Reformed wording differ.** Augsburg IV: "This faith God imputes for righteousness in His sight"; the Apology calls faith righteousness "imputatively" (Art. III §186, Triglot numbering). Westminster XI.1 denies "imputing faith itself, the act of believing"; Heidelberg Q.61, not "the worthiness of my faith". Mostly a difference of wording: both count faith only as it receives Christ (Apology III §187; Q.61). How Augsburg IV's sentence fits the Formula's is taught in 3.1 (the Formula paragraph, aff. 3's clause); Boss 3(a) asks for it in full.

**Catholic side.** Trent VI can. 11 condemns justification "by the sole imputation of the justice of Christ", excluding grace and charity inhering in the soul: **rung 1** as worded. It condemns *sole* imputation, not the word. The imputation that belongs to the atonement is [`christology` 5.4](../christology/lessons/05-04-penal-substitution-and-the-catholic-assessment.md)'s, not reopened.

**After the New Perspective (3.3).** The dispute survived it: John Piper's *The Future of Justification: A Response to N. T. Wright* (2007) defended the imputation of Christ's righteousness; Wright replied in *Justification: God's Plan and Paul's Vision* (2009). The Presbyterian Church in America's General Assembly (2007) recommended declarations "as a faithful exposition of the Westminster Standards", one rejecting any denial that Christ's "perfect obedience and satisfaction" is imputed to believers: a General Assembly's judgment, not an amendment of the Standards.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Alien righteousness

The righteousness that counts before God is another's, Christ's, and stays his while it is credited to the believer (Latin *aliena iustitia*).

**Where the term is.** Not in Augsburg IV, nor in the Formula's Epitome III. It is the Apology's: in forensic usage, to justify is to acquit and declare righteous "on account of the righteousness of another, namely, of Christ" (Triglot Art. III §§184–185; Latin *propter alienam iustitiam*). Bente notes it is also Luther's term. The Formula carries the idea in Solid Declaration III.55, all our righteousness sought outside ourselves (Latin *extra nos*); the Triglot's English reads "outside the merits, works, virtues, and worthiness of ourselves and of all men", so do not put "outside ourselves" in quotation marks as its words. **Standing:** Lutheran confessional texts (Book of Concord); no Catholic level.

**Picture (3.1).** Luther's marriage in *On Christian Liberty* (1520): faith unites the soul to Christ "as the wife to the husband", and the bankrupt bride's estate is the groom's, hers by the vow, not by her housekeeping.

**Trap:** putting the term in Augsburg IV or the Epitome. What "alien" means, in words a Lutheran theologian would sign, is Boss 3(a)'s; 3.1 supplies the texts.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)

### Forensic justification

"Justify" in its courtroom sense: to declare righteous, acquit, absolve, rather than to make inwardly righteous.

**Standing.** Apology Art. III §184 (Triglot): "according to forensic usage, to acquit a guilty one and declare him righteous". Formula Epitome III aff. 5: "to absolve, that is, to declare free from sins"; neg. 3 rejects justification as being made righteous "because of love infused". Calvin, *Institutes* III.11.2–3: a relation, not a quality. Lutheran confessional texts and a theologian's book; no Catholic level.

**The Apology's two senses.** It also says Scripture speaks of being both "made" and "pronounced" just (IV.72), and that by faith alone we are "of unrighteous men made righteous, or regenerated" (IV.117). The Formula rules that where the Apology uses *regeneratio* and *vivificatio* for justification, it is "in the same sense" as absolution (aff. 5). Its forensic reading of James 2:24, "pronounced righteous in a forensic sense" (Art. III §131), is [readings of James 2](#readings-of-james-2).

**Catholic side.** Trent makes justification a translation into the state of grace (VI ch. 4), not a bare reputing-just; so "justify" is one of the words on which canon and confession pass each other ([contradiction and passing-by](#contradiction-and-passing-by)). **Trap:** reading Apology IV.117 as Lutheran infusion; the same passage puts acceptance before love (3.1 P1).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)

### Fiducial faith

Faith as trust (*fiducia*) in God's promise of forgiveness for Christ's sake, not mere knowledge of, or assent to, the history.

**Standing.** Augsburg XX: faith is not "merely the knowledge of the history". Apology IV ("What Is Justifying Faith?"): "to wish and to receive the offered promise"; IV.48: "to assent to the promise of God", in which remission is freely offered for Christ's sake. Formula Epitome III aff. 4: not "a bare knowledge of the history of Christ". Calvin, *Institutes* III.2.7 (a theologian's): "a firm and sure knowledge of the divine favor toward us". No Catholic level.

**Catholic side.** Can. 12 condemns saying justifying faith "is nothing else but confidence in the divine mercy": **rung 1** as worded. Trent's faith is assent to what God reveals, including "this especially, that God justifies the impious by His grace" (ch. 6), living only with hope and charity (ch. 7), and "the beginning ... the foundation, and the root" of justification (ch. 8). Can. 28 (**rung 1** as worded): the faith that remains when grace is lost is "a true faith", though not lively.

**The passing (3.2).** "Faith" names trust that cannot coexist with mortal sin in the Apology (IV.48; IV.115), assent that can in Trent (ch. 15; can. 28): the texts use one word in two senses. What remains once the word is split is narrower: whether the assent that survives mortal sin is still true, God-given faith. **Trap:** "faith alone is bare assent" (3.1, ruled out).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Faith as instrument

Faith as the means by which the believer receives Christ's righteousness, not a work or a merit that earns acceptance.

**Standing.** Formula Epitome III aff. 3: "faith alone is the means and instrument whereby we lay hold of Christ". Westminster XI.2: "the alone instrument of justification" (Latin *unicum instrumentum*), "yet is it not alone in the person justified ... but worketh by love". Heidelberg Q.61. No Catholic level.

**Catholic side, and why they pass.** Trent VI ch. 7 names baptism, "the sacrament of faith", the instrumental cause. Two causal slots, God's conferring means and the receiving hand, so ch. 7 and Westminster XI.2 pass each other (3.2 P6); the real difference over baptism belongs to sacramental doctrine ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)). Each side's "alone" sits in a different slot: Trent's single formal cause (*unica*), Westminster's single instrument (*unicum*).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Simul iustus et peccator

Luther's formula: the justified Christian is at once righteous (by imputation) and a sinner (in himself).

**Standing.** Luther's, not confessional: the 1575 English of his 1535 *Galatians*, on 3:6, has "a Christian man is both righteous and a sinner". It appears in no text of the Book of Concord. A theologian's commentary; no level.

**1999.** The Annex the Catholic Church signed (2A) says, in substance, that Lutherans and Catholics can together call the Christian *simul iustus et peccator* in a stated sense: the justified are truly renewed, yet none is without sin (1 John 1:8). It is part of 3.4's Catholic concession. The [1998 Catholic Response](#catholic-response-1998) had found the formula, as JDDJ §29 explains it, not acceptable to Catholics, and named it first among the points blocking §41's generic verdict. The Annex has **no rung of its own**.

**Trap:** "the Augsburg Confession teaches the *simul*" (3.1 Watch out, P3).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Formal cause
