That by which a person is formally just: for Trent VI ch. 7, the single (*unica*) formal cause is "the justice of God, not that whereby He Himself is just, but that whereby He maketh us just", received within us.

**Level.** Its substance **rung 1** through cans. 10–11; the word "alone" is chapter text, **at least rung 3**. What the formal-cause phrases of ch. 7 exclude is read in [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.1](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) and its Boss 5(a); Module 3 takes that reading, it does not redo it.

**Where it collides.** It is the first point the Catholic reply must meet: the confessions deny that the righteousness that counts is infused (3.1, "What the reply must answer", 1), and 3.2 P2 counts inherent justice among the four collisions. Both sides closed the middle road ([double justice](#double-justice)). Ch. 16 adds that the justice "called ours" is God's too, "infused into us of God".

**The deeper premise (3.2, The crux).** Can a righteousness real but imperfect in degree stand at God's judgment? Chemnitz's question ([*Examen*](#chemnitz-examen)); Westminster XVI.5, our best works "can not endure the severity of God's judgment"; Trent, venial sins do not make the just cease to be just (ch. 11). **Later uses.** 3.3's Flashback restates 3.1's crux in these terms: can the righteousness that counts remain Christ's, counted, or must it be God's gift made ours? 3.4: Malloy's charge that the Joint Declaration's Catholic paragraphs never name it ([critics](#critics-of-the-joint-declaration)); Newman's 1874 withdrawal ([*Lectures on Justification*](#lectures-on-justification)).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Double justice

The view that the justified are accepted by two justices together: an inherent but imperfect one of their own and Christ's imputed one.

**Rejected from both sides.** Lutheran: Formula Epitome III neg. 9 rejects justification "jointly by the imputed righteousness of Christ and by the new obedience begun in them", or partly by each. Catholic: Trent VI ch. 7's single formal cause, its "alone" **at least rung 3**, with can. 10. So Trent and the Formula each shut the middle road, from opposite sides (3.2 P2). Its history (Regensburg, 1541; Seripando at Trent, 1546) is [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.1](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)'s, not retold.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Perseverance

The Reformed doctrine (perseverance of the saints) that the truly justified can neither totally nor finally fall from grace.

**Reformed standing.** Westminster XVII.1: they "can neither totally nor finally fall away from the state of grace"; XI.5: they "can never fall from the state of justification", though sin brings "God's fatherly displeasure". It depends not on their free will but on election, Christ's merit and intercession, the abiding Spirit and the covenant of grace (XVII.2). They may still "fall into grievous sins; and for a time continue therein" (XVII.3). Presbyterian subordinate standard.

**The Lutherans deny it.** Augsburg XII condemns those "who deny that those once justified can lose the Holy Ghost"; the Apology says justifying faith cannot "coexist with mortal sin" (IV.115); the Smalcald Articles (III.3) say that when holy men fall into manifest sins, "as David into adultery, murder, and blasphemy", faith and the Holy Ghost have departed; Formula Epitome IV neg. 3. On this the Lutherans side with Trent.

**Catholic side.** Can. 23 condemns saying the justified cannot "lose grace", or that one who falls "was never truly justified"; can. 27, that grace is lost only by unbelief: **rung 1** as worded; ch. 15 restates them. Can. 22 shares the Reformed premise that perseverance is God's gift: no one perseveres without his special help. The falling-away collision is Catholic against Reformed only (3.2 P3). Where it turns: whether everyone truly justified is also given final perseverance, so that "justified" and "elect to persevere" name the same people (3.2 P2).

**Traps.** "Protestants hold once saved, always saved": true in substance of Westminster, false of the Book of Concord; and "live as you like" caricatures Westminster too (XVII.3; XIII.1). Smalcald III.3 was aimed at 1520s "sectarists", not at Westminster, written more than a century later. The Saxon Visitation Articles (1592), printed in the Triglot's appendix and listing Reformed perseverance among errors, are "not an integral part of the Book of Concord" (Bente).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Assurance

A believer's certainty that he is now in God's favour (for Westminster, also that he will persevere).

**Lutheran.** Doubt forbidden: despite "many weaknesses", believers must regard it as certain that for Christ's sake they have a gracious God (Formula Epitome III aff. 6); the Apology charges Rome with bidding consciences "doubt whether they obtain remission of sins" (IV.119). Assurance of present grace, since faith can be lost.

**Reformed.** Westminster XVIII: true believers "may in this life be certainly assured that they are in a state of grace" (1); "an infallible assurance of faith" (2; Latin *infallibilis quaedam fidei certitudo*), resting partly on "the inward evidence of those graces"; not of "the essence of faith", so a believer may "wait long" (3); it may be "shaken, diminished, and intermitted" (4). Calvin: faith is "a firm and sure knowledge" (III.2.7) held amid "a perpetual struggle" with distrust (III.2.17).

**Catholic levels.** Ch. 9: no one can know "with a certainty of faith, which cannot be subject to error" that he has grace (*certitudine fidei, cui non potest subesse falsum*): **at least rung 3**; ch. 9 also forbids doubting God's mercy, Christ's merit and the sacraments. Cans. 13–14, making certainty necessary to forgiveness: **rung 1** as worded. Can. 16, claiming "an absolute and infallible certainty" of final perseverance without special revelation: **rung 1** as worded.

**Where they meet (3.2 P4, Example 2).** Cans. 13–14 miss Westminster, which also denies that assurance belongs to faith's essence (XVIII.3); ch. 9 meets XVIII.2 head-on, at chapter level. Whether can. 16 reaches Westminster turns on "absolute" (unconditioned, or never shaken?): **rung 5**, no act has applied it. The Lutheran texts are met separately (3.1, "What the reply must answer", 3). **Trap:** "Trent defined that no one can be sure he is in grace".

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Merit

The worth before God of the good works the justified do in grace, to which God has promised a reward: for Catholics, his gifts crowned as truly their own.

**Catholic levels.** Can. 32: the works of the justified are "the gifts of God" and "also the good merits of him that is justified", who does "truly merit" increase of grace, eternal life and an increase of glory: **rung 1** as worded; so are cans. 26 and 31 (the hope of eternal recompense "through His mercy and the merit of Jesus Christ") and can. 33 (nothing taken from Christ's merits). No merit before the first grace: **rung 1** (cans. 1–3, with Orange II can. 18). Ch. 16's grounds (Christ's virtue flowing "as the head into the members", which "always precedes and accompanies and follows" every meritorious work; the reward "according to the promise of God Himself"): **at least rung 3**, except the gift–merit pairing can. 32 carries. Aquinas (ST I-II q.114: God debtor only to his own ordination, a.1 ad 3; mercy the "first cause", merit a "subsequent cause", a.3 ad 2): the schools, no magisterial level. Doctrine taught in [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-03-merit.md).

**Lutheran.** The Apology grants merit of reward: good works "are meritorious, not for the remission of sins, for grace or justification ... but for other rewards, bodily and spiritual", in this life and after it (Art. III §73, Triglot numbering); "different rewards according to different labors". The Formula: God promises good works "a glorious reward in this life and the life to come" (SD IV.38). Against *meritum condigni*: [condign and congruous merit](#condign-and-congruous-merit).

**Reformed.** Westminster XVI.5 denies that our best works "merit pardon of sin, or eternal life", for four reasons: the "great disproportion" to the glory to come, the "infinite distance" between us and God, our having done only our duty (Luke 17:10), and works that are good as the Spirit's are, as ours, "defiled" and cannot endure God's judgment. Yet God, looking on them in his Son, accepts and rewards "that which is sincere" (XVI.6). Calvin: God recompenses works "not because they so deserve" (III.15.3). The Apology argues from Christ's office as Mediator, Westminster from disproportion and stain.

**Where they part.** The collision is narrow: over merit of grace and eternal life, not over reward (3.2 P5). In 1999 the parties confessed together that we are accepted "not because of any merit on our part" (JDDJ §15), and the Catholic explication tied "meritorious" to a promised reward for works that remain gifts (§38). What survives (3.4): whether a gift of God can become the recipient's own without ceasing to be God's.

**Traps.** "Lutherans (or the Reformed) deny that good works are rewarded"; crediting Westminster's reasons to the Apology, or the Apology's Mediator argument to Westminster ([4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), Flashback); "Catholics teach that the saved earn heaven" (3.4).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Trent Session VI

Trent's decree on justification, 13 January 1547: a proem, sixteen chapters and thirty-three canons (Waterworth, 1848). The decree is [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.1](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)'s; this course sets it against the confessions.

**How to weigh it.** Each canon, **rung 1** as worded; a chapter clause no canon repeats, **at least rung 3** ([chapters and canons](#chapters-and-canons)). The fence, the proem's "otherwise than as by this present decree is defined and declared" and ch. 16's "which whoso receiveth not faithfully and firmly cannot be justified", binds Catholics to the chapters' doctrine without making each sentence a definition. Whether a named confessional clause falls under a canon: **rung 5** ([application of a condemnation](#application-of-a-condemnation)); no act applies the canons to Westminster or the Formula.

| Text | What it says | Level as the lessons mark it | Lessons |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cans. 1–3 (with Orange II can. 18) | grace first; nothing before it merits it | **rung 1** | 3.3, 3.4 |
| Can. 5 | free will after sin not "a thing with only a name" (repeats [*Exsurge Domine*](#exsurge-domine) prop. 36) | **rung 1** as worded | 3.2 |
| Can. 9 | "faith alone" condemned only "in such wise as to mean" nothing else co-operates | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1, 3.3 |
| Can. 11 | not by "the sole imputation" of Christ's justice; grace inheres | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1, 3.2 |
| Can. 12 | justifying faith not "nothing else but confidence" | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1 |
| Cans. 13–14 | certainty not required for forgiveness | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1, 3.2 |
| Can. 16 | no "absolute and infallible certainty" of perseverance without special revelation | **rung 1** as worded | 3.2 |
| Cans. 23, 27 | grace can be lost, and not only by unbelief | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1, 3.2 |
| Can. 24 | works preserve and increase justice, not "merely the fruits and signs" | **rung 1** as worded | 3.3 |
| Can. 28 | the faith that remains without grace is "a true faith" | **rung 1** as worded | 3.2 |
| Cans. 26, 31, 32, 33 | eternal recompense; true merit; nothing taken from Christ's merits | **rung 1** as worded | 3.1, 3.2, 3.4 |
| Ch. 7 | the causes; the single formal cause; its "alone" | "alone" **at least rung 3** | 3.1–3.4 |
| Ch. 8 | faith "the beginning ... the foundation, and the root"; nothing preceding justification, "whether faith or works", merits it | wording **at least rung 3** (substance **rung 1** via cans. 1–3) | 3.1–3.3 |
| Ch. 9 | no "certainty of faith, which cannot be subject to error" | **at least rung 3** | 3.2 |
| Ch. 10 | increase of justice; cites James 2:24 | the citation is the council's reasoning; the sense of James **rung 5** | 3.3 |
| Ch. 12 | against rash presumption of predestination | **at least rung 3** | 3.2 |
| Ch. 16 | merit: grace promised, reward by promise; God's gifts as their merits | grounds **at least rung 3** | 3.4 |

**Traps.** Saying Trent *defined* what a chapter only expounds; "every canon was aimed at Luther" (cans. 19 and 21 miss the Lutherans, can. 23 strikes Westminster, not Wittenberg; 3.2 Example 1); citing Calvin's *Antidote* chapter numbers as Trent's (it numbers the chapters one higher: Trent ch. 7 is his "VIII").

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Contradiction and passing-by

3.2's method for setting a canon against a confessional clause: they contradict only if the clause asserts what the canon condemns, in the canon's own sense.

**The three outcomes.** A canon condemns a proposition in its stated sense and names no one (Trent names no Reformer). A confessional clause that asserts that proposition **contradicts** it. One that uses the key word in another sense **passes by**. If no confession asserts the proposition, the canon **misses** the confessions, whoever else it struck.

**The results 3.2 reaches, in outline.** *Collide* (P2–P5): inherent justice; falling away, with the Reformed only; assurance, in ch. 9 rather than in cans. 13–14; merit of grace and eternal life. *Pass by* (P6): "justify", "faith", "instrument". *Miss*: cans. 13–14 against Westminster XVIII.3; cans. 19 and 21 against the Lutherans; can. 23 against the Lutherans, who agree with it. Each is under its own entry ([formal cause](#formal-cause), [perseverance](#perseverance), [assurance](#assurance), [merit](#merit), [forensic justification](#forensic-justification), [fiducial faith](#fiducial-faith), [faith as instrument](#faith-as-instrument)). 3.4's Flashback runs the test on Calvin's [*Antidote*](#calvin-antidote) against can. 11: a contradiction on what justifies, since the canon excludes charity from the ground, not from the person; applying the canon to Calvin is **rung 5**. Boss 3(b) builds both lists from its own excerpts, every entry citing a clause; the pairing is the student's.

**Traps.** Counting passings as Catholic wins (3.2's concession: a Catholic who reads every canon as a hit on Luther reads carelessly). The opposite: a first divergence in sense does not make the whole dispute verbal; Chemnitz refused that exit, and Lehmann warned against reducing the dispute to language ([the 1986 study](#condemnations-of-the-reformation-era)).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Condemnations of the Reformation Era

The German ecumenical study that asked whether the sixteenth-century mutual condemnations still divide: *Lehrverurteilungen – kirchentrennend?* (vol. 1, 1986; English *The Condemnations of the Reformation Era: Do They Still Divide?*, 1990), edited by Karl Lehmann and Wolfhart Pannenberg.

**What it asked and found.** Produced by the Ecumenical Study Group of Protestant and Catholic Theologians for the Joint Ecumenical Commission set up after John Paul II's 1980 visit to Germany. Its question was whether the condemnations still strike "today's partner"; broadly, it concluded that on justification they no longer divide, and its results remain disputed. Lehmann warned in 1998 that this does not make them simply "without object" (both phrases: the lesson's translation).

**Weight:** an ecumenical study, **no magisterial level**. **Trap:** "the 1986 study showed the canons missed Luther". 3.2 presents it as a narrower form of the scholars' question whether the drafters had Luther's own position in view, not as a concession that the canons miss.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Exsurge Domine

Leo X's bull of 15 June 1520 condemning forty-one propositions drawn from Luther.

**Level.** The condemnation is [*in globo*](#in-globo-censure): the propositions are "respectively" heretical, scandalous, false, offensive or misleading, with no note fixed to any one. So the contradictory of any single proposition is **at least rung 3, no precise note**; calling its denial heresy overstates.

**Prop. 36 (3.2).** Free will after sin is *res de solo titulo*, "a thing with only a name": Trent VI can. 5 repeats it nearly word for word, a canon that does have Luther's own words in view. Augsburg XVIII grants the will "some liberty to choose civil righteousness", so what remains is narrower: whether the will, moved by prevenient grace, co-operates in preparing for justification (ch. 5; can. 4), which the Formula denies ("only two efficient causes", the Holy Ghost and the Word, Epitome II).

**Prop. 33 (7.1).** "That heretics be burned is against the will of the Spirit" (lesson's translation). Its condemnation means the contrary was taught with authority in 1520, **at least rung 3, no precise note**, never defined; *Dignitatis Humanae* (1965, **rung 3**) teaches the contrary principle; development or reversal is **rung 5** (7.1 Example 2, where the distinction stops). **Traps:** "*Exsurge* defined that heretics may be burned"; "it never taught it at all".

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Readings of James 2

The confessional readings of James 2:21–24, where Abraham is "justified by works" and a man is justified "not by faith only", set against Romans 4.

**What every party agrees.** James describes people already justified, not Romans 4:5's first justification (3.3 P1): the Apology, the works "of those who have been justified" (Art. III §125); Calvin, "something posterior is pointed out here" (commentary on 2:23); Trent files 2:24 under ch. 10, "the increase of Justification received". So "James refutes Paul" is nobody's confessional position, and on first justification the Catholic signs Paul's exclusion (Trent VI ch. 8).

| Reading | Text | "Justified" in James means | Standing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Luther, 1522 | preface to James and Jude | James is "flatly against Saint Paul" ([Luther on James](#luther-on-james)) | a translator's preface, in no confession |
| Lutheran confessional | Apology Art. III §§123–132 (Triglot); Formula SD III.42–43 adopts it | "pronounced righteous in a forensic sense" (§131), of those with faith and works; Romans 2:13 read of believers | Book of Concord |
| Reformed | Calvin, *Institutes* III.17.11–12, *Commentary* (1551), *Antidote*; Westminster XI.2 and XVI.2, proved from James 2 | righteousness shown "before men", "the proof of it", not the cause; Romans 2:13 the law's terms, which no one meets (III.17.13) | Calvin a theologian; Westminster the Presbyterian subordinate standard |
| Catholic | Trent VI ch. 10; can. 24 | 3.3: God's approving verdict on a faith brought to completion (*eteleiōthē*, 2:22) in works | can. 24 **rung 1** as worded; the sense of James **rung 5** |

**The crux (3.3).** Is James's "justified" a verdict *before God* that takes works done in faith into account, or a showing *before men* of a verdict already given? James 2:12–14 and 2:23 pull one way, 2:18 and the Genesis chronology the other. Against the Lutherans the live question is not James but can. 24's "cause of the increase" and merit (3.4). **Traps:** blurring the Apology's reading with Calvin's; "the Lutheran confessions reject James".

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Luther on James

Luther's 1522 preface to James and Jude (September Testament), which praised the letter and denied that an apostle wrote it.

**What it says (lesson's translations).** James sets up "no human teaching" and drives God's law hard. But, "flatly against Saint Paul", it gives justification to works and draws Genesis 15:6, a verse about Abraham's faith, onto his offering of Isaac. Luther foresaw the harmonizing gloss and refused it on that point. He would not have it among the chief books, yet forbade no one to rank it higher. "A right strawy epistle" beside John, Paul and 1 Peter is in his general preface (*eyn rechte stroern Epistel gegen sie*); its later history and the canon question are [`new-testament` 6.3](../new-testament/lessons/06-03-james-faith-and-works.md) and [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md) (see [Luther's 1522 prefaces](#luthers-1522-prefaces)).

**Standing:** a translator's preface, in no confession. The Lutheran confessions harmonize James instead (Apology Art. III §§123–132; Formula SD III.42–43). **Trap:** giving the preface as "the Lutheran position".

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Vindicative sense of dikaioo

"Justify" (*dikaioō*) meaning to show or declare someone in the right, not to make him righteous.

**Attested.** The people "justified God" (*edikaiōsan ton theon*, Luke 7:29); "That thou mayest be justified in thy words" (Romans 3:4, quoting Psalm 51:4, DR 50:6); wisdom justified (Luke 7:35; Matthew 11:19, where the Douay follows the Vulgate's "children"). The contrasting *factitive* sense, to make righteous, is the one the Apology refuses for James (Art. III §131).

**Use.** It is the Reformed reading's lexical support and part of 3.3's concession: Calvin's reading has real textual support ("Shew me thy faith", 2:18, and the order of Genesis). The Catholic disputes the audience of James's verdict, before God or before men, not that the sense exists. **Trap:** "'justified before men' has no basis in the Greek".

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Increase of justification

Trent's teaching that the justified grow in the justice they received, through good works done in grace.

**Texts and levels.** Trent VI ch. 10: the justified, "faith co-operating with good works, increase in that justice which they have received ... and are still further justified", citing Revelation 22:11, Sirach 18:22 and James 2:24 (Waterworth). Can. 24: justice received is preserved and increased by good works, which are not "merely the fruits and signs" of justification: **rung 1** as worded. Ch. 10's citation of James is the council's reasoning, not its definition: the sense of James 2:21–24 is **rung 5** inside can. 24's fence.

**The strain (3.3 Example 2).** As a reading of James the "increase" is the weakest Catholic option: James never says "more" or "further"; "let him be justified still" follows the Vulgate, the critical Greek (Tischendorf's, for one) reading "let the righteous still do righteousness"; Calvin's *Antidote* disputed the Sirach rendering and answered that James means "not the cause of righteousness, but the proof of it". Nothing defined is lost: can. 24 stands on its own authority. **Against the Lutherans (4.1's Flashback):** the Apology's own "justified" for James is a verdict before God, nearer Trent than Calvin's "before men", so the live question is can. 24: are works done in grace "merely the fruits and signs" or a cause of the increase, and so merit (3.4)? **Traps:** promoting ch. 10's citation to dogma; demoting can. 24 to opinion.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### New Perspective on Paul

The reading (Sanders, Dunn, Wright) that Paul's "works of the law" are chiefly Israel's covenant boundary markers rather than human achievement; taught in [`new-testament` 5.4](../new-testament/lessons/05-04-the-new-perspective-on-paul.md).

**Level:** a hypothesis, **rung 5**. The 1998 Catholic Response (§7) asked that further study take in the whole New Testament, not Paul alone, which puts James back on the table: a request, not a teaching.

**What it did to the dispute (3.3 Example 1).** It moved the referent of Paul's "works"; it left the sixteenth-century crux, imputed or inherent righteousness, where it was. A Catholic does not need it, since Trent VI ch. 8 already excludes every work before justification. Wright keeps justification a verdict, God's declaration of who belongs to his people, and rejects the picture of the judge's own righteousness passing to the defendant: no step toward Trent's inherent justice. The resistance shows the nerve: [imputation](#imputation) (Piper 2007; Wright 2009; the PCA, 2007). **Trap:** "the New Perspective proves Paul agrees with Trent".

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Works of the law

Paul's *erga nomou* (Romans 3:20, 28; Galatians 2:16), read either as human achievement or as Israel's boundary markers.

On either reading the Catholic case holds, since Trent VI ch. 8 excludes every work, "whether faith or works", before justification: Romans 4:4–5 is not the Catholic's opponent (3.3 P2). The exegesis is [`new-testament` 5.1](../new-testament/lessons/05-01-galatians-faith-law-and-the-gentiles.md)–[5.2](../new-testament/lessons/05-02-romans-1-4-the-righteousness-of-god.md) and [5.4](../new-testament/lessons/05-04-the-new-perspective-on-paul.md); the referent's relocation is the [New Perspective](#new-perspective-on-paul)'s and does not reach the confessional crux.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Official Common Statement and Annex

The 1999 texts by which the Catholic Church and the Lutheran World Federation confirmed the Joint Declaration (text finished 1997) in its entirety, the Annex elucidating the consensus in answer to the 1998 reservations.

**The act.** Signed at Augsburg on 31 October 1999 by Cardinal Edward Idris Cassidy and Bishop Walter Kasper for the Pontifical Council for Promoting Christian Unity, and Bishop Christian Krause and the Rev. Dr Ishmael Noko for the LWF. *Lutheran World Information* (23 October 1999) reported that Bishop Johannes Hanselmann together with Cardinal Joseph Ratzinger, whose congregation had prepared the [1998 reservations](#catholic-response-1998), initiated part of the process behind it: not a liberal slip.

**What the Annex concedes (3.4's Concession, paraphrased; copyrighted).** Justification is by faith alone, apart from works, with co-operation in the Spirit's power (2C, quoting the Formula, SD II); Lutherans and Catholics can together call the Christian [*simul iustus et peccator*](#simul-iustus-et-peccator) in a stated sense (2A); the doctrine of justification is the "measure or touchstone" no teaching may contradict (Annex 3); any reward is a reward of grace, to which we have no claim (2E, after quoting Formula SD IV.38).

**Level:** **no rung of their own**, yet official acts of the Church as a party; §41's correctness, **rung 5**. **The strain (3.4 Example 2):** 2E's "no claim" against can. 32's "truly merit" and Aquinas's inheritance "due by right of adoption" (q.114 a.3): a Thomist must gloss 2E, a Scotist hardly needs to; whether 2E fits can. 32 is **rung 5**, and a Catholic may read it as orthodox, not as the Church choosing Scotus over Thomas. **Trap:** "the Annex repealed can. 32". The Declaration's claims and limits as a text are [`creation-grace-last-things` 5.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)'s ([Joint Declaration](#joint-declaration)).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Differentiated consensus

The ecumenists' later name for the Joint Declaration's method: a confession held in common, with differing explications declared not to overturn it.

**Not in the 1999 texts.** The Declaration says "consensus in basic truths" (§§40, 43; §14 "a consensus in the basic truths"; §§5 and 13 have "on", not "in"), the remaining differences being of "language, theological elaboration, and emphasis" (§40). "Differentiating consensus" appears in the World Communion of Reformed Churches' association (2017) and the Notre Dame consultation statement (2019), both printed in the official twentieth-anniversary edition. **Level:** a method, **no magisterial level**.

**The open question 3.4 leaves open.** Real agreement or postponement? The lesson's picture: two maps of one coast in different projections, or one map with an island the other lacks. Its test case is merit, where gratuity is shared and inherence may be the island. Any verdict passes. **Trap:** quoting "differentiated consensus" as the Declaration's words.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Critics of the Joint Declaration

Theologians on both sides who judged the 1999 consensus verbal rather than real.

| Critic | Side | Charge, as 3.4 states it |
|---|---|---|
| Over 140 German Protestant theology professors, Tübingen statement, early 1998 | Protestant | the Declaration had not shown the consensus it claimed; it covered the differences with wording |
| Eberhard Jüngel, *Um Gottes willen — Klarheit!* (1997) | Lutheran | calling justification "an indispensable criterion" (§18) demotes the article by which all doctrine is judged |
| Christopher Malloy, *Engrafted into Christ* (2005) | Catholic | the Catholic paragraphs never name Trent's inherent [formal cause](#formal-cause), so the *simul*, a justification that never grows, and the denial of true merit pass unchallenged, Annex or no Annex |

One charge from opposite ends: the agreement is verbal. **Weight:** theologians' arguments, binding no church. A middle voice (3.4, Connections): Anthony Lane's Reformed evangelical assessment (2002), that agreement on justification would not by itself end the Reformation, because authority lies deeper. **Trap:** merging the early-1998 statement with a later 1999 appeal reported with larger figures; the latter is unverified.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Catholic Response 1998

The Catholic Church's official response to the Joint Declaration (25 June 1998), prepared by the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith with the Pontifical Council for Promoting Christian Unity.

**What it says (paraphrased; copyrighted).** A high degree of agreement, and the "consensus in basic truths" rightly stated, yet not yet a consensus that removes every difference. The chief difficulty is JDDJ §§28–30: the *simul* as §29 explains it is not acceptable to Catholics. Good works are the fruit of grace and of the man inwardly transformed, so eternal life is at once grace and the reward for works and merits (clarification 3). Some differences concern matters of substance, against §40, and §41's generic verdict cannot yet be affirmed, the *simul* first (clarification 5). Further study should take in the whole New Testament, not Paul alone (§7; 3.3).

**Level:** official; its assessments carry **no rung**; its doctrine keeps the weight of what it restates (Trent's). The Annex of 1999 answered it ([Official Common Statement and Annex](#official-common-statement-and-annex)). **Citation trap:** its note 4 labels DS 1528 "chap. 8"; DS 1528 is ch. 7.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

### Condign and congruous merit

Condign merit is merit in the full sense, a reward that matches the act as the Spirit's work; congruous merit is a fittingness without a claim.

**Levels.** The words are school terms no act uses: **rung 4**. Trent says "truly merit", never "condign", and chooses no school. Whether condign worth is intrinsic to grace (Thomists: "the worth of the work depends on the dignity of grace", q.114 a.3; the inheritance "due by right of adoption") or rests on God's free acceptance of an act done in charity (Scotists): **rung 5**.

**The Lutheran objection** (Apology Art. III §§195–203, Triglot numbering; Lutheran confessional standard) against meriting grace and eternal life *de condigno*, four reasons: it makes works from the habit of love a righteousness worthy by itself and robs Christ, "Mediator perpetually, and not merely in the beginning of justification"; consciences find no peace, since "the Law always accuses us, even in good works"; no one can tell when a work is condign, so the distinction was devised "to elude the Scriptures"; the whole Church confesses, with Augustine, that eternal life comes through mercy. **The reply (3.4 P2):** Trent makes Christ's virtue precede, accompany and follow every meritorious work (ch. 16), and can. 33 takes nothing from his merits.

**Traps.** "Trent defined condign merit" (3.4; [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), Flashback). Citing the Apology's arguments as §§195–201: the fourth runs to §203.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Created and uncreated grace

Uncreated grace is God himself given and dwelling in the just; created grace is the gift by which the soul itself is changed and made holy. Taught in [`creation-grace-last-things` 4.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md); this course argues from it.

**Levels as the lessons mark them.** Real participation in God, never one substance with him, and the indwelling: **rung 1** (5.4). Grace truly inheres (Trent VI can. 11): **rung 1** as worded. Grace a "quality": **rung 4** (5.4). The order of uncreated and created grace: **rung 5**; the Thomist answer is at most the schools' common account (**rung 4**), and Rahner, a Catholic, put God's self-gift first in 1939 (6.1).

**Uses.** *Merit (3.4):* Aquinas makes the indwelling Spirit the ground of merit's worth, "a sufficient cause of life everlasting" (q.114 a.3 ad 3). *Newman (3.4 Example 1):* the lesson relates his "Divine Presence" to this pair; Boss 3(c) asks which it is and which words of Trent VI ch. 7 decide it ([*Lectures on Justification*](#lectures-on-justification)). *Palamas (5.4 P2–P3):* the term of the Latin saint's share is uncreated; created grace is the change in the creature that makes it able to receive God, not a substitute for him, against the charge that Latin grace makes deification a metaphor ([essence and energies](#essence-and-energies)). **Trap (6.1):** treating a manuals' consensus on the order as rung 3.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Lectures on Justification

John Henry Newman's *Lectures on the Doctrine of Justification* (1838, as an Anglican; third edition 1874, with a Catholic-era Advertisement and notes): an attempt at a *via media* between the Lutheran and Roman schemes.

**Standing:** an Anglican's lectures, **no confessional or magisterial weight**; the 1874 notes are a Catholic theologian's, **no level**.

**What the lessons teach, in outline.** Lecture 6, "The Gift of Righteousness": §3 judges bare imputation "no answer at all"; §7 is the thesis sentence, quoted in 3.4 Example 1 and supplied in Boss 3. The 1874 Advertisement says the lectures' drift was "to show that there is little difference but what is verbal in the various views on justification", withdraws whatever contradicts Trent's single formal cause, and notes that Trent did not fix what the one form is (Pallavicino: the fathers wrote now "grace", now "charity", to avoid deciding whether they are one thing or two).

**Testing it is Boss 3(c)'s work.** The texts it names: against the Formula, Epitome III neg. 4 and Solid Declaration III.54 (3.1, the Formula paragraph; [Osiander controversy](#osiander-controversy)); against Trent, ch. 7's formal-cause wording ([formal cause](#formal-cause); [created and uncreated grace](#created-and-uncreated-grace)). What a Catholic can take is the last two sentences of 3.4 Example 1. The verdicts are the student's. **Trap:** treating the lectures as Catholic authority.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

**The Church, the papacy, and the Reformation's alternatives (Module 4)**

### Petrine texts

Matthew 16:17–19, Luke 22:31–32 and John 21:15–17: the passages in which Christ singles out Peter. Laid out, with the Greek and the Aramaic question, in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts.md); argued in 4.1.

**Singular beside shared (4.1 P1).** To the Twelve: binding and loosing (Matthew 18:18), forgiving and retaining (John 20:23), the thrones (Luke 22:30). To Peter: the name and the keys (Matthew 16:18–19, *soi*); the prayer and the charge to strengthen (Luke 22:32, *sou*, after the plural *hymas* of v. 31); the flock (John 21:15–17, *poimaine*). Luke 22:26 forbids Gentile lordship yet presupposes "he that is the greater" and "the leader" (*ho hēgoumenos*, the word Hebrews 13 uses for church leaders).

**The readings, by text.** Lutheran: Melanchthon's [*Treatise*](#treatise-on-the-power-and-primacy-of-the-pope) §§22–30, Peter "the representative of the entire assembly of apostles" (§23), the rock "this ministry" of his confession (§25). Reformed confessions argue from the keys and Christ's headship ([power of the keys](#power-of-the-keys); [key of David](#key-of-david)). Calvin, *Institutes* IV.6.4–8 (a theologian's): the rock the confession, rank without power. Cullmann (a Lutheran exegete, 1952): the rock is Peter, a foundation laid once. The Orthodox reading is 5.2's.

**Levels.** *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 1's reading, the primacy promised in Matthew 16 and conferred in John 21: chapter teaching, **at least rung 3**; ch. 4's use of Luke 22:32 for the see: taught, not defined; the referent of "this rock": **rung 5**. **4.1's Concession:** Matthew 16 alone does not reach an office with successors; the exegesis gets you Peter, not the papacy. **Traps:** "the Protestant reading is rock = Christ, *Petros* a pebble" (name the text); "Vatican I defined that Matthew 16 and John 21 prove the primacy".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### Petrine function and Petrine office

A function is what Peter does in the New Testament (confesses, speaks for the others, strengthens, shepherds); an office is that function made permanent, held by successors, located in Rome. The distinction is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts.md)'s.

**The three steps (4.1).** *Exegetical:* do the texts single Peter out? *Perpetuity:* does what he was given continue after him? *Rome:* if so, where? The texts carry the first well; the second needs the [perpetuity argument](#perpetuity-argument); the third needs the historical evidence of 4.2. Cullmann's own Part Two divides "the exegetical question" from "the application of Matthew 16:17–19 to the later church" the same way. Each step needs its own evidence: answering Calvin's challenge about Rome (IV.6.11) with Matthew 16 brings evidence for a step already granted (4.1 Example 1).

**The strain (4.1 Example 2).** Judas's "bishopric" (*episkopēn*) is filled (Acts 1:20–26); James the son of Zebedee is not replaced (Acts 12:2). CCC 860 splits the apostolic office into an aspect that cannot pass on (chosen witnesses of the Resurrection, "foundation stones") and a pastoral aspect that continues; CCC 881 calls Peter's pastoral office part of the foundation, "continued" by the bishops under the pope. The texts do not sort Peter's gifts into the two aspects: the keys and the flock carry the Catholic sorting better than the rock. That sorting is the Church's reading, not the page's.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### Petrine primacy

Peter's primacy of true and proper jurisdiction over the whole Church, received from Christ directly.

**Levels.** **Rung 1**, the canon of [*Pastor Aeternus*](#pastor-aeternus) ch. 1: a primacy of true jurisdiction, not of [honour](#primacy-of-honour) only. That Peter has perpetual successors by divine right and that the Roman pontiff is that successor: **rung 1**, the canon of ch. 2. Florence's primacy definition (1439): **rung 1**, the sense of its *quemadmodum* clause disputed (4.2). LG 22's restatement of the primacy: **rung 1** (5.3).

**What the other side grants.** Calvin gives Peter a place as "the first among the faithful" (IV.6.5), rank but not power; in IV.6.11 he even concedes, for argument, a primacy "fixed in Peter, with the view of remaining for ever by perpetual succession", and fights at Rome. Cullmann reads the rock as Peter and denies the succession.

**Traps.** "Protestant scholars admit the rock is Peter, so they concede the papacy": the exegetical step does not carry the succession. The mirror: "since the exegesis stops at Peter, succession is open for Catholics": the ch. 2 canon is **rung 1**; 4.1's concession concerns what the page proves, not what the Church holds.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### Primacy of honour

First place among sees by rank or dignity, without jurisdiction.

**Catholic level.** *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 1's canon excludes a primacy of honour only: **rung 1**.

**As the Reformation counter-reading (4.2).** Rome's rank was produced by the capital and the apostles' graves and enlarged by human law; its strongest text is [canon 28 of Chalcedon](#canon-28-of-chalcedon). But neither Reformation text denies Rome all standing: the Lutheran *Treatise* has Rome's authority arise "by a human law" (§12), and Melanchthon would concede the pope's superiority "by human right" if he allowed the Gospel; Calvin grants "high honour" (IV.6.16) and Peter rank (IV.6.5). Westminster XXV.6 and Article XXXVII deny headship and jurisdiction without arguing the record.

**As the Orthodox use it (5.2).** Orthodox writers do say "primacy of honour": the Moscow Synod's 2013 *Position*, one church's act, calls the first millennium's universal primacy one of honour and of human institution. Zizioulas calls the phrase misleading, since primacy carries real duties; the joint Alexandria text (2023, §5.1) says that for the Orthodox it is "not merely honorific". Never write that Orthodoxy has dropped the phrase; you may call it an inadequate summary of the categories.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Perpetuity argument

A charge Christ gave for a lasting need of the Church is presumptively given for as long as the need lasts.

**Text and level.** It is *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 2's own reasoning: what Christ established in Peter for the Church's perpetual welfare "must, by the same institution, necessarily remain unceasingly in the Church" (Schaff vol. 2). The reasoning is the act's; its content is carried at **rung 1** by the ch. 2 canon (perpetual successors by divine right). In 4.1 it is P5, resting on P3: the needs the singular gifts answer (a Church Hades will not overpower, brethren to strengthen, a flock to feed) outlast Peter.

**The crux (4.1).** The Reformation reader: a charge given to a person ends with him unless the text says otherwise (Calvin's "personal" horn; Cullmann's foundation laid once). The Catholic: a charge given for a lasting need continues unless the text says otherwise. What would move it: New Testament evidence of a personal charge continuing after its holder, or a principled way to tell the unrepeatable parts of an apostle's commission from the permanent ones, which the Catholic side owes ([burden of the Catholic claim](#burden-of-the-catholic-claim)). The New Testament's silence about a successor neither denies nor attests one ([argument from silence](#argument-from-silence)). 4.3's Flashback marks it as the step a Petrine argument still has to take.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Key of David

The key of the house of David laid on the royal steward Eliacim (Isaiah 22:22), and held by Christ in Revelation 3:7.

**Catholic use (4.1 P4).** In Isaiah 22:20–22 the king's key is laid on Eliacim with the robe and power taken from Sobna: an office passing between holders. Revelation 3:7 fits the image, since the king holds the key and lays keys on a steward; so the keys of Matthew 16:19 read as a steward's. The close reading of Isaiah 22 is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts.md) P1, not re-set.

**Reformed use.** The Second Helvetic Confession (XVIII.13–14; Bullinger, 1566) reads Isaiah 22:22 and Revelation 3:7 as Christ's own absolute power, which he "does not transfer"; ministers hold a steward's "ministerial power", which "is the same and alike in all" (XVIII.16). A Reformed confession, "adopted, or at least highly approved" across the Reformed churches (Schaff); no Catholic level.

**Where it turns.** Both sides grant keys under Christ; the dispute is whether Christ set one steward over the house.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### Power of the keys

The authority to bind and loose, to open and shut the kingdom (Matthew 16:19; 18:18; John 20:23).

| Tradition | Text | Who holds the keys, and what they do | Standing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lutheran | *Treatise* §§23–24 (1537) | given to all the apostles alike; "principally and immediately" to the Church | Book of Concord |
| Reformed | Westminster XXX.1–4 | "to these officers the keys ... are committed": retain and remit, shut by the Word and censures, open by the gospel and absolution from censures; admonition, suspension from the Supper, excommunication | Presbyterian subordinate standard |
| Reformed | Heidelberg Q.83–85 | "the preaching of the holy Gospel and Church discipline" | Reformed catechism with confessional standing |
| Reformed | Second Helvetic XIV.8; XVIII.13–16 | every minister truly called exercises them when he preaches; one ministerial power, alike in all | Reformed confession |
| Catholic | LG 22 (CCC 881) | the binding and loosing given to Peter was granted also to the college joined with its head | **rung 3 at its heaviest** |

**Uses later.** 4.4: the Reformed visible Church does with the keys exactly what the New Testament Church does, which is why "no church authority" caricatures it. 6.3: both sides grant that the keys bind and loose (the Smalcald Articles III.7 say so); the dispute is whether binding includes imposing satisfactions on the forgiven. 6.5 (Flashback): the keys retain as well as forgive (Trent XIV can. 3, **rung 1**), so absolution is a judgment that needs knowledge of the case, against the Lutheran denial of enumeration.

**Traps.** Giving the *Treatise*'s "immediately to the Church" as Westminster's doctrine (Westminster commits them to officers: a real Lutheran–Reformed difference); "the Reformed deny church authority"; "Matthew 18:18 is only about quarrels".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Indefectibility

The Church will not fail as Christ's Church: she lasts to the end, holding the faith and the sacraments, and the whole body cannot err in believing.

**Level.** **At least rung 3**: presupposed by rung-1 acts, defined as a thesis by none (7.3). The whole body's inability to err in believing (*in credendo*): **at least rung 3**, restated in *Lumen Gentium* 12 ([`fundamental-theology` 5.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-03-the-sensus-fidei.md)). It promises no one impeccability ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-04-a-church-of-sinners.md)) and says nothing about conduct or governance.

**Uses.** 4.1: the Catholic reading of "the gates of hell shall not prevail" (Matthew 16:18). 4.4: the frame for the 1378–1417 [Western Schism](#western-schism). 7.1 (P2): the Church will not lose the faith, yet her officers can act wrongly, so the distinction answers the doctrinal charge and not the moral one. 7.2: it promises nothing about conduct. 7.3: the first of the four answers [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-04-a-church-of-sinners.md) owns, from indefectibility to the [*corpus permixtum*](#corpus-permixtum).

**Traps.** "The gates of hell mean no pope can err": that is infallibility, defined only under four conditions ([*Pastor Aeternus*](#pastor-aeternus) ch. 4). "Indefectibility promises good governance", or the reverse, "it is only an opinion" (7.3).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Pastor Aeternus

Vatican I's dogmatic constitution on the Church of Christ (18 July 1870), defining the papal primacy (chs. 1–3) and infallibility (ch. 4). Read line by line in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-03-vatican-i-and-pastor-aeternus.md); cited from Schaff vol. 2.

| Part | Content | Level as the lessons mark it | Lessons |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ch. 1 canon | Peter's primacy of true jurisdiction, received directly from Christ; not of honour only | **rung 1** | 4.1 |
| Ch. 1 text | the primacy promised in Matthew 16, conferred in John 21 | chapter teaching, **at least rung 3** | 4.1 |
| Ch. 2 canon | perpetual successors by divine right; the Roman pontiff that successor | **rung 1** | 4.1, 4.2 |
| Ch. 2 text | the [perpetuity argument](#perpetuity-argument); Rome "founded by him, and consecrated by his blood"; the quotation of the legate Philip at Ephesus | the act's reasoning, **taught, not defined** | 4.1, 4.2 |
| Ch. 3 canon | full, supreme, ordinary and immediate jurisdiction ([universal ordinary jurisdiction](#universal-ordinary-jurisdiction)); communion with Peter's successor a bond of unity | **rung 1** | 4.2, 4.4, 5.2, 5.3 |
| Ch. 3 text | the protection of the bishops' own power | **at least rung 3** (LG 27) | 5.3 |
| Ch. 4 definition | [infallibility](#infallibility) when the pope speaks ex cathedra, defining a doctrine of faith or morals, with *ex sese* | **rung 1** | 5.3, 7.1, 7.3 |
| Ch. 4 text | Luke 22:32 applied to the see | **taught, not defined** | 4.1 |

Vatican I's legitimacy as an ecumenical council: **rung 2** (the CDF's 1998 commentary, §11; 5.3). The three things it did not define: [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-03-vatican-i-and-pastor-aeternus.md).

**Uses.** 5.3: the sharper Orthodox objection is ch. 3, not ch. 4, with "of themselves, and not from the consent of the Church" read as cutting the pope off from the body. 7.1 and 7.3: the guarantee covers definitions, not every Roman act or every pope (Honorius). **Traps.** "Vatican I defined that Matthew 16 and John 21 prove the primacy"; "it defined the history it tells"; "Vatican I defined that popes do not err"; the opposite slip, calling the defined primacy "a reading of the evidence".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Claimed, conceded, defined

[`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-02-the-development-of-papal-claims-read-honestly.md)'s three questions for any piece of primacy evidence: what Rome claimed, what others conceded, what the Church has since defined.

**Run in 4.2 (Example 1) on Calvin, *Institutes* IV.6.16.** *Conceded* by Calvin: the early Church "uniformly" honoured Rome, first because they believed Peter founded it; and his third cause, Rome as a "haven" for bishops driven from their sees, is the appeal function under another name. His second cause, the seat of empire, is canon 28's, which Leo rejected. What Calvin denies is the next step: "on this" no primacy of authority can be reared (IV.6.17). The questions narrow the dispute to 4.2's crux without pretending the honour was jurisdiction.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Nicaea canon 6

Nicaea's canon (325) confirming the bishop of Alexandria's jurisdiction over Egypt, Libya and Pentapolis "since the like is customary for the Bishop of Rome also" (Percival).

**What 4.2 records.** Percival's notes on the Latin versions: Rufinus's *suburbicariarum ecclesiarum* for Rome's area, which the Lutheran *Treatise* follows ("suburban"); the Prisca's *principatum* for Rome's bishop; manuscripts opening "The Church of Rome hath always had a primacy", the form the legate Paschasinus read at Chalcedon. It is the base text of the [*Treatise*](#treatise-on-the-power-and-primacy-of-the-pope) §12 (the Triglot's English heads it "VI"): from this start by a human law, the Roman bishop's authority "first arose". Hefele, the Catholic historian whom Percival prints, judged that the canon has in view Rome's power as a patriarch, not its primacy over the whole Church (4.2's Concession).

**Level:** law, **no doctrinal rung**. **Boss 4(a)** close-reads the canon against the *Treatise* §12: what it grants Alexandria and on what ground, what the Rome clause does and does not say, what the Latin versions add, which of the *Treatise*'s claims it supports. The verdicts are the student's.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Canon 28 of Chalcedon

Chalcedon's canon (451) giving Constantinople privileges equal to Old Rome's and second rank, grounding Old Rome's own privileges in its being the royal city.

**Text (Percival).** "The Fathers rightly granted privileges to the throne of old Rome, because it was the royal city." Constantinople I's canon 3 (381) had ranked Constantinople next "because Constantinople is New Rome"; Chalcedon's canon 9 sends appeals against a metropolitan "to the throne of the Imperial City of Constantinople". Passed in the Roman legates' absence. Leo refused it (Letter CIV.3 to Marcian, 22 May 452): "things secular stand on a different basis from things divine", and a royal city cannot be made an apostolic see.

**Level:** law, **no doctrinal rung** (Eastern law, refused by Leo). **Weight in the argument.** 4.2: the counter-reading's strongest single text, and real evidence of a rival theory of precedence the East kept; but it explains Constantinople's rise, not Rome's role (P3). 5.2, setting out the *taxis* of sees and the [pentarchy](#pentarchy), cites it for grounding both sees' privileges in their being imperial cities. Its close reading, with Leo's grounds, is `church-history` Boss 2, not re-set; the record is [`church-history` 2.2](../church-history/lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md).

**Traps.** Treating it as the whole Church's theory; or waving it away (4.4, Flashback).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Sardica

The Western-dominated synod of 343 or 344, never counted ecumenical, whose canons let a condemned bishop's case be referred to the bishop of Rome.

**Canon 3 (Percival's Greek).** Julius may decide whether a condemned bishop's case is retried, to "honour the memory of Peter the Apostle". Most Eastern bishops had left. It is a concession by a synod, not a Roman claim (4.2 P2). Percival's Greek and Latin versions diverge; Zosimus later quoted the companion canon as Nicene ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md), Flashback).

**Level:** law, **no doctrinal rung**. **Trap:** "no appeals to Rome before the forged decretals": Sardica is some five centuries older than the [Pseudo-Isidorian Decretals](#pseudo-isidorian-decretals).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Primacy of appeal and communion

4.2's name for what the first-millennium record proves: a role as court of appeal, doctrinal reference and centre of communion, on a Petrine warrant, claimed by Rome and conceded by others when it mattered.

**The evidence (4.2 P1–P3).** Rome grounds its role in Peter, never in the city: the legate Philip at Ephesus (431), Peter "lives and judges in his successors"; Leo against canon 28. Others concede more than precedence at decisive moments: [Sardica](#sardica)'s appeal canon; Chalcedon's "Peter has spoken thus through Leo" (451) and Constantinople III's "Peter spoke through Agatho" (681), both after testing the letters. The capital explains Constantinople's rise, not Rome's role.

**What it does not prove.** By itself, [universal ordinary jurisdiction](#universal-ordinary-jurisdiction); that step needs [development](#development-of-doctrine). The CDF's 1998 commentary on the Profession of Faith (§11) says the primacy was "always believed as a revealed fact", while whether the conceptual working-out of "jurisdiction" belonged to revelation stayed open until 1870. Whether the record by itself proves jurisdiction is a historian's judgment, **no rung**.

**The crux (4.2).** One power used sparingly, or a court of correction among equals? Gregory the Great (Letter IX.59, Barmby): "if any fault is found in bishops, I know not what bishop is not subject to it"; but "when no fault requires it to be otherwise, all according to the principle of humility are equal". What would move it: first-millennium cases of Rome exercising ordinary jurisdiction in another patriarchate with no fault or appeal in question, or a reason why power to judge any bishop "if any fault is found" is not already jurisdiction over all.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Universal ordinary jurisdiction

The pope's full, supreme, ordinary and immediate jurisdiction over all churches, pastors and faithful (*Pastor Aeternus* ch. 3).

**Level.** **Rung 1** (ch. 3 and its canon). Ch. 3's protection of the bishops' own ordinary power: **at least rung 3** (LG 27; 5.3).

**What the first millennium shows (4.2).** Not this, by itself: the record proves a [primacy of appeal and communion](#primacy-of-appeal-and-communion), and reaching jurisdiction needs a theory of development. "Honour only" fails the record; so does "1870 already" in the second century.

**The Orthodox objection (5.3).** In a [eucharistic ecclesiology](#eucharistic-ecclesiology) each bishop's church is the whole Church in its place, so a power that enters it from outside by divine right makes it a branch. Infallibility is used rarely and under conditions; this power reaches every diocese every day, which is why ch. 3, not ch. 4, is the sharper objection.

**The Catholic answers.** 5.2's conclusion: a universal primacy with real power is no category error in eucharistic ecclesiology; its categories exclude a primacy that makes the local church a branch, and Catholic doctrine excludes that too (bishops are not "vicars of the Roman Pontiffs", LG 27). Whether "ordinary and immediate" fits is 5.3's question, answered with a cost at each step; defending 1870 by quietly emptying ch. 3 is the [minimalist temptation](#minimalist-temptation) 5.3 refuses.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Honorius

Pope from 625 to 638, anathematized by Constantinople III (681) for his letter to Sergius; Leo II confirmed the council.

**The strain (4.2 Example 2).** The same council received Agatho's letter (read at the fourth session, 15 November 680), which says the Roman church "has never erred from the path of the apostolic tradition". Its address to the emperor answers "Peter spoke through Agatho", and in the same address anathematizes "Honorius, who was the ruler (proedron) of Rome" (Percival). A Catholic reads a see whose faith held while one occupant failed; a Lutheran or Orthodox reader, a council that received Agatho's letter because it was orthodox, not as orthodox because it was Roman. The acts support both.

**Level.** The anathema is a historical fact, **not a level**. What it does to infallibility is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)'s Example 2: the letter met none of the 1870 conditions. It is one of 4.2's four written-in concessions: an ecumenical council anathematized a pope, and a pope confirmed it. The doctrine at stake is [`christology` 2.5](../christology/lessons/02-05-monothelitism-and-constantinople-iii.md)'s. **Traps:** denying the condemnation; answering it as though Vatican I had defined that popes do not err (7.3).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Tierney thesis

Brian Tierney's argument (*Origins of Papal Infallibility, 1150–1350*, 1972) that the doctrine of irreformable papal teaching was first urged about 1280 by the Franciscan Peter John Olivi, to bind later popes to *Exiit qui seminat*, and was resisted by John XXII.

**Who and what.** Tierney was a medieval historian (Catholic University of America, then Cornell), not a Protestant theologian, as the syllabus's Sources once listed him; the lessons name no confession for him (unverified). Alfons Stickler, prefect of the Vatican Library, disputed the thesis in a 1974 published exchange. The thesis is set out in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-02-the-development-of-papal-claims-read-honestly.md), Example 2.

**Level:** a historian's thesis, **no magisterial level**. **Trap:** using it to show "the papacy is medieval": it concerns infallibility as irreformable teaching, not the first-millennium primacy.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Apostolic succession

The handing on of the apostles' office, and in Catholic, Orthodox and Anglican teaching of orders, through a continuous line of episcopal ordination. The doctrine is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-04-apostolic.md) and [3.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-01-bishops-and-apostolic-succession.md)'s; orders as a sacrament, [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)'s.

**Two questions 4.3 keeps apart.** *Must a ministry come through a chain of ordinations at all?* The Lutheran and Reformed confessions say no ([rite vocatus](#rite-vocatus); [succession in doctrine](#succession-in-doctrine)); Rome, the Orthodox and the Anglicans say yes. *Did this chain carry what it claimed?* The validity question, on which Rome parts from Canterbury, which claimed the succession ([*Apostolicae Curae*](#apostolicae-curae); [*Saepius Officio*](#saepius-officio)). The Reformed do not lose the second argument; they decline to enter it.

**Levels.** Orders a sacrament conferring a character and a priesthood with power to consecrate and offer: **rung 1** (Trent XXIII cans. 1, 3, 4). Bishops succeeding the apostles by divine institution: **at least rung 3** (LG 20; 4.1, 4.3). UR 22's sorting of communions by orders: **rung 3**. **Trap:** "the Reformation churches simply rejected apostolic succession" blurs three positions: the Anglicans claimed it, the Apology wished to keep the bishops' grades, and the Second Helvetic ordains with hands and places succession in doctrine.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Rite vocatus

Augsburg XIV: no one may publicly teach or administer the sacraments "unless he be regularly called" (*rite vocatus*; Triglot).

**The Lutheran account of ministry (4.3).** Augsburg V: the ministry of word and sacrament is instituted by God (stated in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-04-apostolic.md)). The Apology (XIV) calls it "our greatest wish to maintain church-polity and the grades in the Church", grades made "by human authority", if the bishops would tolerate the evangelical doctrine. Melanchthon's [*Treatise*](#treatise-on-the-power-and-primacy-of-the-pope) holds with Jerome that bishop and pastor are one grade by divine right, so "ordination administered by a pastor in his own church is valid by divine law" (§65), and when bishops refuse, the churches keep their own right to call, elect and ordain (§§66–67). **Standing:** Book of Concord, a *norma normata* Lutheran pastors subscribe; no Catholic level.

**Traps** (5.1's Flashback). "Lutherans, like the Reformed, call ordination no sacrament": the Apology is "not unwilling to call ordination a sacrament" if it is understood of the ministry of the Word (XIII.11); the Second Helvetic (XIX) is the confession that denies it. "Lutherans abolished bishops". Answering a Lutheran with *Apostolicae Curae*, which judges Canterbury's rite, not Wittenberg's doctrine.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Succession in doctrine

The Reformed (and broadly Lutheran) account of apostolicity: what passes from the apostles is their doctrine and a lawfully called ministry, not a sacrificing priesthood carried by episcopal succession. A position, not an evasion.

**Texts.** The Second Helvetic Confession (Bullinger, 1566): the ministry is "a most ancient ordinance of God himself, not a new device appointed by men"; ministers are lawfully elected and "ordained by the elders with public prayer, and laying on of hands"; ministry and priesthood are "things far different" (all believers are priests; ministers do not offer the host); bishops rank over elders by custom, not divine rule (XVIII). Churches without the true marks gain nothing when they "brag of the succession of bishops" (XVII); ordination is "very profitable, but no sacrament" (XIX). The Belgic Confession (1561, XXXI) agrees: lawful election, equal power of ministers. **Standing:** Reformed subordinate standards; no Catholic level.

**Consequence (4.3 Example 1).** Read a Swiss Reformed ordination by the archbishops' own test, the public formulary: the confession says the minister is no offering priest and ordination no sacrament, so nothing Rome calls priestly orders is conferred, and the Reformed agree. The dispute sits wholly at whether Christ instituted a sacrificing priesthood (Trent XXIII cans. 1, 3, **rung 1**), argued in [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Edwardine Ordinal

The English ordination rite of 1550 (revised 1552), with the additions of 1662. There was no 1549 Ordinal.

**What it says.** The 1550 preface claims bishops, priests and deacons "from the Apostles tyme" and intends them "continued". The priest's form (1550 and 1552): "RECEIVE the holy goste, whose synnes thou doest forgeve, they are forgeven", ending "be thou a faithful despensor of the word of god, and of his holy Sacramentes". The bishop's form begins "TAKE the holy gost". In 1550 the new priest also received the chalice with the bread; 1552 dropped it. In 1662 the forms gained "for the office and work of a Priest" (and "of a Bishop"). Article XXXVI says the book "doth conteyne all thinges necessarie to suche consecration and orderyng" (Schaff, 1571 English). **Standing:** an Anglican rite; the Articles are formularies whose subscription varies across the Communion.

**In the dispute.** *Apostolicae Curae* judges its form ([defect of form](#defect-of-form)) and reads it with its deletions ([native character of the Ordinal](#native-character-of-the-ordinal)); the archbishops answer that the 1550 priest's form suits "no other ministry of the Church but that of a Priest" (*Saepius* XV) and record what was removed, the formula giving "the power of offering sacrifice" among it (XVIII). 6.3: the Ordinal of 1550, like that of 1662, ordains each priest with John 20:23, though Article XXV denies penance a place among the sacraments of the Gospel. Boss 4 supplies the 1550 priest's form.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Apostolicae Curae

Leo XIII's apostolic letter of 13 September 1896 declaring that ordinations according to the Anglican rite "have been and are absolutely null and utterly void" (Art and Book Company's 1896 English).

**Genre and level.** An apostolic letter, **not a bull**. **Rung 2, as the CDF's 1998 *Doctrinal Commentary* on the *Professio fidei* (§11) classifies it**, among truths connected with revelation by historical necessity yet "not able to be declared as divinely revealed". Some Catholic theologians dispute whether an illustrative list, given "without any intention of completeness or exhaustiveness", fixes a level, since its classifications weigh **rung 3, light**, at most; older writers held Leo's act itself infallible, a judgment on a [dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact) (the 1907 *Catholic Encyclopedia*). **On no reading is the question open:** the floor is a papal judgment given "by Our authority, of Our own motion and certain knowledge" (§36, *certa scientia*). Never rung 1; the commentary does not call it a dogmatic fact. Rejecting it falls under can. 750 §2, not heresy under can. 751; the word "infallible" is not itself the error (rung-2 truths are held on the magisterium's infallibility in such matters), "dogma", "heresy" and "defined" are. The level, and the sacramental doctrine, are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)'s; 4.3 argues the letter.

**Its three supports, in outline** (Boss 4(b) asks for them stated): the practice of the Holy See, from Julius III and Paul IV to the Gordon case decided in 1704 (§§7–22), which it says had already settled the question; the [defect of form](#defect-of-form) (§§24–32); the [defect of intention](#defect-of-intention) (§33). The practice confirms; it is not the argument (4.3, ∴ C). §§20–21 set aside both Parker's consecration and the missing tradition of the instruments.

**Since 1896.** A particular Anglican line since 1932, with an Old Catholic co-consecrator: **rung 5**, judged case by case; in 1994 Cardinal Hume ordained Graham Leonard priest conditionally (can. 845 §2), citing a "prudent doubt", and reaffirmed the letter (4.3 Example 2). [ARCIC](#arcic) asked for "a reappraisal" with no level of its own; Rome's position is unrevised. **Citation traps:** two 1896 English versions circulated, so name the one you quote (4.3 uses the Art and Book Company pamphlet, ¶¶1–10, and cites §§ in the common 1–41 numbering; *Saepius* cites the pamphlet's ¶ numbers); Boss 4 asks for the authorised Burns & Oates translation, to be confirmed before quoting. **Traps:** "a bull"; "an ex cathedra dogma"; "a disciplinary ruling ARCIC reopened"; "two grounds" (it gives three supports).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Defect of form

*Apostolicae Curae*'s second support: the Ordinal's form does not definitely signify the priesthood and its chief power, to consecrate and offer.

**The letter's steps (1896 English).** The matter, the laying on of hands, "by itself signifies nothing definite" (§24); "Receive the Holy Ghost" does not express the priesthood (§25); the 1662 words "for the office and work of a priest" came too late (§26); from the other prayers everything about the priesthood was deliberately removed (§27); the same for bishops (§§28–29); the [native character](#native-character-of-the-ordinal) (§§30–31); even the prayer "Almighty God, giver of all good things" will not serve (§32). Behind it: orders confers a priesthood with power to consecrate and offer (Trent XXIII cans. 1, 3, 4, **rung 1**), so there is a definite thing to signify (4.3 P1–P2). Matter and form as Pius XII fixed them (1947): [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md).

**Shared ground.** The archbishops agree that the matter is the laying on of hands and the form a prayer fitting the ministry (*Saepius* VIII–IX); they dispute sufficiency (XV) and the test ([proves too much](#proves-too-much)). **Trap:** "it turned on the missing chalice" (the porrection of instruments): §21 sets that aside.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Defect of intention

*Apostolicae Curae*'s third support (§33): a rite changed with the manifest intention of rejecting what the Church does carries an intention "adverse to and destructive of the Sacrament".

**The shared rule and the split.** The Church judges intention only as it is manifested externally; the archbishops agree (*Saepius* VIII) but read it from the public formularies, the Ordinal's preface above all (XVII), "not from its omissions and reforms". *Saepius* V treats intention as "attached" to the question of form.

**Cases (4.3).** Example 1: a Reformed ordination, read by the archbishops' own test, intends no offering priest, and its church agrees. Example 2: can an Old Catholic co-consecrator's intention, from a church that holds the Eucharistic sacrifice, supply what the rite lacks? *Apostolicae Curae* judged the rite, not this minister, and no act answers in general; Rome has answered a case (Leonard, 1994). *Saepius* ch. XX is the archbishops' counter on intention ([proves too much](#proves-too-much)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Native character of the Ordinal

*Apostolicae Curae* §§30–31: the Ordinal's words must be read in the light of how and why it was composed, by deliberate removal of the sacrifice and the sacrificing priesthood (Latin *nativa Ordinalis indoles ac spiritus*).

**The key sentence (§31, 1896 English).** Words in the Ordinal that "lend themselves to ambiguity, cannot be taken in the same sense as they possess in the Catholic rite". It is Leo's answer in advance to the ancient-forms parity: silence inside a sacrificing Church is not deletion (4.3 P3–P5, with the councillors'-oath miniature in "The idea").

**Where it sits.** 4.3's crux is the dispute over it, set against the archbishops' rule of reading intention "not from its omissions" (*Saepius* VIII); the lesson states the crux and what would move each side (**The crux.**). Boss 4(b) asks for the premise the two documents do not share; the statement is the student's.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Saepius Officio

The reply of the archbishops of Canterbury (Frederick Temple) and York (William Maclagan) to *Apostolicae Curae*, dated 19 February 1897; English version issued with the Latin (Longmans, 1897).

**Standing:** a letter of the two primates, not a synodical act: it carries its signatories' authority, not a synod's; **no Catholic level**.

**Its chapters, in outline** (Boss 4(b) asks for chs. V and XX in full). V: what Leo's judgment hinges on, with intention "attached". VI–VII: the Roman practice uncertain, the Gordon case faulted. VIII–IX: agreement on matter, form, and intention as "externally manifested", read from public formularies, "not from its omissions and reforms". XI: the Anglican doctrine of the Eucharistic sacrifice. XII: the ancient Roman forms (4.3's Source). XIV–XV: the 1550 forms sufficient; the priest's form suits "no other ministry of the Church but that of a Priest". XVII: the Ordinal's preface as the declared intention. XVIII: the ceremonies removed. XX: [proves too much](#proves-too-much).

**Traps.** It did not compare the Roman rites of 1550, whose Pontifical named the power of offering sacrifice outright (XVIII); it went to the ancient forms and the East. Summaries that have it point to the Ordinal's own sacrificial language are inaccurate; Schaff misprints its year as 1877.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Proves too much

The archbishops' argument that Leo's reasoning, applied evenly, would void ordinations Rome itself holds valid.

**Two strands.** *Ch. XII, the ancient Roman forms:* the third-century formulary (the *Canons of Hippolytus*, in Achelis's 1891 edition) and the old Roman Sacramentary, "perhaps" of the sixth century, say nothing of sacrifice or high priesthood, yet the form "is undoubtedly valid"; so either those formulas "were valueless", or Trent is no test of what an ordination prayer must say. A dilemma resting on a premise Rome shares, the validity of the ancient forms. *Ch. XX, the East and Rome's own predecessors:* the same reasoning turned on the Eastern churches, read from their catechisms (the Orthodox Confession of about 1640; the Russian Longer Catechism, 1839), and on Rome's own Fathers. 4.3 states it in "The other side"; Boss 4(b) asks for ch. XX's argument in full.

**Weight in 4.3.** The lesson's Concession: the ancient-forms argument is serious, so the Ordinal's silence about sacrifice cannot, taken alone, be its defect. Leo's answer in advance is the [native character](#native-character-of-the-ordinal). **Trap:** "the archbishops compared the Roman rites of the same period" (XVIII shows the sixteenth-century Pontifical was explicit).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### ARCIC

The Anglican–Roman Catholic International Commission (ARCIC I, 1970–81; ARCIC II from 1983), a dialogue commission.

**On orders.** *Ministry and Ordination* (Canterbury, 1973, §17) says the agreement puts the issues of *Apostolicae Curae* "in a new context"; the *Elucidation* (1979, §6) calls for "a reappraisal" of its verdict. Rome's replies: the CDF's *Observations* (1982) and the Catholic Response (1991, the CDF with the PCPCU) name the ordination of women a further, doctrinal obstacle ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)), and the 1991 Response found substantial agreement not yet reached. Rome's position is unrevised.
