**Level:** dialogue reports, **no Catholic level**; the 1991 Response is official, its assessments without a rung of their own. **Traps:** treating ARCIC as Rome's teaching; "a disciplinary ruling ARCIC reopened" understates *Apostolicae Curae*.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Visible and invisible Church

The Reformation distinction between the Church as God sees it (the elect, or the true believers, known fully to God alone) and the Church as men see it (the professing body, mixed, known by its marks).

**Texts.** Lutheran: the Apology (VII–VIII) calls the Church "originally a fellowship of faith and of the Holy Ghost in hearts", yet "Neither, indeed, are we dreaming of a Platonic state" (Triglot §20): it exists on earth with outward marks. Reformed: Calvin says Scripture speaks of the Church "in two ways" (*Institutes* IV.1.7), "to God alone must be left the knowledge of his Church" (IV.1.2), and the visible Church is the believers' mother (IV.1.4). Westminster XXV.1: the invisible catholic Church is "the whole number of the elect"; XXV.2: the visible is all who profess the true religion, with their children, "out of which there is no ordinary possibility of salvation"; XXV.3 gives it the ministry and ordinances. The Church proper is the true believers for the Apology (who can fall), the elect for Calvin and Westminster.

**Catholic levels.** The Church a visible society, one complex reality with the Mystical Body: **at least rung 3** (LG 8; *Mystici Corporis* §14, **rung 3 at its heaviest**, aimed at an invisible bond uniting differing communities, not at Westminster). Constance condemned Hus's article that the universal Church is "the totality of the predestined" by a [global censure](#in-globo-censure): its contradictory **at least rung 3, no precise note**; whether Westminster XXV.1 falls under it, **rung 5** (Westminster, unlike Hus, counts reprobate professors in the visible Church).

**The real target (4.4).** Not "an invisible Church", which no confession teaches, but the claim that the Church in its truest sense is the hidden company, known on earth by marks; the elect as such cannot hold a meeting (P2), and the confessions answer by giving the visible Church real authority (P3). **Traps:** "the Reformers taught an invisible Church" (4.4; made from inside, 5.2's "a useful human arrangement" caricature of Westminster); "visibility is Bellarmine's opinion".

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Marks of the Church

The visible signs by which a true church is recognized: for the Reformation confessions, the pure preaching of the Word and the right administration of the sacraments, to which the Belgic Confession adds discipline.

**Texts.** Augsburg VII: the congregation of saints "in which the Gospel is rightly taught and the Sacraments are rightly administered"; Calvin, *Institutes* IV.1.9: the Word preached and heard, the sacraments administered; Article XIX: "the pure Word of God is preached, and the Sacraments be duly ministered"; Belgic Confession art. 29: a third mark, "church discipline is exercised in punishing of sin" (Schaff's English prints "the truer Church" there, apparently a misprint, so quote around it). Westminster XXX.3 makes censures necessary "for vindicating the honor of Christ". Confessional standards in their traditions; no Catholic level. The creed's four marks, one, holy, catholic and apostolic, are [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-01-one.md) Module 2's.

**Uses.** 4.4: the marks make the Reformation Church visible and recognizable, which is why "they believe in an invisible Church" is a caricature; Augsburg names two marks, the Belgic three. 7.3: the Reformed form of the argument from scandal starts from discipline as a mark ([discipline as a mark](#discipline-as-a-mark)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Satis est

Augsburg VII's sufficiency clause: for the true unity of the Church, agreement concerning the doctrine of the Gospel and the administration of the sacraments "is enough".

**Text and standing.** The Church is "the congregation of saints, in which the Gospel is rightly taught and the Sacraments are rightly administered" (Triglot); Lutheran confessional standard, no Catholic level. The full text and its reading are [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-01-one.md)'s; Boss 4(c) close-reads it.

**In 4.4.** Cited, not argued: the question 4.4 presses is who settles a dispute in the visible Church, and the [fragmentation argument](#fragmentation-argument) asks what decides when two churches bearing the marks divide.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Ministerial authority

Real church authority exercised as a servant under Scripture: synods determine controversies "ministerially", and their decrees bind if consonant with the Word, yet may err.

**Texts.** Westminster XXXI.3 (XXXI.2 in the American numbering): decrees, if consonant to the Word, are "received with reverence and submission, not only for their agreement with the Word, but also for the power whereby they are made"; XXXI.4 (American XXXI.3): every post-apostolic synod "may err" (close-read in Boss 7(a)). Article XX: the Church has "authority in Controversies of Faith" under Scripture; XXI lets councils err (dropped by the American revision, 1801). Presbyterian subordinate standard; Anglican formularies; no Catholic level.

**In 4.4.** The Reformation answer to "who settles a dispute": an authority real, ministerial and openly fallible. The lesson grants the Protestant counter is real: a fallible authority acknowledged as fallible is a different thing from an infallible one. **The crux:** can an openly fallible authority do the settling the New Testament church needed done (Acts 15:6, 28)? The Reformed side: Acts 15 was apostolic and is now Scripture; later judgment is ministerial and revisable, with conscience answerable to God alone (Westminster XX.2). The Catholic side: the function did not end with the apostles; the "ancients" sat with them, and the Church is "the pillar and ground of the truth" (1 Timothy 3:15).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Synod of Dort

The Dutch Reformed national synod at Dordrecht (1618–19) that issued the Canons of Dort against the Remonstrants (the Dutch Arminians) and deposed their leaders.

**The Sentence on the Remonstrants (1619; 4.4's Source, the lesson's translation of the Latin in Schaff vol. 3, whose English omits it).** The synod acts "aware of its authority from the word of God" (*ex verbo Dei*) and "strengthened by the authority of the States-General"; it forbids the pastors it judged teachers of error every ecclesiastical office (*ab officiis suis abdicat*) and admits no one to the ministry who refuses to subscribe (*subscribere*) its doctrine and teach it. Three moves: its **ground** is authority from the word of God, not inerrancy; its **acts** are the New Testament church's (judgment, removal from office, a test for admission); its **enforcement** runs through the magistrate.

**Standing.** The Canons are a Dutch Reformed confessional standard (Schaff notes them still in force, with the Belgic Confession and the Heidelberg Catechism); the Sentence is a synod's judicial act; no Catholic level. **In 4.4:** evidence that authority reappeared (P4), and of the limit P5 names: the Remonstrant kept his reading and lost his pulpit.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Royal supremacy

The English Crown's claim to govern the Church of England in all causes, excluding papal jurisdiction, without exercising the ministry of word and sacrament.

**Text.** Article XXXVII (1571 English, spelling modernized) gives the queen "the chief government of all estates of this Realm, whether they be Ecclesiastical or Civil, in all causes"; denies princes "the ministering either of God's word, or of Sacraments"; defends the prerogative given "always to all godly Princes in holy Scriptures by God himself"; and ends "The bishop of Rome hath no jurisdiction in this Realm of England." Henry VIII took the title "supreme head" (1534), Elizabeth "supreme governor" (1559) ([`church-history` 6.3](../church-history/lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)). The American revision (1801) denies the civil power authority "in things purely spiritual". Anglican formularies, their standing varying across the Communion; no Catholic level.

**In 4.4 (Example 1).** The purest case of authority reappearing in a new place: Rome's jurisdiction passed by statute to a lay governor, defended as theology, a governing power, not a priestly one. When the Americans denied the magistrate that power, someone else had to govern. **Citation trap:** Schaff's 1563 Latin column labels Article XXXVII as "XXXVI. [XXXVII.]".

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Fragmentation argument

The Catholic argument that the Reformation principle supplies no judgment both sides must accept when two churches bearing the marks divide over what each treats as necessary.

**Stated properly (4.4 P6).** Structural, not statistical. Lutheran and Reformed divided over the Supper (Formula of Concord VII); no judgment exists that both must accept; such a division can be borne (Calvin, *Institutes* IV.1.12) or ended by agreement, never decided.

**The best replies (4.4).** Catholic unity rests on authority, not agreement, so it proves nothing about Scripture's clarity ([2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)). An arbiter whose own authority is disputed cannot settle disputes with those who reject it: Rome's claim prevented neither 1054 nor 1517. Churches can end divisions by agreement, as the Leuenberg Agreement (1973) did for European Lutheran and Reformed churches. And the mirror question: the [Western Schism](#western-schism).

**Trap:** arguing by a denomination count. The usual 33,000 (*World Christian Encyclopedia*, 2001) counts organized bodies country by country, splits the Catholic Church into 242, and is mostly independent churches.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Western Schism

1378–1417: two, then three, claimants to the papacy divided the Latin Church, until Constance ended the schism with Martin V's election (11 November 1417). The record is [`church-history` 5.4](../church-history/lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)'s.

**The mirror question (4.4 Example 2).** A Reformed critic's best counter to the visible-unity argument: for thirty-nine years Latin Christians could not agree who the pope was, and the schism ended through a council whose [*Haec Sancta*](#haec-sancta) claimed power immediately from Christ. The arbiter failed exactly where the argument needs one; 4.4 writes this into its Concession. **The Catholic reply has substance:** every obedience held one principle, one pope, and disputed a fact, whose election was valid; and it ended with one undoubted pope. **Its limit:** it shows the Church needs an identifiable final authority, not that identification is always possible, and a Protestant can call his own divisions disputes of application inside a shared principle. What survives: the schism ended because every party wanted the same office filled. [Indefectibility](#indefectibility) says nothing about such failures of governance.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

### Haec Sancta

Constance's decree (6 April 1415) that the council held its power immediately from Christ, and that all, the pope included, must obey it in matters of faith, the ending of the schism and reform.

**Level:** **disputed, no level**: a standing rule, an emergency measure, or not validly enacted; later acts closed off the strong reading (*Execrabilis*, 1460; Lateran V, 1516; *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 3), as [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-02-the-development-of-papal-claims-read-honestly.md) sets out. **In 4.4:** part of the Protestant counter to the visible-unity argument ([Western Schism](#western-schism)); `church-history` 5.4's exercises on it are not re-set.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)


**Against the Orthodox case (Module 5)**

### Monarchy of the Father

The Father as the one source (*arche*) within the Trinity: Son and Spirit come from him, and he from no one.

**Rung 1** in its Latin wording: *Firmiter* (Lateran IV, 1215, DS 800), the Father "from no one" (*a nullo*); *Cantate Domino* (Florence, 4 February 1442, DS 1331), the Father alone "principle without principle". The 1995 clarification's phrase "sole Trinitarian cause (*aitia*)": **no rung**. That text concedes the monarchy and says the filioque must not be presented so that it appears to contradict it (5.1's **Concession**). Photius's charge (*Mystagogy* ch. 11): two causes destroy the monarchy and bring back polytheism. The Latin reply (5.1 P3): the Son has from the Father that the Spirit proceeds from him (DS 1301), so the Son's share is the Father's gift and the monarchy stands.

**Tension to name.** Florence DS 1301 makes the Son, "according to the Greeks", *causa* of the Spirit's subsistence, as the Father is. Harmonized (5.1, Example 2): Florence's *causa* names the Son within the one principle, holding it from the Father; 1995's sole *aitia* names the Father as principle without principle; both exclude a second, independent cause. **Traps:** "the Son is a cause in no sense" contradicts DS 1301; "Florence defined what the Greek Fathers meant by 'through the Son'" turns a reading into a definition. The doctrine itself is [`trinity-and-god` 5.1](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-01-the-latin-doctrine.md)–[5.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-03-the-clarifications.md); the Cappadocian monarchy is [`patristics` 3.3](../patristics/lessons/03-03-the-two-gregories.md).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Ekporeusis and processio

Greek *ekporeusis* names the Spirit's taking origin from the Father as sole cause; Latin *processio* (*procedere*) is the wider word for any coming-forth.

Taught in [`trinity-and-god` 5.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-02-the-greek-doctrine-and-the-addition.md)–[5.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-03-the-clarifications.md), not re-taught. 5.1 uses it only in its concession: the [1995 clarification](#clarification-1995) makes the Father the sole origin of the Spirit's *ekporeusis*, and Rome refuses *kai tou Huiou* in the Greek creed, which remains legitimate without it. **Trap:** reading the Latin "proceeds from the Father and the Son" back into Greek with the narrow verb yields the two-cause sentence the Greeks refuse and Rome never taught.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Lawfulness of the addition

Whether the West could rightly insert the filioque into the common creed of 381: a question independent of whether the clause is true.

**The act.** Florence, *Laetentur caeli* (6 July 1439), DS 1302: *Diffinimus insuper, explicationem verborum illorum 'Filioque' veritatis declarandae gratia, et imminente tunc necessitate, licite ac rationabiliter Symbolo fuisse appositam*: the explanation was "lawfully and reasonably added to the Creed, for the sake of declaring the truth and because of a necessity pressing at that time" (lesson's translation). Read it: *diffinimus* is the verb DS 1300 used for the doctrine, so this is a definition; *explicationem* calls the clause an explanation, not a new article (Florence's answer to "a different faith"); *tunc* ties the necessity to the time of insertion. It is silent on who inserted the clause, whether a council was needed, and whether Greeks must recite it.

**Level: rung 2**, a defined judgment on an ecclesiastical act connected with the faith. Some theologians class it as disciplinary; the floor is still **at least rung 3**, so it is not freely disputed. Consequence: a Catholic may concede the manner, and that the necessity was *tunc* (nothing binds Greeks to recite the clause now; Rome refuses it in the Greek creed), but may not call the addition unlawful. Reciting the creed without the clause is discipline, **no rung**.

**The Orthodox case, in outline** (Boss 5(a) asks for it in full; 5.1, **The other side**): the [Ephesian decree](#ephesus-canon-7); no council of the whole Church added the clause; Rome's own long refusal (Leo III, 810; the silver shields the *Liber Pontificalis* reports; the [council of 879-880](#council-of-879-880)); the 1895 encyclical's "utterly lawless addition". Why the Orthodox make it a question of authority, and the crux, are in 5.1 (**The other side**; **The crux.**).

**Traps:** citing DS 1302 to the Orthodox as though it settled anything begs the question, since they do not [receive](#reception) Florence; "Florence was a failed reunion council, so its definitions lapsed" is false on the Catholic account; calling the lawfulness "freely disputed" understates, and putting DS 1302 at rung 1 because it says "we define" overstates (its object is not a revealed truth).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Mystagogy of the Holy Spirit

Photius's treatise (about 886) against the procession from the Son: the classic Orthodox case on the doctrine.

Photius was patriarch of Constantinople (858-867, 877-886) and is an Orthodox saint. As 5.1 paraphrases him: from John 15:26 (the Spirit proceeds from the Father); what two persons share in God is common to all three, so a Spirit from Father and Son proceeds from himself (ch. 6); by parity Father and Spirit would beget the Son, every property becomes common and the persons collapse into one, a revived Sabellianism (ch. 9); to cause is the Father's own property, so a Son who shares it usurps the Father's place or the Father was incomplete; two causes destroy the [monarchy](#monarchy-of-the-father) and bring back polytheism (ch. 11). Chapters in the standard 96-chapter division.

**Standing:** a saint's treatise, not a conciliar act. **The Catholic reply** (5.1, Example 1): the premise fails, because active spiration belongs to two persons, neither to the nature nor to one (a relation that is not a property, ST I q.30 a.2 ad 1); paternity and being from no one stay the Father's, and the Son holds spiration from him (DS 1301). [Lyons II](#lyons-ii) already condemns two principles, so the definition is not the doctrine Photius attacked. *Why* spiration can be shared is school theology, **rung 4**.

**Traps:** the Sabellianism of ch. 9 comes from the parity argument, not from the shared-causal-property argument; do not give Photius the eternal manifestation through the Son that Gregory of Cyprus and Palamas allow (Blachernae, 1285), since he denies the Son any role in the Spirit's eternal being; "Photius is a saint, so the *Mystagogy* is Orthodox dogma" overstates, and refuting it refutes no church.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Ephesus canon 7

The decree of Ephesus (431), carried as canon 7 in Percival, forbidding anyone to compose "a different (*heteran*) Faith as a rival" to Nicaea's.

Issued after Charisius's case and the reading of a Nestorian creed; Percival's Roman editors note that the Vatican manuscripts have six canons only. **Orthodox reading:** it fences the common creed against any unilateral change. **Catholic reading** (5.1 P6): it forbids a *different* faith, not an explanation; the creed of 381 itself enlarged Nicaea's; and Cyril, who presided, wrote that one who makes plain his faith "innovates in nothing" (to Acacius of Melitene, in Percival's excursus).

**Level:** conciliar law, **no doctrinal rung**. As the act of an ecumenical council both churches receive, it binds both: "an Eastern canon that never bound the West" understates, and the quarrel is over what *heteran* covers. Name it as "the decree carried as canon 7 (Percival)". Its close reading is [`trinity-and-god` 5.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-02-the-greek-doctrine-and-the-addition.md); its occasion [`christology` 2.2](../christology/lessons/02-02-nestorius-cyril-and-ephesus.md).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Council of 879-880

The council at Constantinople that restored Photius and, with Roman legates present, reaffirmed the creed of 381 without the filioque.

As the 2003 North American Orthodox-Catholic statement reports it, the council also anathematized anyone composing another confession. **Standing:** some Orthodox count it ecumenical, others do not. **Use:** a plank of the Orthodox lawfulness case, and part of the Catholic concession on the manner of the addition: Rome's own legates reaffirmed the unaltered creed.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Lyons II

The Second Council of Lyons (1274): two acts under one name, and only one of them defines.

| Act | What it says | Level |
|---|---|---|
| The constitution *Fideli ac devota* (DS 850) | the Spirit proceeds eternally from Father and Son "as from one principle ... by a single spiration"; condemns those who deny it and those who say the Spirit proceeds from two principles | **rung 1** |
| Michael VIII Palaeologus's profession of faith (proposed by Clement IV in 1267, read at the council; DS 851-861) | on purgatory (DS 856), *poenis purgatoriis seu catharteriis*, "purgatorial or cleansing (*catharteriis*) pains, as Brother John explained to us": a Greek word glossing the Latin through an interpreter | **defines nothing of itself** |

**Uses.** 5.1: the West condemns the two causes Photius attacks; Florence's procession decree carries no anathema of its own, the condemnation is Lyons II's; the [1995 clarification](#clarification-1995) reads Lyons II through Lateran IV to make the one principle Father and Son, not the essence. 5.5: Florence kept the profession's *poenis purgatoriis* and dropped the gloss *seu catharteriis* (see [Purgatory](#purgatory)). **Traps:** citing Michael VIII's profession as the act that defines the procession or purgatory; "Lyons II defined purgatory".

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Letter to Marinus

Maximus the Confessor's seventh-century letter, written from Rome, reporting that the Romans do not make the Son the Spirit's cause.

As the [1995 clarification](#clarification-1995) cites it (PG 91:136A-B), paraphrased: the Romans do not make the Son the *aitia* of the Spirit, knowing the Father to be sole cause of Son and Spirit, but say the Spirit comes forth (*proienai*) through the Son. 5.1 P5: a Greek Father found the Latin sense orthodox. No public-domain English is used and the year is not fixed. Maximus as an author is [`patristics` 5.4](../patristics/lessons/05-04-maximus-and-john-of-damascus.md).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Clarification 1995

The Pontifical Council for Promoting Christian Unity's *The Greek and the Latin Traditions regarding the Procession of the Holy Spirit*, published unsigned in *L'Osservatore Romano* (13 September 1995) at John Paul II's request.

Three moves 5.1 uses: it concedes the [monarchy](#monarchy-of-the-father) (the Father sole Trinitarian cause or principle of Son and Spirit and sole origin of the Spirit's [*ekporeusis*](#ekporeusis-and-processio); the filioque must not appear to contradict that); reading [Lyons II](#lyons-ii) through Lateran IV, it says the divine essence cannot be the "one principle", which answers Lossky's nature-before-person charge; and it cites Maximus's [*Letter to Marinus*](#letter-to-marinus). It also records that Rome admitted the clause into its liturgical creed only in 1014 and that Leo III resisted it. It never cites Florence or DS 1301, so the *causa* tension is left to the reader ([Monarchy of the Father](#monarchy-of-the-father)).

**Level:** defines nothing; **no rung (rung 3 at most)**. It cannot lower a definition: "since 1995 Catholics may take the procession from the Son or leave it" understates a **rung 1** doctrine (5.3 Flashback). Read closely in [`trinity-and-god` 5.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/05-03-the-clarifications.md).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Eucharistic ecclesiology

Each local church gathered at one altar around its bishop is the whole Church in that place, and the churches are one by being the same Church, not by adding up parts.

**As its defenders state it.** Nicholas Afanasiev ("The Church Which Presides in Love", in *The Primacy of Peter*, English 1963): each eucharistic assembly is the whole Church, no church has power over another, and Rome's first place was a priority of witness, not of jurisdiction; he traced the rival *universal ecclesiology* chiefly to Cyprian. John Zizioulas (*Eucharist, Bishop, Church*; *Being as Communion*) corrects him: the one Eucharist under the one bishop makes a church, the churches are one by identity, which councils show, and the extreme local axiom would destroy canonical unity. The consequence they draw: a power held by one church over the others is a category error, a church with power over the Church; applied to 1870 (5.3), a power entering a local church from outside by divine right makes it a branch. **Standing:** theologians' books, not a magisterium's teaching.

**Catholic texts that accept the premise:** LG 26 (the Church of Christ truly present in every legitimate local congregation) and LG 23 (the one Church in and from the particular churches), **at least rung 3**; UR 15 (each Eastern church's Eucharist builds up the Church of God), **rung 3**. LG 27: bishops are not vicars of the Roman pontiffs. So "a diocese is a branch of the universal Church, run from Rome" is not Catholic doctrine either. 5.2 argues primacy inside these categories (P1-P5: premise accepted; communion tests which altars are the Church; a first at every level; a first who can act for communion between councils; a ministry interior to each church). Stated in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-02-the-local-and-the-universal-church.md).

**Traps:** arguing for primacy from "a universal Church needs a universal head" argues from the premise this ecclesiology denies; "Orthodoxy is a federation of sovereign national churches" is a Western juridical category (Alexandria §5.1 rejects both pyramid and federation).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Taxis

The canonical order of rank among sees (and, by analogy, among the divine persons): an order with a first that implies no inequality of nature.

Chieti (2016) §15: between the fourth and seventh centuries the *taxis* of the five patriarchal sees came to be recognized, Rome first, exercising a primacy of honour (*presbeia tes times*). Ravenna §5 grounds the *taxis* of local churches in the order of the divine persons, without subordination. Ravenna §41: both sides agree that Rome, the church that "presides in love" (Ignatius's phrase), was first in the *taxis* and its bishop *protos* among the patriarchs; they disagree on his prerogatives, understood differently already in the first millennium. The echo (5.2, Sideways): Zizioulas's communion ecclesiology rests on the [monarchy of the Father](#monarchy-of-the-father) ([`trinity-and-god` 6.4](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-04-social-trinitarianism-and-its-critics.md)), so the dispute over a universal first echoes the dispute over the Father as sole cause. **Level:** the commission texts have **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Pentarchy

The five patriarchal sees in fixed order, Rome, Constantinople, Alexandria, Antioch, Jerusalem, governing the Church in concert.

The order stood by the seventh century (Chieti §15). Constantinople I (381), canon 3: Constantinople has "the prerogative of honour after the Bishop of Rome; because Constantinople is New Rome" (Percival). [Canon 28 of Chalcedon](#canon-28-of-chalcedon) (451) gives New Rome equal privileges and the second rank, grounding both sees' privileges in their being imperial cities. The canons are **law, no doctrinal rung**. **Trap:** "five equal patriarchs" with no first, or "the Orthodox never gave the pope any primacy", is false to the Orthodox sources themselves (Ravenna §41; the [1895 encyclical](#patriarchal-encyclical-of-1895); the Moscow Synod, 2013).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Chieti and Alexandria

The Catholic-Orthodox Joint International Commission's documents on synodality and primacy: Chieti on the first millennium, Alexandria on the second millennium and today.

| Text | What the lessons use |
|---|---|
| *Synodality and Primacy during the First Millennium* (Chieti, 21 September 2016) | §15 the *taxis* of five sees, Rome first with a primacy of honour; §16 the Petrine reading of Rome's primacy not adopted in the East; §18 Rome not personally present at any ecumenical council; §19 appeals to Rome, from the East too (Sardica, 343), and to Constantinople, always handled synodically; Rome did not *exercise* canonical authority over the churches of the East |
| *Synodality and Primacy in the Second Millennium and Today* (Alexandria, 7 June 2023) | §3.7 *ex sese* read as excluding juridical ratification; §3.10 the post-1870 Orthodox objections (Vatican I obscures the catholicity of each local church; infallibility belongs to the Church as a whole, expressed by councils the people of God receives), admitted to have been often used polemically; §5.1 the Church neither a pyramid nor a federation; for Catholics synodality is not merely consultative, for Orthodox primacy not merely honorific; §5.2 distinguish the pope's patriarchal ministry in the Latin Church from his primatial service |

Alexandria returns in [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md) as the record of the Orthodox objection. **Level:** **no magisterial level**; neither binds any Orthodox church. **Traps:** "Rome had no canonical authority over the East" garbles Chieti §19, which says it did not *exercise* it, and the sentence is Chieti's, not Ravenna's; Alexandria §3.10 records what Vatican I's ecclesiology is *for the Orthodox* and concedes nothing for Rome; do not copy Alexandria §3.11, which calls *Praeclara gratulationis* an encyclical of 1895 (it is an apostolic letter of 20 June 1894).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Apostolic Canon 34

A fourth-century Antiochene canon received East and West: a region's bishops do nothing weighty without their first, and he does nothing without the consent of all.

The pattern of [conciliarity](#conciliarity) at the regional level: Ravenna §24 quotes it, and §10 extends the pattern to three levels with a *protos* at each. Numbered 35 in Percival's alternative count. **Level:** canon law received by both churches; for Catholics **law, no doctrinal rung**. How Vatican II keeps its first clause at the universal level without making the second a juridical condition of the pope's acts is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-05-vatican-ii-primacy-collegiality-synodality.md).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Romanides thesis

John Romanides's claim that the schism was not East against West but Franks against Romans: Charlemagne's schism imported into Old Rome.

**As he states it** (*Franks, Romans, Feudalism, and Doctrine*, Holy Cross Orthodox Press, 1981; some bibliographies give 1982): there was one Orthodox Roman nation, East and West, and "Greek" was the Franks' label for its eastern half; the Franks, Germanic conquerors of the West Romans, built a feudal Church, pressed the filioque against Leo III (Aachen, 809) and branded the East Romans heretics; German emperors then installed non-Roman popes, from 983 (Otto II's appointment of Peter of Pavia) and again after Sutri (1046). So the schism, on his account, was not between East and West Romans. **Standing:** a minority thesis among Orthodox historians, not "the Orthodox position".

**Tested** (5.2, problem P2): on his own dating the popes before 983 were Roman, yet the primacy dispute is older (Leo I refused Chalcedon's canon 28 in 452; Nicholas I contested Photius from 863), so the dividing papal claim is no Frankish import, and if the first-millennium Roman see was Orthodox its claims belong to the inheritance Romanides defends. **True part to concede:** the filioque was a Frankish project Rome resisted until 1014, and Leo IX, whose legates excommunicated Cerularius in 1054, was a German appointee of Henry III ([`church-history` 4.1](../church-history/lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)). **Traps:** "Romanides says the Latins are heretics" or "blames Western racism" is caricature; dismissing the thesis as nationalism without testing it on the record.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Ravenna document

The Catholic-Orthodox Joint International Commission's text of 13 October 2007, *Ecclesiological and Canonical Consequences of the Sacramental Nature of the Church: Ecclesial Communion, Conciliarity and Authority*.

Its preface says it is not to be read as an official declaration of the Church's teaching. What the lessons use: §5 *taxis* grounded in the divine persons; §10 three levels (local, regional, universal) with a *protos* at each; §24 [Apostolic Canon 34](#apostolic-canon-34); §39 since the break no ecumenical council in the strict sense is possible; §41 Rome first in the *taxis*, disagreement on its prerogatives; §42 Rome did not convene the early ecumenical councils and never personally presided over them, though closely involved; §43 the fact of universal primacy accepted by both, its manner of exercise and its scriptural and theological foundations not; §44 prerogatives at each level at least until the ninth century; §45 the bishop of Rome's role, and the reading of Vatican I and II in the light of the first millennium, left for deeper study.

**Level:** **no magisterial level**; binds no Orthodox church. **Traps:** "Ravenna settled primacy" (§45 postponed it); "the Vatican signed that Rome had no canonical authority over the East" (that sentence is Chieti §19, and it says *did not exercise*). The text is silent on the Moscow delegation's walkout, which [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-05-vatican-ii-primacy-collegiality-synodality.md) reports.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Bishop of Rome 2024

The Dicastery for Promoting Christian Unity's study document *The Bishop of Rome: Primacy and Synodality in the Ecumenical Dialogues and in the Responses to the Encyclical Ut unum sint*, its publication approved at an audience of 2 March 2024.

What the lessons use. 5.2: §91 quotes Joseph Ratzinger (*Theologische Prinzipienlehre*, Munich 1982, p. 209) that on primacy Rome need not require of the East more than the first millennium formulated and lived; §97 the Orthodox never contested Rome's primacy of honour; §98 dialogues saying first-millennium honour carried real authority. 5.3: §§57-59 and 67-68, the dialogues' readings of Vatican I with Orthodox and Anglican objections to universal jurisdiction, "ordinary" and "immediate" glossed after Zinelli (§67); §179 (proposal 15), distinguish the pope's patriarchal role in the Latin Church from his ministry of unity.

**Level:** by its own words a study document that does not summarize the magisterium: **below the seam** (5.2), **no magisterial level** (5.3). Ratzinger's sentence is a theologian's, **no magisterial level**, and quoting it here does not raise it. Cite it as "2024": the release date is not in the document.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Ut Unum Sint

John Paul II's encyclical on ecumenism (25 May 1995), with its invitation to help find a new way of exercising the primacy.

§95 asks for a way of exercising the primacy that renounces nothing essential to its mission yet is open to a new situation; it recalls UR 14 (for a millennium the Roman See acted, by common consent, as moderator) and grants that what should have been a service sometimes showed itself in a very different light, which is part of 5.2's **Concession**. §96 invites patient and fraternal dialogue; §88 asks forgiveness for painful memories; §97 speaks of a ministry presiding in truth and love. **Level: rung 3** for its doctrine; the invitation is a request, not a teaching. **Use** (5.3 P5): the power is fixed; its form is not. **Trap:** UR 14's "by common consent" and *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 4's "not from the consent of the Church" do not contradict: one describes how a role was exercised, the other a definition's force ([Ex sese non ex consensu](#ex-sese-non-ex-consensu)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Patriarchal encyclical of 1895

The Patriarchal and Synodical Encyclical of Anthimos VII of Constantinople and his synod (dated August 1895), answering Leo XIII's apostolic letter *Praeclara gratulationis* (20 June 1894); public-domain English by Eustathius Metallinos (Manchester, 1896), published with the patriarch's blessing.

**Standing inside Orthodoxy:** the Ecumenical Patriarchate's official synodal answer, weighty and widely cited, polemical; not an ecumenical council's act and not a pan-Orthodox definition, since Orthodoxy has no magisterium to make it binding on all. **No Catholic level.** It is neither "the Orthodox position" nor "one patriarch's private letter". Not the 1848 *Encyclical of the Eastern Patriarchs* (Anthimos VI with three other patriarchs).

| Section | What it says | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| §XII | the Church prays "for the forgiveness and rest" of the departed; rejects as innovations "purgatorial fire, a superabundance of the virtues of the saints, and the distribution of them", and a full reward before the universal resurrection and judgment | [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md) |
| §XIII (with §VI) | the Word's incarnation "alone pure and immaculate"; 1854 laid down "a novel dogma", unknown to the ancient Church. Its grounds are novelty and Christ's unique purity | [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md) |
| §§XIV-XV | no bishop of Rome was ever supreme head; every bishop heads his own church, "subject only to the synodical ordinances and decisions of the Church universal"; Rome's "honorary prerogative of presidency", first among equals, rested on the capital (citing Chalcedon's canon 28) | [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md) |
| §XVI | each church was "totally independent and self-administered"; the bishop of Rome had "no right to interfere"; the ecumenical council "alone was and is the supreme tribunal" | [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md) |
| §XVIII | apart from Christ, the Church "knows no one that was infallible upon earth"; cites Liberius, Zosimus, Vigilius, Honorius | [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md) |
| §XIX | the Orthodox churches, "the pillar and ground of the truth", "remain, and will always remain, faithful" to the ancient constitution | [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md) |
| §XX | the old, divinely transmitted doctrines received, councils adding nothing | [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md) |
| on the filioque | an "utterly lawless addition" | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md) |

**Traps:** quoting it alone as "the Orthodox position", or dismissing it as one man's opinion when his synod issued it; "jurisdiction, not infallibility" is the modern theologians' emphasis, not every Orthodox text's (§XVIII attacks infallibility at length); "the Orthodox Church condemned the Immaculate Conception in 1895" overstates; its history of Leo III, who it says denounced the addition synodically in 809, overstates (he approved the doctrine and refused the insertion, 810).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Infallibility

The Church's immunity from error when she defines doctrine of faith or morals, and the pope's, under stated conditions, when he defines *ex cathedra*.

**Rung 1** (*[Pastor Aeternus](#pastor-aeternus)* ch. 4, 18 July 1870): the pope "when he speaks ex cathedra", defining "a doctrine regarding faith or morals to be held by the universal Church" (Schaff); councils with their head and the ordinary universal magisterium share it. Can. 749 §3: nothing counts as defined unless manifestly so. It attaches to a kind of act, not to a person or to every Roman act; it is assistance in guarding the deposit, not new revelation; it covers definitions, not governance, and [indefectibility](#indefectibility) (**at least rung 3**) promises nothing about conduct (7.1, 7.3). Its conditions and Honorius are [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md); *ex sese* has [its own entry](#ex-sese-non-ex-consensu).

**Two opposite objections** (5.3), which one reply cannot answer. **Orthodox:** the Church is infallible, expressed by councils the people of God receives (Alexandria §3.10); the [1895 encyclical](#patriarchal-encyclical-of-1895) §XVIII knows no one infallible on earth but Christ and cites Liberius, Zosimus, Vigilius and Honorius, while §XIX keeps the guarantee for the Orthodox churches: relocated, not abolished (7.1, 7.3). **Anglican and Reformed:** Article XXI of the [Thirty-Nine Articles](#thirty-nine-articles) (general councils may err, and have erred, even in things pertaining to God) and [Westminster](#westminster-confession) XXXI.4 (XXXI.3 in the American numbering: councils may err, a help and not the rule of faith; I.10 makes the Spirit speaking in Scripture the supreme judge). For them 1870 is a fallible body claiming Scripture's place; for the Orthodox a received council cannot err. Boss 7(a) close-reads Article XXI and Westminster XXXI.4: cite them, do not restate them here.

**Traps:** "Vatican I made the pope a fourth person of the Trinity, like God" and "the Orthodox say the Church can err" are both caricatures; answering the Orthodox only on infallibility misses ch. 3, where their strongest voices aim, but do not overcorrect (the 1895 encyclical and Alexandria §3.10 object to infallibility too); "Vatican I defined that popes do not err" overstates. Against the [retreat objection](#retreat-objection), name in advance what would refute the claim: a definition meeting ch. 4's conditions, shown false (7.1).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### German bishops 1875

The German episcopate's collective declaration (January-February 1875) answering Bismarck's circular of 1872: an early reading of *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 3 that Pius IX expressly approved.

DS 3112-3116, a Latin version of a German original (lesson's translations). It quotes the circular's claim that the bishops were now "nothing but his instruments, officials without any responsibility of their own" (DS 3112), the vicar reading, and refuses it: the episcopate rests on the same divine institution as the papacy, with rights the pope has "neither the right nor the power to change" (DS 3115); the pope is "not bishop of Cologne or of Breslau" (DS 3113); yet papal power is in force "not only in certain exceptional cases but holds and binds at every time and in every place" (DS 3113). Pius IX approved it in *Mirabilis illa constantia* (apostolic letter to the German bishops, March 1875, DS 3117), rejecting the press's claim that the bishops had approved a "softened" (*emollitam*) doctrine, and in a consistorial allocution of 15 March 1875. Give *Mirabilis* no day: 4 and 12 March both circulate.

**Level: rung 3** by papal approval; it can neither narrow nor widen the definition. **Traps:** "the earliest authoritative reading" (Fessler's work of 1871 already had papal approbation); "1875 defined that bishops are not papal delegates" overstates; "1875 made ch. 3 a primacy of honour" inverts it ([Minimalist temptation](#minimalist-temptation)); and no earthly tribunal enforces the 1875 protection for a single bishop (5.3's **Concession**). Read with the constitution in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-03-vatican-i-and-pastor-aeternus.md).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Ex sese non ex consensu

*Pastor Aeternus* ch. 4: *ex cathedra* definitions are irreformable "of themselves, and not from the consent of the Church" (Schaff).

**Level: rung 1**, part of the definition. **The Orthodox objection** (Alexandria §3.10): infallibility belongs to the Church as a whole, so a definition irreformable without the Church's consent looks like one man's faith set over the Church's. **The Catholic answer** (5.3 P4): the phrase excludes juridical ratification (Gallican Article 4, 1682), not the Church's faith, and the joint Alexandria text (§3.7) reads it the same way. Newman's postscript to the *Letter to the Duke of Norfolk* (1875) does not count reception among "the necessary conditions of a de fide decision"; "ratified" meant "brought home to us as authentic". **Trap:** UR 14's "by common consent" (quoted in UUS 95) and ch. 4's "not from the consent" do not contradict: one describes how a role was exercised, the other a definition's force; yet the first-millennium model ran on consent and the defined primacy does not (5.3, Example 2). The phrase as a crux is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-03-vatican-i-and-pastor-aeternus.md)'s, cited and not re-argued.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Minimalist temptation

Defending a definition by quietly emptying it: for 1870, calling ordinary and immediate jurisdiction a courtesy title or a primacy of honour.

5.3 names and refuses it. Ch. 3's canon (**rung 1**) condemns "the office merely of inspection or direction", and ch. 1's canon a primacy "of honor only" (Schaff). The 1875 bishops affirmed papal power "at every time and in every place" (DS 3113), and Pius IX rejected the claim that they had softened the doctrine (DS 3117). Newman's "wise and gentle minimism" (*Letter to the Duke of Norfolk* §9) limits what one imposes on others as *de fide*; it does not empty a definition. The ladder is not a device for retreat: it fixes ch. 3 at rung 1. **What the Catholic owes instead:** defend a real power to act in any church, and argue that its exercise can take a first-millennium form (5.3's conclusion). **Trap the other way:** making the pope bishop of every diocese, which DS 3113 denies. Kin to the [retreat objection](#retreat-objection) of 7.1.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Ecumenical council

A council of the whole Church whose decrees bind all. 5.3 sets the Catholic and Orthodox accounts of what makes one ecumenical side by side; Boss 5(b) grades that comparison, so this entry gives its parts only.

**Catholic account, with levels.** LG 22: no council is ecumenical unless Peter's successor confirms or at least accepts it as such, and he convokes, presides and confirms: **rung 3 at its heaviest**. Code, cann. 338 §1 (the pope alone convokes, presides, transfers, suspends, dissolves, approves) and 341 §1 (decrees bind only when approved by the pope with the fathers, confirmed by him and promulgated at his order): **law, no rung**. Vatican I's legitimacy as an ecumenical council: **rung 2**, under the CDF 1998 commentary's class of the legitimacy of a council's celebration (§11; no act names Vatican I). [Reception](#reception) recognizes; it does not constitute ([`fundamental-theology` 5.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-03-the-sensus-fidei.md)).

**Orthodox account, its parts** (5.3, **The other side**). Florovsky ("The Authority of the Ancient Councils and the Tradition of the Fathers", 1967; *Bible, Church, Tradition*, 1972, ch. 6): no formal mark (convocation, attendance, confirmation); authority is the truth witnessed, recognized afterward. Precedents: Ephesus 449 (the Robber Synod), Hieria 754 (denied the name by Nicaea II), Florence (signed, not received). Root text: the 1848 *Encyclical of the Eastern Patriarchs* §17, the whole people as guardian of the faith (paraphrase only). Not a plebiscite and not a juridical ratification. For 1870: the Orthodox declined the 1868 invitation, the minority left rather than vote ([`church-history` 8.2](../church-history/lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)), and the East has not received the decrees. Where the two accounts divide is the Boss's question; 5.3's argument (P4) and its P2 problem are the materials.

**Traps:** treating reception as a vote, on either side; "our councils are infallible the moment they meet" is denied by the Orthodox tradition itself (Ephesus 449); the Anglican and Reformed claim that councils may err is a different objection ([Infallibility](#infallibility)); since the break no council in the strict sense is possible (Ravenna §39).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Essence and energies

Palamas's distinction between God's essence, beyond every creature's knowing and sharing, and his uncreated energies, in which the saints really share God himself.

**As its defenders state it**, the parts 5.4 teaches (Boss 5(c) asks for the statement in full; 5.4, **The other side**): the essence beyond participation, now and in the age to come; the energies (glory, life, grace, the light of Tabor) uncreated, God himself; not parts, accidents or a second God, God whole in each; a distinction not made by our minds that divides nothing. Texts: Palamas's *Triads* and *One Hundred and Fifty Chapters*; the 1351 Tome. Basil's Letter 234 §1 (NPNF2 8), "His operations come down to us, but His essence remains beyond our reach", is claimed by both sides: Basil asserts a distinction there but not what kind.

**Weight in Orthodoxy:** taught by the Constantinopolitan councils of 1341-1351 ([Hesychast controversy](#hesychast-controversy)), local councils whose teaching binds across Orthodoxy by [reception](#reception): the Tome, the *Synodikon of Orthodoxy*, the liturgy, the 1368 canonization. Modern statements, each a theologian's book: Lossky (*The Mystical Theology of the Eastern Church*, 1944, English 1957, ch. 4 "Uncreated Energies"), the ground of real union and a key to the East-West divide; Meyendorff (*A Study of Gregory Palamas*, 1959, English 1964), Palamas the heir of the Cappadocians and Maximus; Bradshaw (*Aristotle East and West*, 2004), the Latin objection presupposes a metaphysics the East never shared.

**Catholic level:** the distinction has **no magisterial level** and has never been condemned: Denzinger, through 1957, never names Palamas, the hesychasts or the energies, and no later act does. Whether it is compatible with [divine simplicity](#divine-simplicity): **rung 5**. The Latin objection is under [Divine simplicity](#divine-simplicity), the Orthodox counter-charge under [Created and uncreated grace](#created-and-uncreated-grace) and [Deification](#deification), the crux under [Real distinction](#real-distinction).

**Traps:** making the energies a second God, a go-between or a part of God (his defenders deny all three; "lower divinity" is his opponents' best text, not his thesis); "Rome condemned Palamism"; "Rome approves the distinction" because some Byzantine-rite Catholic churches keep his feast ([Gregory Palamas](#gregory-palamas)).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Deification

The saints' real share in God's life, "partakers of the divine nature" (2 Peter 1:4, Douay-Rheims), by grace and never by nature.

The datum both sides protect: both churches confess a real share in God's life (5.4 P1 and **Concession**). The Greek *theias koinonoi physeos*, "sharers of divine nature" (lesson's rendering). **Catholic levels:** real participation, **rung 1**; never one substance with God, **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. 3 on God the Creator); the indwelling of the Persons (John 14:23), **rung 1**. CCC 460 sets 2 Peter 1:4 beside Irenaeus (*Against Heresies* 3.19.1), Athanasius (*On the Incarnation* 54.3) and Aquinas (Opusc. 57): it locates and adds no level. **The Orthodox counter-charge** (theologians after Lossky): Latin simplicity plus an unshareable essence leaves the saint only created grace, so "deification" becomes a figure for likeness. **The Catholic answer** (5.4 P2-P3): the term of the share is uncreated, God himself ([Created and uncreated grace](#created-and-uncreated-grace)); it answers the metaphor charge, not the simplicity question. The doctrine is owned by [`creation-grace-last-things` 4.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md); Maximus on deification by grace is [`patristics` 5.4](../patristics/lessons/05-04-maximus-and-john-of-damascus.md).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Divine simplicity

God's essence is "altogether simple" (*simplex omnino*): no composition, nothing in God that is not God.

**Rung 1:** *Firmiter* (Lateran IV, 1215, DS 800), repeated in *Dei Filius* ch. 1 (1870). With it, Florence's rule in *Cantate Domino* (4 February 1442, DS 1330), *omniaque sunt unum, ubi non obviat relationis oppositio*: in God all things are one where no opposition of relation intervenes, **rung 1**. `trinity-and-god` 1.3's three denials (not parts, not really distinct, not accidents) are its reading of what *omnino* excludes, and the Thomist and Scotist accounts of it are schools. Taught in [`trinity-and-god` 1.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-03-simplicity-as-doctrine.md); derived in [`thomistic-synthesis` 3.4](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/03-04-divine-simplicity-and-the-attributes.md); its philosophical objections are [`philosophy-of-religion` 1.4](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/01-04-eternity-immutability-and-simplicity.md).

**The Latin objection to Palamas**, in outline (Boss 5(c) asks for it stated): the energy is either the essence or a creature, since essence and energies are not opposed by relation (5.4, **The argument**). **Its application to Palamas: rung 5.** No act applies these definitions to him, and can. 749 §3 forbids inferring a condemnation.

**Traps:** "simplicity is a Thomist thesis a Catholic may doubt, rung 4" understates (6.1 Flashback): *simplex omnino* is rung 1, and only the Thomist account of what it excludes belongs to a school; "Lateran IV condemned Palamism" is anachronism, *Firmiter* predating Palamas's birth by about eighty years; "simplicity is de fide, so the distinction is formally heretical" gives an application the definition's rung.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Hesychast controversy

The fourteenth-century Byzantine dispute over the Athonite monks' prayer of stillness and their claim to see the uncreated light of the Transfiguration.

| When | What |
|---|---|
| 1337 | Barlaam, a Calabrian Greek monk, crosses Palamas over whether anything about God can be demonstrated, then attacks the monks: God is known through creatures and by negation, and any light seen is created |
| 10 June 1341 | a council at Constantinople condemns Barlaam |
| August 1341 | a second condemns Gregory Akindynos |
| 1344 | reversal: Palamas excommunicated under Patriarch John XIV Kalekas |
| February 1347 | Kalekas deposed, Palamas vindicated; Palamas archbishop of Thessalonica |
| 1351 | a council (opened 28 May) vindicates Palamas and issues its Tome that August; its anathemas enter the *Synodikon of Orthodoxy* |
| 1368 | a synod under Patriarch Philotheos Kokkinos canonizes Palamas |

The quarrel began inside Byzantium (Meyendorff), over whether the saints' experience of God is knowledge of him, not as an East-West dispute. **Orthodox weight:** local councils whose teaching binds by [reception](#reception). **Catholic level:** none. **Trap:** the 1351 Tome is neither Orthodoxy's *ex cathedra* definition (Orthodoxy has no magisterium whose definitions bind of themselves) nor one synod's opinion an Orthodox may set aside (it was received into the *Synodikon*, and Palamas was canonized): a local council's act that binds by reception (5.4; 6.1 Flashback).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Gregory Palamas

Athonite monk (c. 1296-1359), archbishop of Thessalonica from 1347, defender of the hesychasts and of the essence-energies distinction.

Works: *Triads in Defence of the Holy Hesychasts*; *One Hundred and Fifty Chapters* (cite by work). An Orthodox saint, canonized in 1368, kept on the Second Sunday of Lent. His language of a "lower divinity" (the energies, the essence being higher), which opponents heard as polytheism, is their best text, not his thesis. In 5.1 he appears with Gregory of Cyprus allowing an eternal manifestation of the Spirit through the Son, which Photius denied.

**Catholic side:** never condemned. Several Byzantine-rite Catholic churches keep his commemoration on the Second Sunday of the Great Fast, among them the Melkite Church and the Byzantine Catholic Metropolitan Church of Pittsburgh: **liturgical law, no rung**, neither approval nor condemnation of the distinction; practice is not uniform. Catholic assessments range widely: Adrian Fortescue (*Catholic Encyclopedia*, 1910) called the distinction "fundamentally opposed to the whole conception of God in the Western Scholastic system"; Martin Jugie argued against it at length in the *Dictionnaire de theologie catholique*; Jurgen Kuhlmann (1984) judged the quarrel over the vision a misunderstanding born of the ambiguous word "essence"; Aidan Nichols's *Light from the East* (1995) reads him with sympathy. **Traps:** "the Byzantine Catholic churches hold him a saint" as a blanket claim; "no Catholic may honour Palamas"; "a calendar shows Rome approves the distinction".

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Benedictus Deus

Benedict XII's constitution of 29 January 1336, defining that the purified see the divine essence by intuitive, face-to-face vision, no creature mediating as the object seen.

Its key words: *divinam essentiam*, seen with "no creature mediating as the object seen" (lesson's translation). **Level: rung 1 if *ex cathedra*, at least rung 3 regardless** ([`creation-grace-last-things` 6.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)). The word *essence* in the Catholic doctrine of the vision rests on it, not on Florence: *Laetentur caeli* (6 July 1439) has the purified "clearly behold God himself, three and one, as he is" (*intueri clare ipsum Deum trinum et unum, sicuti est*), **rung 1**, with no word for essence. In 5.4 P2 it shows the Latin saint united to God himself.

**What it is not**, in outline (Boss 5(c) asks what the magisterium has and has not said about Palamas): it predates the first Barlaam-Palamas letters (1337) and every hesychast council; it answers John XXII's question of *when* the vision begins; and neither it nor Florence names Palamas or the energies. The tension that remains: the Catholic holds the essence seen and never comprehended (God incomprehensible, **rung 1**; said of the blessed, **rung 4**), the Palamite holds it never seen; whether the two "essences" are one notion is the [real distinction](#real-distinction) crux again. **Trap:** reading either text as a condemnation of Palamas. The 1895 encyclical's rejection of a full reward before the resurrection targets this doctrine and Florence's *mox* ([Patriarchal encyclical of 1895](#patriarchal-encyclical-of-1895)).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Real distinction

In scholastic Latin, a distinction between one *res* and another, not made by the mind; in Palamite usage, a distinction not made by our minds that implies no division.

**The crux of 5.4.** The premise one side must deny: *whatever in God is uncreated and is God is the essence.* Thomists affirm it: their real distinction holds between one thing and another, and only the relations escape Florence's rule. Palamites deny it: their "real" means not made by our minds, distinguishing God in himself from God giving himself, without division. If the Palamite "real" is the scholastic one, *Firmiter* and Florence bite; if it lies nearer Scotus's [formal distinction](#formal-distinction), they do not, as the Church applies them. **Level: rung 5.** **What would move it:** the *Triads* and the 1351 Tome showing the energies other than the essence as thing from thing, or a Latin argument that any non-relational distinction on the side of the thing breaks *omnino*. The Latin grades of distinction: real, formal, of reason; P4 of 5.4: the Church defined that God has no composition, not how a Greek distinction maps onto those grades.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Formal distinction

Scotus's distinction between formalities on the side of the thing, prior to any act of the mind, within one wholly indivisible reality.

**The Scotist precedent** (5.4, Example 2): [`trinity-and-god` 1.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-03-simplicity-as-doctrine.md) permits it, Thomist and Scotist both affirming *Firmiter*. God's wisdom and goodness are distinct formalities, not opposed by relation, yet no act reads Florence's rule against Scotism. So the Latin acts, as the Church applies them, do not exclude every distinction in God that is neither relational nor ours. **Its limit:** the energies differ from the essence in shareability (creatures share them, never the essence), which formalities do not; Thomists deny that shareability can differ without a scholastic [real distinction](#real-distinction), Palamites affirm it, and the precedent cannot settle which. Whether Palamites would accept "formal" as a translation is unsettled. **Trap:** "the energies are merely Scotist formalities", or "the Scotist precedent settles compatibility", overstates what it shows. Scotus's univocity, next door, is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 8.2](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/08-02-scotus-the-univocity-of-being.md).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Nullity and dissolution

A declaration of nullity finds that no marriage bond ever arose; a dissolution ends a real bond that is not both sacramental and consummated.

**Nullity** is a finding of fact about consent at the wedding (CCC 1629: the marriage never existed), made against a presumption of validity (can. 1060); a putative marriage, invalid but celebrated in good faith by at least one party, has legitimate children (cann. 1061 §3, 1137). **Dissolution** reaches only bonds below the ratified-and-consummated line: the non-consummated and the non-sacramental, including the privilege of the faith (cann. 1141-1143). A ratified, consummated bond no human power can dissolve, **at least rung 2** ([Indissolubility](#indissolubility)). Grounds and procedure: **law, no rung**. Taught in [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.4](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md); 5.5's premise P3 uses the pair: no power is claimed over the bond Christ called indissoluble.

**Worked case** (5.5, Example 2, invented): a twenty-two-year marriage declared null for lack of the discretion of judgment consent requires (can. 1095, 2°). The doctrine defends the category; it cannot certify any one verdict, and a court that read defects back from breakdowns would turn a true distinction into a fiction ([Legal-fiction objection](#legal-fiction-objection)). **Traps:** "the Church has never dissolved a marriage" (cann. 1142-1143); calling dissolutions "annulments"; Waterworth's "dissolve" in Trent XXIV cans. 3-4 renders *dirimere*, "make void" (the nullifying impediments), while cans. 5 and 7 keep *dissolvi* for the bond. Henry VIII sought nullity, not divorce ([`church-history` 6.3](../church-history/lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Oikonomia

"Stewardship" (lesson's gloss): the Orthodox Church's merciful application of a rule for a soul's salvation, as against *akribeia*, "exactness"; in marriage, the blessing of a second or third marriage after a church divorce, with a penitential rite.

**As Orthodox texts state it:** marriage is lifelong and divorce opposed; a second and even a third marriage may be permitted by economy, with a penitential rite, once the first is completely dissolved or annulled (Holy and Great Council, Crete 2016, *The Sacrament of Marriage and Its Impediments* II.2; the Orthodox Church in America; both via [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.4](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)); a fourth is excluded. The Moscow [*Basis of the Social Concept*](#basis-of-the-social-concept) (X.3): the Church does not approve a second marriage, yet after a church divorce the innocent spouse may remarry and the guilty after penance; a third is exceptional. John Meyendorff (*Marriage: An Orthodox Perspective*, 1970; 3rd ed. 1984): marriage a mystery of the Kingdom, not a contract, met by condescension, not by a second norm.

**Argued** (5.5 P4): economy presupposes the first marriage dissolved or annulled, so it ends, or declares ended, a real bond while both spouses live; renaming the act "recognizing a death" leaves that premise standing. **Weight:** Crete met without Antioch, Moscow, Bulgaria and Georgia (Antioch and Georgia had reservations about this very document); the Moscow text binds one Church; **no Catholic level**. **Trap:** "the Orthodox simply ignore Christ's words", or "allow divorce on demand", caricatures: they ground indissolubility in Matthew 19:6 and 9 and present economy as condescension, not denial.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Purgatory

The purification after death of those who die in God's grace but not yet wholly purified, helped by the prayers of the living.

**Defined, rung 1:** Florence, *Laetentur caeli* (6 July 1439), DS 1304-1305: if the truly penitent die in the love of God before making satisfaction "by worthy fruits of penance for what they have done and failed to do", they are purged after death "by purgatorial pains" (*poenis purgatoriis*); the suffrages of the living help them; the purged are received "at once" (*mox*) into heaven (lesson's translation). Trent VI can. 30 (1547), **rung 1**: a "debt of temporal punishment to be discharged ... in the next in Purgatory" (Waterworth); with XXII can. 3 (6.3). **Not** Trent XXV, which restates without a canon. **Rung 5:** fire, place, duration, mechanism; no definition names fire. [Lyons II](#lyons-ii)'s profession defines nothing of itself; Florence kept its *poenis purgatoriis*, the formula Cesarini presented at Ferrara, and dropped its Greek gloss *seu catharteriis*. Two words carry the Latin framework: *satisfecerint*, and *poenis*, which the official Greek at Ferrara rendered *kathartikais timoriais*, "cleansing punishments". CCC 1472 reads temporal punishment as following from the nature of sin, not as vengeance inflicted from without: it locates a reading and fixes no rung. The doctrine is owned by [`creation-grace-last-things` 6.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/06-03-purgatory.md).

**Against the Orthodox** (5.5). Shared: prayer for the dead and, for [Mark of Ephesus](#mark-of-ephesus), a cleansing after death in proportion to faults. Refused: a purgatorial fire, a debt of punishment paid after death, and the [treasury](#treasury-of-the-church); Orthodoxy has defined nothing ([State of the dead](#state-of-the-dead)). The crux: payment or healing? Must Trent's remaining debt be a penalty paid to justice, or can it be CCC 1472's unhealthy attachment? If the latter, Mark's fear and conscience describe much the same purgation, and the dispute shrinks to Florence's authority and the treasury.

**Against the Reformed and the Anglicans** (6.3; Calvin's *Institutes* is a theologian's book, Westminster the Presbyterian subordinate standard, Article XXII an Anglican formulary). Calvin: purgatory is "the satisfaction for sin paid after death by the souls of the dead" (*Institutes* III.5.6, Beveridge), so it falls with satisfaction; yet he concedes "thirteen hundred years" of prayer for the dead (III.5.10). Westminster XXXII.1: the souls of the righteous are "made perfect in holiness" at death, and Scripture knows no third place; Article XXII calls "the Romish Doctrine concerning Purgatory" a thing "vainly invented". Augustine on a cleansing fire after death: "this I do not contradict, because possibly it is true" (*City of God* XXI.26, Dods). 6.3's conclusion: purgatory applies the one offering to members still being sanctified; it is not a rival payment.

**Traps:** "Florence defined the fire"; "Lyons II defined purgatory"; "Trent XXV defined purgatory"; "the Orthodox deny purgatory"; "Florence wrote *poenae* in answer to Mark" (it was already the Lyons formula the Latins presented on 5 June 1438).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Treasury of the Church

The Church's store of the superabundant satisfactions of Christ, with the saints' added, from which she grants [indulgences](#indulgences).

**Level: rung 3, not defined** (Clement VI, *Unigenitus*, 1343; Paul VI, *Indulgentiarum Doctrina*, 1967, §5). Trent XXV never uses the word, and Florence's purgatory definition does not mention it; calling it defined overstates (7.2). 6.3's level table lists it under **at least rung 3**. **Refused by:** the Orthodox, who reject "a superabundance of the virtues of the saints, and the distribution of them" (1895 encyclical §XII; 5.5); Calvin, with Hebrews 10:14, "by one oblation he hath perfected for ever them that are sanctified" (*Institutes* III.5.2; 6.3). Luther's Theses 56-62 against it are worked in `theology-of-debt` Boss 3 and not repeated here. Owned by [`theology-of-debt` 3.5](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-05-the-christian-jubilee-and-the-treasury.md).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Indulgences

The Church's remission, before God, of the temporal punishment still due for sins whose guilt is forgiven, granted from the [treasury](#treasury-of-the-church).

**Levels:** the Church's power to grant them and their usefulness, **rung 1** (Trent XXV, decree on indulgences, 4 December 1563, under anathema); the treasury, **rung 3**; the abolition of gain and of the collectors, and every indulgence norm, **discipline, no rung**. An indulgence looses what the keys can bind (Trent XIV can. 15; 6.3 P5).

**The record, conceded** (6.3, 7.2): the sale was real and systemic. Trent XXV calls the "evil gains" connected with indulgences "a most prolific cause of abuses" and abolishes them; Session XXI (ch. 9, 1562) had abolished the professional alms-collectors; in 1567 Pius V revoked every grant involving fees or other financial transactions (as the *Catholic Encyclopedia*, 1910, reports). The 1517 St Peter's campaign ran on a papal grant ([`church-history` 6.1](../church-history/lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)). **The test** (6.3): the money went in 1567, but the counting of days and years lasted until Paul VI dropped it in 1967, so the Lutheran "natural fruit" reply partly stands. **7.2's crux:** was the doctrine a cause of the abuse or only its occasion? The defence depends on the [disciplinary-doctrinal distinction](#disciplinary-and-doctrinal) holding.

**Objections:** the Orthodox refuse the distribution of the saints' superabundant merits (1895 encyclical §XII; 5.5); Article XXII lists "Pardons" (*de Indulgentijs*) among things "vainly invented"; Luther's Theses are `theology-of-debt` Boss 3, cited and not repeated. Owned by [`theology-of-debt` 3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-06-luther-trent-and-the-reform-of-indulgences.md) and [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Basis of the Social Concept

The Russian Orthodox Church's official statement on social and moral questions, adopted by its Jubilee Bishops' Council in Moscow (August 2000).

Section X.3 on divorce, paraphrased: marriage is indissoluble on Matthew 19:6 and 9, and divorce a sin; adultery, the one ground the Lord named, breaks the bond of marital fidelity; Byzantine law admitted various grounds and drew no objection from the Church; the Local Council of 1918 listed further grounds (apostasy, perversion, impotence, leprosy or syphilis, prolonged disappearance, conviction with loss of rights, attempts on a spouse's life, malicious abandonment, incurable mental illness and more), and the *Basis* adds chronic alcoholism, drug addiction and abortion without the husband's consent; divorce is granted by the Church; the Church does not approve a second marriage at all, yet the innocent spouse may remarry, the guilty after penance; a third marriage is exceptional. **Standing inside Orthodoxy:** binds one autocephalous Church (Moscow), not a pan-Orthodox act; the fullest official list of grounds. **No Catholic level.** **Trap:** treating it, or Crete 2016, as canon law for every Orthodox church.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Legal-fiction objection

The Orthodox charge that Catholic annulment reaches the Orthodox result, a second church wedding, while declaring that years of common life rested on no marriage.

**At strength, as an Orthodox canonist presses it** (5.5 attributes it to no named author): the outcome is the same; the defect is placed at the wedding but read back from the breakdown, under grounds as wide as incapacity (can. 1095); Rome also dissolves some real bonds itself; the Orthodox description, a real marriage that died, mourned with penance, a second union blessed as condescension, is the honest one. **Drop** what a canonist would not sign: "sells it" (a charge of simony) and "find a psychologist" (the presumption of validity, can. 1060, stands until disproved).

**The reply's parts** (5.5, problem P2): nullity is a finding of fact under a presumption of validity; the putative marriage honours the years and the children (cann. 1061 §3, 1137); bad findings argue for better tribunals, not for a power to dissolve; and Orthodoxy still ends a real, consummated bond (Crete II.2). **Concession available:** *Mitis Iudex* itself admits its briefer process could endanger indissolubility ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.4](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)). **Trap:** building the charge on Waterworth's "dissolve" in Trent XXIV cans. 3-4, which renders *dirimere*, "make void".

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Mark of Ephesus

Mark Eugenikos (d. 1444), metropolitan of Ephesus, the Greek champion at Ferrara-Florence who refused to sign the union; an Orthodox saint.

His purgatory texts from Ferrara, June-July 1438 (L. Petit, *Patrologia Orientalis* 15, Greek with Petit's Latin; no public-domain English, so the lesson translates): prayers, liturgies and alms help the departed; souls that die in faith but stained are cleansed in proportion to their faults, not by fire or in a place, but by fear, by a conscience that "gnaws the mind more sharply than any fire", by the loss of God's glory, "as in custody" (doc. II §1); no doctor taught a temporary fire, and it invites Origen's error that punishment ends (doc. III §4, the joint reply with Bessarion); penances are medicine for the living, and for the dying God forgives "both guilt and punishment" (doc. VI, q. 14). **Weight:** a canonized saint writing as a theologian, not a definition; Orthodoxy has defined nothing on the [state of the dead](#state-of-the-dead).

**Traps:** he pressed his case at Ferrara (1438), not "at Florence"; Mark's view stated as Orthodox dogma, or "the Orthodox deny any cleansing after death", both misstate (6.2 Flashback); "the Greeks accepted purgatory" or "were tricked" outruns the record: Mark refused to sign, the signers withdrew at home, and signature is not reception ([`church-history` 4.2](../church-history/lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Ferrara purgatory debate

The written exchange on purgatory between the Latin and Greek delegates at Ferrara, June-July 1438, edited by Louis Petit (*Patrologia Orientalis* 15; Greek with a Latin version).

| Document | What it does |
|---|---|
| doc. I, the Latin *cedula* (Cardinal Julian Cesarini, 5 June 1438) | gives the Lyons formula without fire, the one Florence would define, then argues for a set place and a purgatorial fire; records the Greeks calling the formula "not a dogma of the Eastern Church"; the official Greek renders *poenis purgatoriis* as *kathartikais timoriais*, "cleansing punishments" |
| doc. II §1 | Mark's first oration: cleansing by fear, conscience and the loss of glory, not by fire ([Mark of Ephesus](#mark-of-ephesus)) |
| doc.
