III §§4-5, the joint Greek reply (Mark and Bessarion, 14 June 1438) | splits the question in three: (1) whether God remits venial sins after death: granted; (2) whether by some punishment, such as prison or darkness, or by his mercy and the Church's prayers alone: deferred; (3) whether necessarily by fire, a bodily fire: refused (no doctor taught it; Origen's error) |
| doc. VI, q. 14 | Mark's answers on penance: medicinal; for the dying, guilt and punishment forgiven alike |

**What the record allows** (5.5, problem P3): the Greeks signed a text without the fire and place they had refused, whose *poenae* could bear Mark's custody, fear and conscience; it cannot show what each signatory privately meant, nor that Orthodoxy received the decree.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### State of the dead

What happens to the souls of the departed between death and the resurrection.

**Orthodoxy has defined nothing here** (5.5), so "the Orthodox deny purgatory" is imprecise: they pray for the dead at every liturgy (the 1895 encyclical §XII prays "for the forgiveness and rest" of the departed); many, with [Mark of Ephesus](#mark-of-ephesus), allow a cleansing after death; and they refuse a purgatorial fire, a debt of punishment paid after death, and the [treasury](#treasury-of-the-church). Mark wrote as a theologian; the 1895 encyclical is one patriarchate's letter. Peter Mogila's *Orthodox Confession* (1643, qq. 64, 66), which refuses any cleansing punishment after death while praying for the dead, is one of the [seventeenth-century confessions](#orthodox-confessions-of-the-seventeenth-century) that modern Orthodox theologians hold of limited authority and partly Latinized: never "the Orthodox position". The toll-houses and other disputed Orthodox eschatology are deliberately left out.

**Catholic side:** [purgatory](#purgatory) **rung 1** (Florence; Trent VI can. 30); the purged received at once into heaven (*mox*), **rung 1** in Florence, with *[Benedictus Deus](#benedictus-deus)* for the precisions; the 1895 encyclical rejects a full reward for the just before the universal resurrection and judgment. **Traps:** stating Orthodoxy from Mogila's *Confession*; "undefined, so anything goes" (Mark and the 1895 encyclical state a clear position); Mark's view as Orthodox dogma.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Indissolubility

The property by which a valid marriage cannot be dissolved: absolutely, by no human power, once it is ratified (sacramental) and consummated.

**Levels:** no human power can dissolve a ratified and consummated marriage, **at least rung 2** (John Paul II to the Roman Rota, 21 January 2000, §8; can. 1141; CCC 1640). Trent XXIV can. 7 (1563), which strikes whoever says the Church errs in holding that adultery leaves the bond intact: **rung 1 as worded**, whether it defined indissolubility as revealed being disputed. Can. 5 (no dissolution for heresy, irksome cohabitation or affected absence): **rung 1**. The meaning of *porneia*: **rung 5** ([Exception clause](#exception-clause)).

**The marriage crux** (5.5): can a consummated sacramental bond cease before death? Catholics deny it; [*oikonomia*](#oikonomia) must affirm it, since it blesses no second marriage over a bond in force. What would move it: whether Matthew's clause governs Mark, Luke and Paul or is governed by them. **Worked case** (5.5, Example 1, invented): a valid marriage broken by a husband's adultery; under the Moscow text the innocent wife may divorce in church and remarry; for Rome separation is lawful (Trent XXIV can. 8), remarriage while he lives is not (can. 1141).

**The record, conceded as mixed** (5.5's **Concession**): Origen reports that "some even of the rulers of the church have permitted a woman to marry, even when her husband was living", against Scripture yet "not indeed altogether without reason" (*Commentary on Matthew* XIV.23, ANF 9); Basil: the Lord's sentence applies "to men and women alike", though custom differs, and the deserted husband who remarries is "pardonable" (Letter 188, can. 9, c. 374); Jerome grants the laws of Caesar differ from the laws of Christ and calls Fabiola's second marriage a fault made in good faith (Letter 77.3, 399); Justinian legislated grounds of divorce (Novel 117, 542). **Reformed:** [Westminster](#westminster-confession) XXIV.5-6 allows divorce and remarriage for adultery, "as if the offending party were dead", and for desertion no remedy can cure, the ground Trent XXIV can. 5 had condemned.

**Traps:** "Trent anathematized the Orthodox practice" (can. 7 was worded at Venice's request to spare the Greeks; [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)); the opposite slip, "so remarriage after adultery stayed open", understates (**at least rung 2**).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

### Exception clause

Matthew 19:9 (and 5:32): "except it be for fornication" (Douay-Rheims; Greek *me epi porneia*).

Three of the four witnesses carry no exception: Mark 10:11-12, Luke 16:18, and "the Lord's" command in 1 Corinthians 7:10-11 (5.5 P1). **Orthodox reading:** adultery is the one ground the Lord named, and it breaks the bond (Moscow *Basis* X.3). **Reformed reading:** Westminster XXIV.5 lets the innocent party divorce and remarry. **Catholic readings:** the three of [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 5.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), none defined. **Level:** the meaning of *porneia*, **rung 5**, inside Trent XXIV can. 7's fence, **rung 1 as worded**: *porneia* is open; a licence to remarry is not.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)



**The contested distinctives (Module 6)**

### Marian dogmas

The four Catholic dogmas about Mary: she is *Theotokos* (Mother of God), ever-virgin, immaculately conceived, and assumed body and soul into glory.

Each is **rung 1** by its own act: *Theotokos* (Ephesus, 431); perpetual virginity (ordinary universal magisterium, no single defining act); the [Immaculate Conception](#immaculate-conception) (*Ineffabilis Deus*, 8 December 1854, *ex cathedra*, every clause including the merits clause); the Assumption (*Munificentissimus Deus* 44, 1 November 1950), though **not** whether Mary died. The acts and their content are taught in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.1–6.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-01-theotokos-and-mary-in-scripture-and-the-fathers.md); 6.1 argues them and re-teaches none.

**Who shares what.** The Orthodox confess the first two, call Mary All-Holy, and keep the Dormition as her death and bodily glorification, liturgically, with nothing defined. The Lutheran confessions call her "mother of God" (Formula of Concord, Epitome VIII) and, in the Latin of the Smalcald Articles I.4, *semper virgine* (Luther's German has only "the pure, holy" Virgin; the Triglot's English brackets "[and always]"). The Reformed Second Helvetic Confession XI.4 has *ex Maria semper virgine*. No Reformation confession holds the Immaculate Conception, the Assumption or invoking Mary.

**Where the Reformation objection lands.** On warrant: the Anglican Article VI says what Scripture neither contains nor proves "is not to be required of any man, that it should be believed as an article of the faith"; 1854 says "to be firmly and constantly believed by all the faithful". The clash is over *requiring* Mary's sinlessness, not over anyone *holding* it, so on warrant Mary is a test case of [*sola scriptura*](#sola-scriptura). "You invented a doctrine in 1950" mistakes the definition for the belief, which the Dormition liturgy carried for centuries; whether that growth is development is [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)'s question.

**Traps:** "the Reformers abolished Mary" and "Luther was a Marian Catholic whom Protestants abandoned" are both false; Lutherans are bound by their confessions, not by Luther's sermons.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Immaculate Conception

Mary was preserved from all stain of original sin from the first instant of her conception, by a singular grace given in view of Christ's merits: redeemed, not exempted from redemption.

*Ineffabilis Deus* (Pius IX, 8 December 1854; Schaff vol. 2) defines her preserved "in view of the merits of Christ Jesus". **Rung 1**, every clause, the merits clause included. The Latin is *ab omni originalis culpae labe*: *culpa* usually means fault, and Schaff renders it "original sin". The merits clause is the answer to "she needed no Saviour" ([*praeredemptio*](#praeredemptio)).

**Three objections, different in kind.**

- *Anglican* (Article VI): it may not be required as an article of faith; Article XV, Christ alone is without sin. Formularies of varying subscription.
- *Orthodox, on Kallistos Ware's account* (*The Orthodox Church*, rev. ed. 1993, paraphrased): all Orthodox hold Mary free from actual sin, but since 1854 most reject the dogma as unnecessary, as implying a false idea of original sin as inherited guilt, and as seeming to set Mary apart from Adam's other descendants and the righteous of the Old Testament. Ware calls it theological opinion: an Orthodox who held it could not be called a heretic. Its ground is [ancestral sin](#ancestral-sin).
- *Orthodox, synodical* (the [1895 encyclical](#patriarchal-encyclical-of-1895) of Anthimos VII, §XIII, Metallinos 1896): Christ's incarnation "is alone pure and immaculate"; 1854 laid down "a novel dogma", "unknown to the ancient Church". Its grounds are novelty and Christ's unique purity, not anthropology. A weighty synodical witness of one patriarchate, not a definition; Orthodoxy has **no defined position** on the dogma.

**Levels on original sin** (Trent V, 1546): that sin, not death only, passes to each, **rung 1** (can. 2); privation of original justice as its essence, **rung 4**; the mode of transmission, **rung 5** ([`creation-grace-last-things` 3.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)). So the dogma needs a lack Mary was spared, not Augustine's account of how the lack is handed on.

**Crux (6.1):** does the dogma depend on the Augustinian account of original guilt? If 1854's "original sin" names guilt for Adam's act, the East is right; if it names the absence, from the first instant, of the holiness Adam lost, the dispute shrinks to 1895's: handed down, or invented? **Where the reply stops:** the Catechism's "contracted", not "committed" (CCC 404–405) shows the dogma *can* be stated without inherited guilt, but no act fixes that reading of 1854's *culpa*.

**Traps:** "the Orthodox reject it because they think Mary sinned" (caricature); "the Orthodox Church condemned it in 1895" (overstates).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Ancestral sin

The Orthodox term for what passes from Adam to his descendants: death and corruption, not Adam's guilt.

It grounds the Orthodox objection to the framing of the [Immaculate Conception](#immaculate-conception). 6.1's picture: tell a friend her house is exempt from the flood levy and she will ask what flood levy. The West wrote original sin as inherited guilt and then had to exempt Mary; the East never wrote that rule and needs no exemption. The exegetical root is Romans 5:12, whose *eph' hō* Orthodox writers will not read as the Vulgate's *in quo*, "in whom" (the Greek is [`patristics` 4.5](../patristics/lessons/04-05-augustine-against-the-pelagians.md) and [`new-testament` 5.3](../new-testament/lessons/05-03-romans-5-11-adam-spirit-israel.md)). No Orthodox act defines it; theologians (Ware) state it.

**Catholic counterpart:** Trent V cans. 1–3 (**rung 1**): Adam lost holiness and justice for us, and "sin, which is the death of the soul", not death only, passes to each as his own. Trent defines *that*, not *how*. The Catechism's "contracted", not "committed", with no personal fault (CCC 404–405), locates a reading and fixes no level. What would move the dispute: whether Orthodox theology holds that every child is born without the communion with God Adam lost. If it does, the quarrel is with Trent V can. 2, not with Mary.

**Trap:** "the Orthodox deny original sin" overstates; the objection is to inherited *guilt*.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Latria, dulia, hyperdulia

*Latria* is the worship owed to God alone; *dulia* the honour given a creature for God's gifts in it; *hyperdulia* the highest *dulia*, given to Mary.

**Defined by object, not intensity.** *Latria* acknowledges God as Creator and last end, which no creature shares (Augustine, *City of God* X.1; ST II-II q.103 a.3 ad 1; sacrifice to God alone, q.85 a.2). *Hyperdulia* is the highest honour given a creature for gifts received (q.103 a.4 ad 2). The line falls between Creator and creature, not between more and less.

**Enforced in acts**, which is why it is a real answer and not a label (6.1 P2). The sacrifice is offered "unto God alone", never to a saint (Trent XXII ch. 3, a chapter clause no canon repeats, **at least rung 3**). Nicaea II reserves *latreia* to God (**rung 1**). In 2025 the Dicastery judged the title "Co-redemptrix" always inappropriate (*Mater Populi Fidelis*, 4 November 2025, **rung 3**; the title itself **not defined**). In prayer God is asked to have mercy, the saints to pray for us: "To Him alone do we offer religious worship when praying, from Whom we seek to obtain what we pray for" (ST II-II q.83 a.4 ad 1).

**Levels:** *latreia* for God alone, **rung 1**; *dulia*, *hyperdulia* and Thomas's "relative *latria*" are school words, **no rung**.

**The Reformed objection** (Calvin, *Institutes* I.12.2–3, a theologian's book): the distinction is an evasion, since the question concerns "not the word, but the thing"; it is lexically inverted (*douleia* is service); and people never keep the line in practice. Satan asked Christ only for *proskynēsis* (Matthew 4:10), and the angel refused John's (Revelation 19:10; 22:9). Defining by object answers the lexical argument and leaves the practical one standing, which 6.1 partly concedes.

**Strain (6.2 Example 2):** Aquinas gives Christ's image *latria*, relatively (ST III q.25 a.3), where Nicaea II denied images *latreia*. Reply: the council denies it to the image as a thing, Thomas gives none to the thing, and both end the honour in the person. What to call the act passing through Christ's image is **rung 5**.

**Trap:** "*hyperdulia* is dogma" overstates.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### One Mediator

Christ is the one mediator between God and men, the one who gave himself as the ransom; the intercession of Mary and the saints depends on his entirely and is no second ransom.

1 Timothy 2:5–6 (DR): the "one mediator of God and men" is the one who "gave himself a redemption for all" (*antilytron*, a ransom), and the same paragraph commands "supplications, prayers, intercessions" for all (2:1). Trent names Christ "the one mediator" (Session V can. 3, **rung 1**). Lumen Gentium 60 makes all Mary's saving influence depend entirely on his merits; LG 60–62 on her subordinate mediation is **at least rung 3**. "Co-redemptrix" is **not defined**, and *Mater Populi Fidelis* (2025, **rung 3**) judges the title always inappropriate.

**Opposing texts.** Westminster XXI.2 (Reformed): religious worship goes to God alone, "not to angels, saints, or any other creature", and "nor in the mediation of any other but of Christ alone". The Belgic Confession XXVI (Reformed): no creature loves us more than Christ or has more power, so seeking another intercessor is distrust. The Lutheran Apology XXI.27 is subtler: granting that "the blessed Mary prays for the Church", does she receive souls in death, conquer death, give life? Run on Catholic doctrine, that test returns the Lutheran answer (6.1 Example 1). What remains is whether one may *ask* her prayer: [invocation of saints](#invocation-of-saints), argued in [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md).

**In short (6.1 P3):** asking a prayer is not offering a second ransom.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Praeredemptio

Preservative redemption: Mary was redeemed by being kept from original sin rather than cleansed of it, Christ's merits applied in advance.

The 1854 merits clause, "in view of the merits of Christ Jesus", is **rung 1**; Scotus's argument for the doctrine is theology, **no magisterial level**. The definition prevailed against Bernard's premise that the parents' concupiscence passes on sin ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-03-the-immaculate-conception.md)).

**Its use in 6.1** is narrow: the answer to the Orthodox objection that the [Immaculate Conception](#immaculate-conception) sets Mary apart from Adam's race. She differs from us in the *mode* of her redemption, not in her need of it. It does not settle the deeper framing objection, which turns on what "original sin" names ([ancestral sin](#ancestral-sin)).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Communion of saints

The one Church of the living on earth, the dead being purified, and the blessed in heaven, bound together in charity and prayer.

The Creed's "communion of saints" is **rung 1**, but it names the communion, not invocation as its mode (doctrine: [`creation-grace-last-things` 6.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)). 6.2's argument from it: every side grants that asking another Christian's prayer is good (Calvin, *Institutes* III.20.19; Smalcald Articles II.ii); the saints are alive in God, who "is not the God of the dead, but of the living" (Luke 20:38, DR); and Jerome argues *a fortiori*: "how much more must they do so when once they have won their crowns" (*Against Vigilantius* 6, 406). Death expels no one from Christ's body. How the saints know our prayers is **rung 5** (Aquinas: it needs only that God make it known, ST II-II q.83 a.4 ad 2).

**What the other side grants.** The Lutheran Apology XXI.9: the saints in heaven pray "for the Church in general". Calvin III.20.24: they pray for us in general, but "far less are they, therefore, to be invoked by us". Luther, Smalcald II.ii: a Christian on earth can pray for me, "But for this reason I am not obliged to adore and invoke you".

**Crux (6.2):** is invoking a saint the same kind of act as asking a friend's prayer, the friend being in heaven? Or a different kind, because a request made in prayer to the unseen is prayer (Westminster XXI.3) and prayer belongs to God? For the act and its level see [invocation of saints](#invocation-of-saints).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Second commandment

The prohibition of Exodus 20:4–5 against making images to bow down to: numbered as a separate second commandment by the Reformed and the Orthodox, folded into the first by Catholics and Lutherans.

The Reformed Heidelberg Catechism counts four commandments in the first table, the image clause second (Q.92–93), and reads it as a [rule of worship](#regulative-principle). Luther's Small and Large Catechisms and the Roman Catechism (1566) follow Augustine's division, and the Roman Catechism prints the whole image clause under the first. Philaret's nineteenth-century Russian *Longer Catechism* (Q.516–521) numbers it second and defends icons. **Numbering decides nothing**: the Orthodox count as the Reformed do and still venerate images.

**Catholic reading:** the commandment forbids images made to be adored as gods, since God himself commanded the cherubim (Exodus 25:18) and the brazen serpent (Numbers 21:8–9), and the Incarnation gave God a face ([`patristics` 5.4](../patristics/lessons/05-04-maximus-and-john-of-damascus.md)). The Reformed reply to the serpent: Hezekiah broke it when incense was burned to it (4 Kings 18:4, DR; 2 Kings 18:4).

**Weight:** Heidelberg is a Reformed confessional standard; Luther's catechisms stand in the Book of Concord; Philaret's catechism is not an Orthodox definition (the Orthodox act is Nicaea II); the Roman Catechism carries the weight of what it restates.

**Trap:** "Catholics deleted the second commandment" is false.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Regulative principle

The Reformed rule of worship: God may be worshipped only in the ways he has commanded in Scripture.

Heidelberg Catechism Q.96: God is to be worshipped in no "other way than he has commanded in his Word"; Q.97 allows pictures of creatures, but not to worship them or serve God by them. Q.98 applies the rule to the classic Catholic defence: "But may not pictures be tolerated in churches as books for the laity? No; for we should not be wiser than God" (Schaff vol. 3). That refuses Gregory the Great's images as books for the unlettered (to Serenus, *Register* XI.13) in advance, so answering Q.98 with Gregory alone misses. Westminster XXI.1 states the same rule for the Presbyterian standard: worship as "instituted by himself".

**The Lutheran analogue** concerns invocation, not images: the Apology XXI.10 finds "neither a command, nor a promise, nor an example" in Scripture, so such prayer cannot be made in faith. Aquinas met the objection (ST III q.25 a.3, objection and reply 4) from unwritten tradition and the legend that Luke painted an image of Christ kept at Rome.

**Where it leads:** it is the sufficiency question in liturgical form ([2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)). A Reformed reader is moved only by showing that "worship only as commanded" does not decide the case, which returns the saints-and-images dispute to [*sola scriptura*](#sola-scriptura).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Nicaea II

The seventh ecumenical council (787), which defined the veneration of images against iconoclasm and reserved *latreia* to God.

Its *horos* (Percival, NPNF2 14) gives images "honourable reverence" (*timētikē proskynēsis*), not the *latreian* "which pertains alone to the divine nature", grouping them with the Cross and the Book of the Gospels, because "the honour which is paid to the image passes on to that which the image represents". **Rung 1**: the council "define[s]", with penalties (deposition for clerics, excommunication for laity) and the synod's acclamation anathemas; Trent XXV restates it "as ... has been defined". It is received as ecumenical by Orthodox and Catholics. The Reformed may say it erred (Westminster XXXI.4), and then lose the story of a papal corruption (6.2 P6).

**Quote the translation you read.** Percival prints "passes on to that which the image represents"; "passes to the prototype" is Basil's phrase, which the council cites; Trent's English is "referred to the prototypes".

**The Frankish complication.** The Caroline Books (c. 790–793) and the Synod of Frankfurt (794, can. 2) condemned giving images "service and adoration as to the Divine Trinity", which Nicaea never taught. They misread Constantine of Constantia, who at session 3 honoured the images and reserved the worship of adoration to the Trinity alone; Latin *adoratio* rendered both *latreia* and *proskynēsis*. Calvin repeats the misreading (*Institutes* I.11.16). Frankfurt: **no magisterial level**, never received. Gregory the Great and the Franks kept images but refused them *adoratio*.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Veneration of images

Honour paid to an image of Christ or a saint, which passes through the image to the person represented: like kissing a photograph of your mother, it honours the person, never the paint.

**Rung 1.** [Nicaea II](#nicaea-ii)'s *horos* defines it; Trent XXV restates it and closes its images section with an anathema on whoever teaches or holds "contrary to these decrees" (DS 1824). Trent: no "divinity, or virtue" is believed to be in images; the honour is "referred to the prototypes". The reform clauses (no unusual image without the bishop's approval; "every superstition shall be removed", DS 1825) are **discipline**.

**Opposing positions, kept apart.**

- *Reformed:* Heidelberg Q.96–98 (the [rule of worship](#regulative-principle); Q.97 allows pictures of creatures; Q.98 refuses even "books for the laity"); Calvin, *Institutes* I.11–12 (the thing, not the word; Elvira can. 36; Epiphanius tearing an image from a church curtain in 394; five hundred years of churches "completely free from visible representations", I.11.13).
- *Anglican:* Article XXII names the Romish doctrine of "Worshipping and Adoration, as well of Images as of Relics".
- *Lutheran:* the confessions condemn no image as such.
- *Orthodox:* allies; they receive Nicaea II and venerate icons while numbering the [second commandment](#second-commandment) separately.

Gregory the Great (to Serenus, 599 and 600) kept images and forbade their adoration, calling images books for the unlettered; Heidelberg Q.98 already refuses that defence.

**Concession (6.2):** images treated as if power lived in them were real, and Trent legislates against it; Lumen Gentium 51 still asks that abuses be corrected. **Trap:** "a tolerated custom, which a Catholic may judge unlawful" understates (6.4 Flashback).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Relics

The bodies or belongings of saints, honoured because those bodies were members of Christ and temples of the Spirit, and will be raised.

**At least rung 3.** Trent XXV (DS 1822): those who deny relics honour are "wholly to be condemned", as the Church long since condemned them; whether the decree's closing anathema (DS 1824) reaches relics is not manifest (can. 749 §3). Trent's ground: those bodies were "the living members of Christ, and the temple of the Holy Ghost", to be raised unto eternal life. The oldest evidence: Smyrna gathered Polycarp's bones as more precious than jewels, while it was Christ "we adore" and the martyrs "we worthily love" (*Martyrdom of Polycarp* 17–18, c. 155, ANF). The oldest practice already drew Nicaea's line. See also Acts 19:12; 4 Kings 13:21 (DR).

**Concession and discipline (6.2):** relic traffic was real and profitable. Trent XXV orders "all filthy lucre be abolished" and no new relic without the bishop's approval (DS 1825): **discipline**. The Anglican Article XXII condemns the Romish doctrine of relics with that of images. Relics as practice are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 7.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md).

**Traps:** "Trent only regulated relics; honouring them is discipline" understates; raising them to rung 1 on the images anathema overstates.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Trent Session XXV

Trent's last session (3–4 December 1563): decrees on purgatory, on the invocation and veneration of saints, their relics and sacred images, and on indulgences.

**The saints-and-images decree has no canons, but its images section ends with an anathema** on whoever teaches or holds "contrary to these decrees" (DS 1824). Clause by clause:

| Clause | Level |
|---|---|
| Invoking the saints is "good and useful"; deniers "think impiously" (DS 1821) | **at least rung 3**: the anathema's reach is not manifest, though manuals mark it *de fide*; Trent XXII can. 5 (**rung 1** as worded) presupposes it |
| Relics: deniers "wholly to be condemned" (DS 1822) | **at least rung 3** |
| Images: no "divinity, or virtue" in them; honour "referred to the prototypes" (DS 1823), restating Nicaea II | **rung 1** |
| Abuses abolished, superstition removed, "all filthy lucre" banished, nothing new without the bishop (DS 1825) | **discipline** |

**The other decrees.** *Purgatory* (3 December): restates without a canon; the defining acts are Florence (1439), Trent VI can. 30 and XXII can. 3, never XXV ([purgatory](#purgatory)). *Indulgences* (4 December): their power and usefulness under anathema, **rung 1**; the abolition of "all evil gains", "a most prolific cause of abuses", is **discipline**. Session XXV never names the [treasury](#treasury-of-the-church).

**Traps:** "the decree has no anathema, so invocation is optional" understates; "Trent defined invocation under anathema" overstates. The Anglican Article XXII, agreed in Convocation in 1562 by the English reckoning, predates Session XXV and cannot be aimed at it.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Guilt and punishment

Forgiveness removes the guilt of sin and the eternal punishment, but a temporal punishment can remain, as a drinker reconciled with his wife still lives with what his drinking caused.

**Rung 1:** temporal punishment can remain after justification, discharged in this world or in purgatory (Trent VI can. 30, 1547); and it is condemned to say that God always remits the whole punishment together with the guilt (XIV can. 12, 1551). The ledger itself (*culpa*, *poena*, satisfaction) is [`theology-of-debt` 3.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md).

**Both sides grant that the forgiven suffer.** Westminster XVII.3 (Reformed): the saints may "bring temporal judgments upon themselves"; Calvin, *Institutes* III.4.31: chastisement. What Calvin denies is the distinction as Trent draws it: "if we are freed from guilt by Christ, the punishment consequent upon guilt must cease with it" (III.4.30). So the dispute is what the suffering *is*: [chastisement or satisfaction](#chastisement-and-satisfaction).

**The vocabulary in 6.5.** Trent offers the Mass for "sins, punishments, satisfactions" (XXII ch. 2; canon 3 renders *poenis* "pains"). The Anglican Article XXXI condemns Masses said to win "remission of payne or gylt" (*poenae aut culpae*). The Lutheran Apology's propitiatory sacrifice is "a work which makes satisfaction for guilt and punishment". The same pair links all three texts.

**Trap:** "temporal punishment after forgiveness is a scholastic theory" understates (rung 1).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Satisfaction

The penitent's share, made in Christ and through his merits, in discharging the temporal punishment that can remain after guilt is forgiven.

**Rung 1:** satisfaction for temporal punishment is made to God through Christ's merits, by penances imposed, by voluntary penances and by sufferings patiently borne (Trent XIV can. 13), and it is a worship, not a human tradition (can. 14). Chapter 8 (Waterworth): our satisfaction is not "so our own, as not to be through Jesus Christ", "in whom we satisfy". Chapter 8's further clause, that satisfactions serve "not only ... a medicine of infirmity, but also for the avenging and punishing of past sins", is a chapter no canon repeats: **at least rung 3**, never "defined".

**Opposing texts.** Westminster XI.3 (Reformed): Christ "did fully discharge the debt" of the justified and made "a proper, real, and full satisfaction to his Father's justice", with Hebrews 10:10 and 10:14 among its proofs; XV.3: repentance is not "any satisfaction for sin". Calvin, *Institutes* III.4.29–30; and III.5.6 makes purgatory "the satisfaction for sin paid after death", so it falls with satisfaction. The Lutheran Apology XII (satisfactions not necessary by divine law) is read in [`theology-of-debt` 3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-06-luther-trent-and-the-reform-of-indulgences.md).

**Common ground (6.3 P1):** on the price, Trent and Hebrews 10:14 agree; the dispute is the [crux](#chastisement-and-satisfaction).

**Trap:** "Trent defined satisfaction as vengeance" overstates (ch. 8, at least rung 3).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Chastisement and satisfaction

The crux of 6.3: is the suffering that follows forgiveness satisfaction owed to justice for past sins, as Trent holds, or a father's correction aimed only at amendment, as Calvin holds?

**Calvin** (*Institutes* III.4.31, a theologian's book): God's chastisement of his own is "not properly punishment or vengeance, but correction and admonition", the act of a father, not of a judge. **Trent** (XIV ch. 8): satisfactions are a medicine and also "for the avenging and punishing of past sins", a chapter clause no canon repeats (**at least rung 3**); canon 13 defines only *that* satisfaction is made, through Christ's merits (**rung 1**). Calvin ties purgatory to the first answer ("the satisfaction for sin paid after death", III.5.6), so the whole system turns here.

**The texts pull both ways.** 2 Samuel 12:14 (DR 2 Kings 12:14), "because thou hast given occasion to the enemies of the Lord to blaspheme", names the past sin as cause (worked in [`theology-of-debt` 3.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md)). Hebrews 12:10, "that we might receive his sanctification", names holiness as goal.

**What would move it:** a showing that correction aimed at holiness can still be owed to justice, as the Catechism attempts: temporal punishment follows from the very nature of sin, not as vengeance inflicted from outside (CCC 1472, paraphrased). A Catholic should notice how little his side has fixed: canon 13 defines that satisfaction is made; the vengeance clause is a chapter's.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Private confession

Confessing one's sins to a priest and receiving absolution, in the sacrament of penance.

**The fact Catholic apologists routinely miss: the Lutheran confessions kept it.** Augsburg XI: "Private Absolution ought to be retained in the churches"; XXV: confession "is not abolished among us". Smalcald III.8: absolution is "ordained by Christ [Himself] in the Gospel" and "ought by no means to be abolished". Small Catechism V: before the confessor, "those sins alone which we know and feel in our hearts". Apology XIII counts "Absolution, which is the Sacrament of Repentance" a true sacrament. Calvin urges the troubled to "a private confession to his own pastor" (*Institutes* III.4.12). The Anglican Ordinal of 1550, like that of 1662, ordains each priest with John 20:23, though Article XXV does not count penance among the "Sacraments of the Gospel".

**What they reject is narrower:** the [enumeration of sins](#enumeration-of-sins) by divine law, and absolution as a trial; its ministry "is favor or grace, it is not a legal process" (Apology, "Of Confession and Satisfaction").

**Trent's canons that touch Lutheran texts** (Session XIV, **rung 1**): can. 4 condemns "two parts only" of penance (terrors of conscience and faith), nearly verbatim Augsburg XII; cans. 6–7 make confession of divine right and integral; can. 9 makes absolution a judicial act. **No canon condemns keeping private absolution.**

**Crux (6.5 Flashback):** is retaining sins (John 20:23; the [power of the keys](#power-of-the-keys)) a verdict on the case, or a refusal to the impenitent that needs no list? Trent argues no priest can judge without knowledge of the cause; the Apology answers that ministers absolve even sins we do not remember.

**Trap:** "Protestants abolished confession" blurs three traditions into one error.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Enumeration of sins

Telling the confessor each grave sin by kind and number: of divine right for Trent, not necessary for the Lutheran confessions.

**Trent XIV cans. 6–7, rung 1:** sacramental confession is of divine right and integral. Chapter 5 argues from the judicial nature of absolution (no judgment without knowledge of the cause), cites "From my secret sins cleanse me, O Lord" for forgotten sins, and calls it impious to call such confession impossible or a "slaughter-house of consciences", answering the Apology's Latin *carnificina*.

**Lutheran** (Book of Concord): Augsburg XI, "an enumeration of all sins is not necessary. For it is impossible", citing Psalm 19:12 ("Who can understand his errors?"); Augsburg XXV adds that the Gloss admits confession is "of human right only"; Apology XI asks the adversaries to show it from divine law; Smalcald III.8: "the enumeration of sins ought to be free to every one"; Small Catechism V: only the sins "we know and feel in our hearts". Trent and Augsburg cite the same psalm.

**Not here:** what integral confession does and does not require (the mortal sins remembered) is [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md) and its Boss 4(a).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Once for all

Hebrews' claim that Christ offered himself a single time: a sacrifice never to be repeated and fully sufficient.

**The words** (Hebrews, DR; Greek Tischendorf). *Ephapax*, "once for all": 7:27, 9:12, 10:10. *Hapax*, "once": 9:26, 9:28 (not *ephapax* at 9:26). He did not "offer himself often", "for then he ought to have suffered often" (9:25–26). "By one oblation he hath perfected for ever them that are sanctified" (10:14). "There is no more an oblation for sin" (*ouketi prosphora peri hamartias*, 10:18). The Levitical priest "standeth daily ... often offering the same sacrifices" (10:11), and repetition shows the old sacrifices never perfected (10:1–2). Hebrews itself is [`new-testament` 6.2](../new-testament/lessons/06-02-hebrews.md).

**Against satisfaction (6.3).** Westminster XI.3 cites 10:10 and 10:14 for Christ's full discharge of the debt; Calvin uses 10:14 against the treasury (*Institutes* III.5.2). The Catholic reply: on the price Trent agrees ("in whom we satisfy", XIV ch. 8). And "them that are sanctified" renders *tous hagiazomenous*, a present participle, "those being sanctified" (6.3's rendering), in a letter whose Father scourges every son "that we might receive his sanctification" (12:6, 10). 10:14 completes the cause, not the effect in each member.

**Against the Mass (6.5).** The text every Reformation tradition rests on. Trent's chapter 1 starts from it (Christ "about to offer Himself once on the altar of the cross"; **at least rung 3, not open to denial**). The Catholic claim: "once" excludes another offering, not the one offering [made present](#re-presentation). Whether 10:18 as worded excludes Trent's offering "for the sins" of the living is where the dispute sits.

**Trap:** treating the argument from Hebrews as a misunderstanding; both sides receive the text and read it with care.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Disciplinary and doctrinal

The line between conduct and changeable law (discipline: binding as law, no doctrinal rung) and what the Church proposes as belonging to faith (doctrine: graded on the ladder). A grievance against the first can be wholly true without touching the second.

**Penance (6.3).** Trent condemned the indulgence trade (Session XXI ch. 9 abolished the professional collectors; XXV abolished "all evil gains") and kept the doctrine under anathema (XXV). The whole defence depends on that line holding. The Lutheran reply: the trade was the doctrine's natural fruit. The separability test: remove the money and see whether the abuse regrows (indulgences have run without fees for four centuries, though the counting in days and years lasted until 1967).

**Galileo (7.1).** The 1616 [Index decree](#index-decree-of-1616) had both parts: a doctrinal censure (**rung 3 at its lightest while in force**) and book bans (**discipline, no rung**). Calling it "only disciplinary" dodges the doctrinal charge. *Ad extirpanda* (1252) is **law, no rung**; the 1633 sentence a **judicial act, no rung**.

**The grievances (7.2).** Both sides draw such a line. Augsburg Part II opens: "our churches dissent in no article of the faith from the Church Catholic, but only omit some abuses"; Calvin says conduct does not take away the name of Church but doctrine "inverted" does (*Institutes* IV.2.1). The quarrel is where the line runs: Augsburg files private Masses under abuses, Trent files the propitiatory sacrifice under doctrine.

**The objection that it is too convenient (7.2):** (1) the abuses grew from doctrine (Augsburg XXIV; Smalcald II.2); (2) abolishing the money while defining the doctrine treats the symptom and protects the cause; (3) the line is drawn after the event by the party it protects (Calvin, IV.5.7: they "cloak these abominations with the name of church"). It is the [retreat objection](#retreat-objection) in another form.

**Where it stops:** it shows a doctrine does not *entail* an abuse; it cannot show the doctrine did not make it *likely*. It sorts acts by level, not blame by rank: a papal grant is governance, not acquitted.

**Traps:** "discipline" does not mean minor or blameless, since reform decrees bind as law; and the line answers the doctrinal charge, never the moral one.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Real presence

The doctrine that Christ himself, body and blood, is truly given in the Eucharist. Catholics, Lutherans and the Reformed all say "truly", so the phrase alone sorts nothing.

**Catholic** (Trent XIII, 1551, Waterworth): Christ, body and blood with soul and divinity, is truly, really and substantially contained, not "only ... as in a sign, or in figure, or virtue" (can. 1); the whole substance of the bread is converted, only the species remaining, which the Church "most aptly" calls transubstantiation (can. 2). **Rung 1** for the presence, the change and the term, **not** for Aristotle. Eating "spiritually only, and not also sacramentally and really" is condemned (can. 8, **rung 1** as worded). The eucharistic reading of John 6 is **not defined** (Trent XXI ch. 1 leaves it to the Fathers' various interpretations).

**Four ways to make "This is my body" true** (6.4):

| Reading | Whose | "Is" taken as |
|---|---|---|
| It **signifies** my body, as a ring signifies a marriage | Zwingli ([memorialism](#memorialism)) | a figure |
| A sign that **gives** what it names, as a deed conveys land | Calvin, Heidelberg, Westminster ([spiritual presence](#spiritual-presence)) | a figure that conveys ([metonymy](#sacramental-metonymy)) |
| The body truly **with** the unchanged bread | the Formula of Concord ([sacramental union](#sacramental-union)) | an "unusual" predication |
| What was bread **has become** the body | Trent ([transubstantiation](#transubstantiation)) | identity |

All three Protestant doctrines reject transubstantiation, and they fight each other as hard as they fight Trent. Of the Formula's twenty-one negative theses on the Supper only three are Rome's; Calvin found Rome's doctrine "more tolerable, or at least more modest" than Lutheran ubiquity (*Institutes* IV.17.30).

**Concession (6.4):** the definition is not committed to Aristotle; whether "substance" in canon 2 must be read in the *Categories*' sense is **rung 5**. And the Reformed confess a real feeding on Christ's own body, so the quarrel with them is over where and how it is given, not whether.

**Crux:** can "this is my body" be true of bread without a change in what the bread is?

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Sacramental union

The Lutheran doctrine that Christ's true body and blood are present with the unchanged bread and wine, so that the bread "is" the body by a unique union that is no change of substance.

**Stated as the Formula of Concord states it** (art. VII; Epitome in Schaff's English, Solid Declaration in the Triglot):

- by the sacramental union the bread and wine "are truly the body and blood of Christ" (Epitome VII, affirmative II);
- "under the bread, with the bread, in the bread" rejects transubstantiation and marks the union of the bread's *unchanged* substance with the body (SD VII.35);
- the words are an "unusual" predication (*praedicatio inusitata*), not "a figurative" one, modelled on "the Word was made flesh", yet expressly "not a personal union" (SD VII.36–38);
- the body is eaten by the mouth, "not Capernaitically" (affirmative VI), and by the unworthy too, to judgment ([*manducatio indignorum*](#manducatio-indignorum));
- nothing is a sacrament apart from the use Christ instituted (SD VII.85–87).

Augsburg X (1530): the body and blood are truly present (*vere adsint*) and distributed to those who eat. Melanchthon's 1540 *Variata* ("with the bread and wine are truly offered") dropped the rejection of contrary teachers, and Calvin signed it; Lutherans subscribe the unaltered Confession. **Weight:** confessions of the Book of Concord, *norma normata*, not a magisterium.

**Catholic level:** canon 2's first clause (the bread's substance "remains conjointly with" the body) condemns it, **rung 1**. Aquinas's argument that with the bread remaining one should rather say "Here is My body" (ST III q.75 a.2) has **no magisterial level**.

**Crux (6.4; 6.6 Flashback):** whether the Christological analogy carries. In Christ one person bears what is said of both natures ([`christology` 3.2](../christology/lessons/03-02-the-communication-of-idioms.md)); the Formula calls the Supper's union "not a personal union", so it owes an account of what single thing "this" names.

**Traps:** "consubstantiation" is not the Formula's word; "in, with and under" is later shorthand, and the English trio is Westminster's, in a denial (XXIX.7); the Formula rejects a body "locally spread out" ([ubiquity](#ubiquity)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md), [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

### Manducatio indignorum

The Lutheran teaching that the unworthy and unbelieving also receive Christ's true body and blood in the Supper, to their judgment.

Formula of Concord, Epitome VII, affirmative VII (1 Corinthians 11:27, 29); the Smalcald Articles III.6 (Luther): the true body and blood are received "not only by the godly, but also by wicked Christians". **Weight:** Book of Concord, *norma normata*.

**The opposite, Reformed and Anglican:** Westminster XXIX.8 and the Anglican Article XXIX (first printed in 1571) deny that the wicked receive the thing signified, the body itself. The Catholic premise from the same chapter of Paul: the unworthy eater is "guilty of the body and of the blood of the Lord" (1 Corinthians 11:27), so the offence is aimed at the body, not at a reminder (6.4 P3).

**Trap:** swapping the Lutheran and Reformed positions on the unworthy.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Modes of presence

Luther's account, quoted in the Formula of Concord, of how Christ's one body can be present somewhere in more than the local way.

**Luther's four reasons** (Formula, SD VII.93–97, from his *Large Confession concerning the Supper*, 1528): (1) Christ is God and man in one undivided person; (2) God's right hand is everywhere; (3) God's word does not lie; (4) God has and knows "many modes of being in any place", not only the local one.

**The three modes** (SD VII.98–101): (1) the comprehensible, bodily, circumscribed mode he used on earth; (2) the incomprehensible, spiritual mode that takes up no room, by which he left the sealed tomb, came through shut doors, and is in the bread and wine; (3) the divine, heavenly mode by which he is one person with God. **Weight:** a Lutheran confession, *norma normata*.

**Compare:** Aquinas puts the body in the sacrament neither circumscriptively nor definitively (ST III q.76 a.5 ad 1; [presence by way of substance](#presence-by-way-of-substance)), **no magisterial level**.

**Boss 6(a)** sets these four reasons against Calvin's two restrictions (under [ubiquity](#ubiquity)) and asks for the single premise on which they divide; the card gives the parts, not the answer.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Communication of idioms

Because Christ is one person in two natures, what belongs to either nature is truly said of the one person.

The rule itself is [`christology` 3.2](../christology/lessons/03-02-the-communication-of-idioms.md). **The Lutheran use** (Formula of Concord VIII): by the communication of majesty the exalted human nature shares the divine majesty, so that "not only as God, but also as man" Christ "is present to all creatures" (Epitome VIII, affirmative XI), though not "locally spread out" into all places (negative X). It grounds the Lutheran answer to the Reformed objection that a body is in one place.

**The Reformed reply:** by his human nature Christ "is now not upon earth" (Heidelberg Q.47), and the Godhead is beyond the bounds of the manhood and yet in it (Q.48; later called the *extra Calvinisticum*, a term not in the text).

**Two Eucharistic uses of the Incarnation** (6.4 Example 2): the Formula uses it for two natures that remain unchanged and united (SD VII.36–37, naming Justin, Augustine, Gelasius and others); Ambrose uses it for a real change under an unchanged appearance (*On the Mysteries* 9.52–53; [`patristics` 4.1](../patristics/lessons/04-01-ambrose-of-milan.md)). The Fathers' "nature" is not the thirteenth century's "substance".

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Ubiquity

The opponents' name for the Lutheran teaching that, by the personal union and Christ's exaltation, his human nature shares the divine majesty and is present to all creatures, so that his body can be given wherever the Supper is held.

**Whose word:** not the Formula's (in the Triglot it appears only in Bente's 1921 introductions). It is Calvin's: "an ubiquity contrary to its nature" (*Institutes* IV.17.16), "the monstrous dogma of ubiquity" (IV.17.30). The Formula's own terms are the [communication of idioms](#communication-of-idioms) and Luther's [modes of presence](#modes-of-presence), with local extension rejected (Epitome VIII, negative X).

**The Reformed objection it answers** (Calvin, a theologian's book; Heidelberg Q.47): the risen body is declared "not by Aristotle, but by the Holy Spirit, to be finite, and to be contained in heaven until the last day" (IV.17.26); and "The question here is not, What could God do? but, What has he been pleased to do?" (IV.17.24). Hence IV.17.19's **two restrictions**: nothing derogatory to Christ's heavenly glory, such as binding him to earthly elements; nothing inconsistent with his human nature, such as a body in many places at once.

**Boss 6(a)** asks for the single premise dividing these restrictions from Luther's four reasons (it is not what "the right hand" means, which neither side takes for a place) and whether the Catholic doctrine needs either; the card does not answer it. The Catholic account to cite is [presence by way of substance](#presence-by-way-of-substance) and [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md).

**Traps:** a body "spread out everywhere like air" is not the Formula's teaching; answering Calvin with omnipotence alone (IV.17.24 already meets it); saying Christ leaves heaven at the consecration (Trent XIII ch. 1: he always sits at the Father's right hand in the natural mode).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Spiritual presence

The Reformed doctrine that believers truly feed on Christ's own body and blood, which remain in heaven, by the power of the Holy Spirit through faith, and not by any presence in or under the bread.

**Stated from its texts:**

- Calvin (*Institutes* IV.17, a theologian's book): a "true and substantial communication" of the body and blood (IV.17.19); the Spirit "truly unites things separated by space" (IV.17.10); "I rather feel than understand it" (IV.17.32).
- Heidelberg (1563): "although he is in heaven, and we on the earth", we are flesh of his flesh by the Holy Ghost (Q.76); we are "as really partakers of his true body and blood, through the working of the Holy Ghost" (Q.79).
- Westminster XXIX.7 (1646): worthy receivers feed on Christ "really and indeed, yet not carnally and corporally, but spiritually"; the body is not "in, with, or under the bread" but present to faith as really as the elements are to the senses. XXIX.8: the wicked do not receive the thing signified.
- The Anglican Article XXVIII, Reformed in character: the body is given, taken and eaten "only after an heavenly and spiritual manner", and the mean of receiving it is faith.

**Weight:** Heidelberg and Westminster are Reformed subordinate standards; the Articles are Anglican formularies of varying authority.

**Catholic level:** Trent XIII can. 8 condemns eating "spiritually only, and not also sacramentally and really" (**rung 1** as worded). Whether Calvin's "true and substantial communication" is what canon 8 condemns is **rung 5**: no act names him.

**Trap:** making it Zwingli's bare [memorial](#memorialism). Calvin shares Zwingli's figurative "is" ([sacramental metonymy](#sacramental-metonymy)), not an empty sign.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Sacramental metonymy

The Reformed reading of "This is my body": as the usage of sacraments requires, the sign is given the name of the thing it signifies.

The Consensus Tigurinus (Zurich and Geneva, 1549) takes the words "figuratively", by metonymy (art. 22), and forbids imagining a local presence (art. 21). Heidelberg Q.78: the bread is not changed, but is called the body "agreeably to the nature and usage of sacraments". Calvin rests on Zwingli's exegesis of "is" (Schaff, *History* VII §111) but holds that the sign gives what it names, as a deed handed over conveys the land.

**Three readings of one word** (6.4 P1–P2): over what he held Jesus said "is": *touto estin to sōma mou* (Matthew 26:26; Mark 14:22; Luke 22:19), in Paul *touto mou estin to sōma* (1 Corinthians 11:24). Metonymy makes "is" mean "is called"; the Formula's unusual predication makes it, on 6.4's argument, "is with"; Trent reads identity, since a sentence of identity is false of two substances that both remain (Aquinas, ST III q.75 a.2, **no magisterial level**).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Memorialism

Zwingli's doctrine: "This is my body" means "this signifies my body", and the Supper is the community's commemoration of Christ's death, with Christ present to the believing heart but his body not given in or with the bread.

**As its author held it** (via Schaff, *History of the Christian Church* VII, public domain): *est = significat*, argued from John 6:63, "the flesh profiteth nothing" (DR 6:64), in the *Commentary on True and False Religion* (1525; §106). At Marburg (1529) Zwingli and Luther agreed on fourteen articles and most of the fifteenth, splitting on "whether the real body and blood of Christ are corporally present in the bread and wine" (§108). In 1531 (*Exposition of the Faith*) he confessed the body eaten "not in a gross and carnal manner, but in a sacramental and spiritual manner" by the believing heart (§111). So it is not a bare symbol of an absent Christ. Calvin still faulted Zwingli for not adding that "the reality is conjoined" with the signs (*Short Treatise on the Lord's Supper*, 1541, §58).

**Weight:** Zwingli wrote as a theologian; Marburg is an agreed statement of a colloquy, no church's confession.

**Traps:** "memorialism" = "no presence"; Zwingli = Calvin (Calvin's is [spiritual presence](#spiritual-presence)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Paschasius and Ratramnus

The two ninth-century monks of Corbie whose exchange (830s and 840s) first put the question whether the Eucharist is Christ's body "in figure or in truth".

**What the exchange proves (6.4 P4):** both said "truly" and both said "figure"; they divided over what *veritas* means, and the West later took Paschasius's side when forced to choose (the oath of 1079; Lateran IV, 1215; [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)). It shows which answer the Western Church chose, not that earlier centuries held a theory. **No magisterial level.**

**The early witness around it:** Ignatius calls the Eucharist "the flesh of our Saviour" (*Smyrnaeans* 7); Justin says it is received "not as common bread" (*1 Apology* 66). That witness excludes an absent body more clearly than it chooses among the other readings. Tertullian has Christ make the bread "His own body" and, in the same sentence, call it "the figure of my body" (*Against Marcion* IV.40): reading either Zwingli's sign or Trent's conversion into it is [anachronism](#anachronism).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Presence by way of substance

Aquinas's account of how Christ's body is in the sacrament: after the manner of a substance, whole in the whole and whole in each part, not as a body is in a place, so it is neither moved, multiplied in space nor stretched.

ST III q.76 a.5 (English Dominican translation): "not in this sacrament as in a place, but after the manner of substance"; *ad* 1: neither circumscriptively nor definitively. **No magisterial level**; whether a Catholic must hold it is **rung 5**.

**Trent's own words** (XIII ch. 1, Waterworth): Christ always sits at the Father's right hand "according to the natural mode of existing", yet is present to us "sacramentally ... in his own substance, by a manner of existing, which, though we can scarcely express it in words", faith holds possible to God. A chapter no canon repeats: **at least rung 3**.

**Its use (6.4 P5):** the Catholic answer to the Reformed objection that a finite body is in heaven ([ubiquity](#ubiquity)): the ascended body stays one and in its natural mode. **Crux against the Reformed:** whether a body can be present in its substance without being in a place.

**Trap:** presenting Aquinas's account as Trent's definition. Trent says only that the manner can scarcely be expressed; resting on Aquinas overstates.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Sacrifice of the Mass

The Catholic doctrine that the Mass is a true and proper sacrifice: the one sacrifice of the cross made present, with the same victim and the same principal offerer, only the manner of offering different.

**Trent XXII** (17 September 1562, Waterworth):

| Text | Content | Level |
|---|---|---|
| Canons 1–4 | a true and proper sacrifice; "Do this" made the apostles priests to offer; [propitiatory](#propitiatory) for the living and the dead, "for sins, pains, satisfactions"; nothing taken from the cross | **rung 1** as worded |
| Canon 8 | private Masses not unlawful | **rung 1** as worded |
| Ch. 1 | Christ "about to offer Himself once on the altar of the cross"; the one sacrifice *repraesentaretur*, [made present](#re-presentation) | **at least rung 3, not open to denial** |
| Ch. 2 | the same victim, "the same now offering by the ministry of priests, who then offered Himself on the cross", "the manner alone of offering being different" | **at least rung 3, not open to denial** |
| Ch. 1 on Malachi 1:11 and 1 Corinthians 10 | readings; no defined exegesis | **at least rung 3** |
| Reform decree; stipend canons | the traffic | **law, no rung** |
| Accounts of the *how* (de la Taille, *Mysterium Fidei*, 1921; Vonier; Casel) | | **rung 5** |

Augustine's four questions frame it: "to whom it is offered, by whom it is offered, what is offered, for whom it is offered" (*De Trinitate* IV.14.19). The doctrine is taught in [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.4](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md); 6.5 argues it.

**Four traditions, one considered rejection**, resting on Hebrews' [once for all](#once-for-all):

- *Lutheran:* Augsburg XXIV keeps the Mass, "celebrated with the highest reverence", and rejects the opinion that it takes away the daily sins of living and dead [*ex opere operato*](#ex-opere-operato) (Schaff prints it as "Art. III"). The Apology XXIV allows only a [eucharistic species](#two-species-of-sacrifice) of sacrifice. The Smalcald Articles II.2 (Luther): of this article "nothing is to be surrendered or conceded".
- *Reformed:* Westminster XXIX.2: Christ is "not offered up to his Father, nor any real sacrifice made at all for remission of sins of the quick or dead", only a commemoration and a spiritual oblation of praise. Calvin, *Institutes* IV.18 (a theologian's book), meets "application" and "bloodless" in advance.
- *Anglican:* Article XXXI (English of 1571, printed whole in 6.5's Source; quote it in full or not at all, since `sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.4 trims it): Christ's one offering is the only satisfaction for sin, wherefore the sacrifices of Masses, in which it was commonly said the priests offered Christ for the quick and the dead to have remission of pain or guilt, were blasphemous fables and dangerous deceits. Newman's minority reading is [Tract 90](#tract-90).

**The ransom picture (6.5):** a ransom is paid once; in one picture a herald carries the release out (everything moves from God to us), in the other the one who paid pleads the payment before the king (something moves Godward, and nothing is paid again).

**Traps:** "the Reformers objected because they thought Catholics believe Christ dies again" (Calvin records the Catholic denial, IV.18.5); "Trent defined *how* the Mass is the same sacrifice" overstates; "the how is open, so the sacrifice is opinion" understates canons 1–4.

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Tract 90

Newman's *Remarks on Certain Passages in the Thirty-Nine Articles* (Tracts for the Times no. 90, 1841), written as an Anglican, arguing that the Articles were aimed at popular errors, not at the Roman creed as such.

**§9, on Article XXXI** (as 6.5's Source reports it): the Articles target "actual existing errors" in Rome, "whether taken into its system or not"; "commonly said" is "a strictly historical mode of speaking"; the blasphemous fable is a sacrifice for sin other than Christ's death, and the dangerous deceit the trade in Masses. On the plain reading, by contrast, any Mass offered to remit punishment or guilt is a second satisfaction.

**Weight:** one Anglican theologian's reading, with no standing as a formulary; **no Catholic level**.

**Boss 6(b)** sets the Article beside Trent XXII ch. 2 and canon 3 and asks whether Trent's offering "for the sins, punishments, satisfactions" falls under the Article on each reading; the card does not answer it.

**Trap:** treating Tract 90 as *the* Anglican reading of the Article.

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Two species of sacrifice

The Lutheran Apology's division: a sacrament is God's work presenting something to us, a sacrifice our work rendered to God, and sacrifice has only two kinds, propitiatory and eucharistic.

**Apology XXIV** (1531; Triglot): "the proximate species of sacrifice are two, and there are no more". The *propitiatory* is "a work which makes satisfaction for guilt and punishment", one that reconciles God or merits remission for others. The *eucharistic* "does not merit the remission of sins or reconciliation, but is rendered by those who have been reconciled". "The death of Christ alone is truly a propitiatory sacrifice"; the Levitical sin-offerings were so called only to signify a future expiation. The Apology allows the Supper as a eucharistic sacrifice. **Weight:** Book of Concord, *norma normata*.

**Catholic reply (6.5 P5):** the dichotomy catches the Mass only if every propitiatory act now is a *new* satisfaction. Trent claims the one satisfaction made present, offered principally by Christ, obtaining mercy for those who come "contrite and penitent".

**In 7.1's Flashback:** Justin's prayers and thanksgivings, "the only perfect and well-pleasing sacrifices" (*Dialogue* 117), fit the Apology's eucharistic species as they stand; on Malachi 1:11 the Apology itself lets the pure offering include the ceremony, provided not the ceremony alone and not *ex opere operato*.

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Ex opere operato

"From the work done": a sacrament acts by Christ's power when validly performed, not by the merit of minister or recipient.

Trent VII can. 8 (1547; Waterworth, "through the act performed"), **rung 1** as worded; the doctrine and its fruitfulness are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 1.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md).

**The Mass-specific charge (6.5).** Augsburg XXIV rejects the opinion that the Mass is a work taking away the sins of the living and the dead *ex opere operato* (the Triglot's English: "by the outward act"). The Apology XXIV: the Supper "does not confer grace ex opere operato", and applied for others it does not merit for them the remission of sins, of guilt or of punishment.

**The answer (6.5 P5):** Trent XXII ch. 2 conditions the mercy obtained on drawing near "contrite and penitent", and the dead it names are those departed in Christ. How much a Mass avails anyone Trent does not say; Aquinas's measure of devotion is theology.

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Re-presentation

The Catholic claim that the Mass makes the one sacrifice of the cross present, remembered and applied, rather than repeating it or adding to it.

Trent XXII ch. 1: the bloody sacrifice was to be *repraesentaretur*, represented, made present (**at least rung 3, not open to denial**); CCC 1366–1367 restate it and fix no level. How the one sacrifice is present (de la Taille, Vonier, Casel, the destruction theories) is **rung 5**.

**The crux of 6.5: is a re-presentation a distinct offering?**

- Count a sacrifice by its *acts of offering*, and every Mass is a new oblation, which Hebrews 10:18 excludes: the one sacrifice "is no more to be repeated" (Calvin, *Institutes* IV.18.3).
- Count it by its *victim*, its *principal offerer* and the self-offering Hebrews says continues before God (7:25; 9:24; 8:3), and the Mass is that one offering made present.

Both sides agree the cross is complete and must reach us; they divide over whether it reaches us by offering as well as by gift: "As widely as giving differs from receiving, does sacrifice differ from the sacrament" (IV.18.7). Calvin met "not repetition, but application" in advance: the benefit was to be communicated by preaching and the Supper (IV.18.3).

**What would move it:** whether "Do this for the commemoration of me" sets a memorial before God or a reminder before us; whether the heavenly intercession (8:3, which Reformed exegetes read of the cross) is itself an offering. Trent's reform decree calls the victim "daily immolated", words that, unexplained, say what Hebrews 10:11 denies (6.5 Example 2).

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Propitiatory

Of a sacrifice: obtaining God's mercy for sinners. For Trent the Mass obtains, by the one sacrifice made present, the mercy the cross won: it pleads the cross and pays nothing again.

Trent XXII ch. 2: "truly propitiatory"; through it "we obtain mercy" if we draw near "contrite and penitent"; offered for the living's "sins, punishments, satisfactions" and for those departed in Christ and not yet fully purified. Canon 3 (**rung 1** as worded): propitiatory for the living and the dead, "for sins, pains, satisfactions" (*poenis* in both texts). **Direction:** God provides the propitiation; the Son does not change the Father's mind ([`christology` 5.1](../christology/lessons/05-01-the-paschal-mystery-and-the-threefold-office.md)).

**The Lutheran contrast term:** the Apology's propitiatory species, "a work which makes satisfaction for guilt and punishment"; "the death of Christ alone is truly a propitiatory sacrifice" ([two species](#two-species-of-sacrifice)).

**Concession (6.5):** "propitiatory" needs explaining, not asserting.

**Not in the second century in Trent's terms (7.1 Flashback):** Justin's *Dialogue* 117 attests an offering Christ commanded, offered to God as thanksgiving; not that it is propitiatory, that Christ is what is offered, or that it avails for others. Reading Trent into it is [anachronism](#anachronism).

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Private Masses and stipends

Masses at which only the priest communicates, and offerings given to have a Mass applied for an intention. Their multiplication and the trade in them were a real late-medieval abuse, conceded.

**The charge.** Augsburg XXIV: "Masses have been basely profaned and applied to purposes of lucre", and the Lutherans discontinued private Masses; it traces Masses said "only for fees or stipends" to "the opinion which infinitely increased Private Masses" (read in [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)). The Smalcald Articles II.2 list the buying and selling of Masses; Westminster XXIX.4 counts "Private masses" contrary to the sacrament; Calvin, *Institutes* IV.18.

**Trent's answer, in two parts.** The "Decree concerning the things to be observed, and to be avoided, in the celebration of Mass" (17 September 1562) told bishops to prohibit "all manner of conditions and bargains for recompenses" and to banish "a fixed number of certain masses" as superstition: **law, no rung**, as are the stipend canons of the 1983 Code. But Trent **kept** the private Mass: chapter 6 calls such Masses truly common, and canon 8 condemns calling them unlawful (**rung 1** as worded).

**Trap:** "Trent abolished private Masses". It reformed the traffic and defended the private Mass.

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Mozley objection

J. B. Mozley's charge (1847) that Newman's theory of development cannot detect corruption by excess, so Rome's later doctrines are safe by definition.

**Who:** a Tractarian who stayed in the Church of England when Newman left; he died in 1878 as Regius Professor of Divinity at Oxford. His review of the *Essay* (*Christian Remembrancer*, January 1847) was reprinted as *The Theory of Development* (1878). **At full strength** (pages of 1878):

1. He grants explanation: "All allow that Christian fundamental truth has been explained" (p. 147); care for the dead, honour to Mary and the Eucharistic change are Christian things (pp. 30–31).
2. His charge is **excess**: "the faults of an adding, not a diminishing system" (p. 30).
3. The notes cannot see excess. Corruption is defined as loss of type, so exaggeration "has no head for it to come under" (p. 34), and Rome's doctrines are "secure by the definition" (p. 36).
4. Logical sequence decides nothing: "to make logic infallible, we must have an infallible logician" (p. 43). The keystone is papal infallibility (p. 83), and on that footing "whatever is is right" (p. 118).
5. The Nicene parallel fails: Nicaea explained what was held from the start, and "implicit knowledge in multitudes of cases is no knowledge at all" (p. 204).

**Weight:** a theologian's book, no confessional standing; the Anglican formulary beneath it is Article VI. ARCIC I (*Authority in the Church II*, 1981, §30) reports many Anglicans judging the Marian definitions insufficiently grounded in Scripture (a dialogue text, **no Catholic level**).

**Newman's falsifiers** (*Essay*, 1878): a teaching that does but "contradict and reverse" what went before is "certainly corrupt" (ch. 5 §6); if the Church "has ever contradicted her own enunciations", his hypothesis "is at once shattered" (ch. 3 §2 ¶12). They meet a charge of contradiction, not Mozley's of excess; Newman's reply to excess, that truth cannot be excessive (ch. 5 §6), is a denial, not a test.

**Concession (6.6):** the objection has force. A theory every later doctrine passes would be unfalsifiable, and the notes have sometimes been used that way.

*Lessons:* [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

### Ad fontes

"Back to the sources": the humanist and Reformation programme of correcting accretion by returning to Scripture and the early Church.

**In confessional words.** The Augsburg Confession, closing its doctrinal articles (after Article XXI): its doctrine varies neither from Scripture nor from the Church Catholic, and the dispute concerns only "certain Abuses, which have crept into the Church without rightful authority" (Triglot). Westminster I.6 (Reformed) allows what "by good and necessary consequence may be deduced from Scripture" and forbids additions "by new revelations of the Spirit, or traditions of men". Deduce, never add: legitimate inference against accretion.

**Weight:** Augsburg, a Lutheran confession (*norma normata*); Westminster, the Presbyterian subordinate standard.

**Set against *Dei Filius* ch. 4 (7.2's Flashback):** both allow later formulation and forbid new content; they divide on whether a later doctrine is the same faith only if it follows from Scripture by necessary consequence, or by the sense the Church "has once declared".

**Trap:** "Protestants reject all development". Westminster grants "good and necessary consequence" and Mozley grants explanation; the objection is to addition and excess.

*Lessons:* [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

### Received, not developed

The Orthodox counter-theory: the faith is received whole and lived in the Spirit, definitions put into words what the Church already knows, and "development of doctrine" mistakes theological elaboration for growth and then makes it binding.

**Synodical form:** the 1895 encyclical of Patriarch Anthimos VII and his synod (§XX, Metallinos, 1896) finds in the sources "the old and divinely-transmitted doctrines" and none of the West's later innovations; its §XIII calls 1854 "a novel dogma" ([6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)). A synodical letter of one patriarchate, not a definition.

**Theologians (paraphrased):** Vladimir Lossky ("Tradition and Traditions", 1952): Tradition is the life of the Holy Spirit in the Church. Andrew Louth (foreword to John Behr, *The Way to Nicaea*, 2001): development of doctrine is a Romantic idea that Newman popularized; theology grows, the apostolic confession does not. **Not a bloc:** Daniel Lattier (*Pro Ecclesia*, 2011) argues that Florovsky and Staniloae sit closer to development than is usually supposed. Orthodoxy has defined nothing about development. The Orthodox sing the Dormition and define nothing ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-04-the-assumption.md)).

**Catholic reply (6.6 P1):** every party accepts some later formulation, and the Orthodox receive Nicaea II and the fourteenth-century councils on Palamas. The dispute is which later statements are the same faith, not whether any are.

**Trap:** "the Orthodox stopped in 787", or "deny the Assumption".

*Lessons:* [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

### Deep in history

Newman's sentence "To be deep in history is to cease to be a Protestant" (*Essay on the Development of Christian Doctrine*, 1878 edition, Introduction §5).

**Genuine, and what it claims.** It closes a paragraph on English neglect of church history; its claim, one sentence earlier, is that "the Christianity of history is not Protestantism": antiquity does not countenance the Protestant doctrines of faith, sacraments, ministry and the visible Church (§6). It stands in the *Essay*, published after Newman's reception in October 1845, so it is not an Anglican's admission. **No magisterial level.**

**Tested (6.6 Example 1):** it follows only as a negative, against the claim that the Reformation restored the primitive Church. It does not deliver Rome. Newman himself concedes "apparent inconsistencies and alterations" (§7) and that Vincent's rule strikes Rome too (§8); Mozley adds that depth in history could land a man in the East, whose creed is "the creed of St. Basil and St. Chrysostom" (p. 137); Harnack read the same centuries as overgrowth ([`church-history` 8.3](../church-history/lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)). Getting from it to Rome needs development, the point in dispute. [`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md) left the question open for this course.

**Trap:** using it as proof of Rome, or as an Anglican's admission.

*Lessons:* [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

**The hard history (Module 7)**

### Inquisition

The medieval papal commissions of inquisitors and the later Spanish and Roman tribunals for trying baptized Christians suspected of heresy.

**The record, taken whole from `church-history`** ([5.3](../church-history/lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md), [6.5](../church-history/lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)). Papal inquisitors worked under commissions from the 1230s; Innocent IV's *Ad extirpanda* (15 May 1252) let lay officials torture suspects (**law, no rung**). The registers show execution as the exception (Bernard Gui relaxed at most 45 of some 636 convicted to the stake) and record the prosecutors' own categories. The [Spanish Inquisition](#spanish-inquisition) was a crown tribunal with papal sanction. The Roman Inquisition (1542) sent Bruno to the stake in 1600, among some 1,250 death sentences (Del Col's estimate). Relaxation to the secular arm excused no one.

**The teaching behind the practice, with levels (7.1 P4):** the coercion of heretics was law (*Ad extirpanda*), theology (Aquinas, ST II-II q.11 a.3: convicted heretics may be "put to death"; **no magisterial level**) and once a papal censure ([*Exsurge Domine*](#exsurge-domine) prop. 33, 1520: its contradictory **at least rung 3 as a 1520 act, no precise note**). No act defined it, and [*Dignitatis Humanae*](#dignitatis-humanae) teaches the contrary principle; development or reversal is **rung 5**.

**Concession (7.1):** the inquisitions killed people for heresy, and the Church gave the practice her doctrinal approval. John Paul II called the Church's children to repent of the "acquiescence" given to intolerance and violence in the service of truth (*Tertio Millennio Adveniente* 35, 1994); 7.1 says the record warrants a stronger word.

**Traps:** "the state did the burning, so the Church is clear"; answering the moral charge with the line between definitions and other acts, which answers only the doctrinal charge.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Spanish Inquisition

A crown tribunal with papal sanction (Sixtus IV's bull, 1 November 1478), whose inquisitors the monarchs named; finally abolished in 1834.

**Numbers only as [`church-history` 6.5](../church-history/lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md) gives them, as a range:** historians estimate its executions from under 3,000 (Kamen; Monter) to about 12,000 (Del Col), most of them before 1530. Llorente's 31,912 (1817–18) was rejected by Lea (1907). Never one number, never "millions". It tried baptized Christians, chiefly conversos suspected of judaizing.

**Both legends fail:** see [Black Legend](#black-legend).

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Black Legend
