The anti-Spanish caricature that inflated Spanish and inquisitorial cruelty; Julián Juderías popularized the name, *la leyenda negra*, in 1914 ([`church-history` 7.1](../church-history/lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)).

**Corrected in both directions (7.1):** "millions" has no documentary basis (Lea rejected Llorente's 31,912 in 1907), and neither has the "White Legend" (Benjamin Keen's term) of a fair court that hardly killed anyone. Use the [range](#spanish-inquisition) of estimates.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Galileo affair

The 1616 censure of Copernicanism and the 1633 condemnation of Galileo: conceded in this course as an error really made, by the Church's own tribunals, with papal involvement.

**The acts and their levels** (Gebler, trans. Sturge, 1879; the record is [`church-history` 7.3](../church-history/lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)):

| Date | Act | Level |
|---|---|---|
| 24 February 1616 | Holy Office consultors: the sun's immobility "formally heretical" | unpublished advice, **no level** |
| 5 March 1616 | Congregation of the Index decree, Paul V approving ([Index decree of 1616](#index-decree-of-1616)) | **not a definition**; its censure **rung 3 at its lightest while in force**; its book bans **discipline, no rung** |
| 22 June 1633 | Holy Office sentence: Galileo "vehemently suspected of heresy" | **judicial act, no rung** |
| 1758–1835 | the bans lapse in stages | |
| 31 October 1992 | John Paul II to the Pontifical Academy of Sciences | **the lightest grade of rung 3** for any principle restated; **no rung** for its historical verdicts |

The 1633 sentence calls the 1616 judgment "declared and defined to be contrary to Holy Scripture": a court's description, not a definition, and the critic's best evidence for the [retreat objection](#retreat-objection). The 1992 address blames most theologians of the time (§9), recalls that the 1633 sentence was not irreformable (§9), and never says a definition erred.

**Not re-run here:** the conflict thesis ([`apologetics-foundations` 4.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-01-the-conflict-thesis-tested-in-both-directions.md)) and `church-history`'s Bellarmine exercise (its Boss 7).

**Traps:** naming the Holy Office as author of the 1616 decree; treating "declared and defined" as making 1616 a definition; calling the decree "only disciplinary".

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Index decree of 1616

The decree of the Congregation of the Index (5 March 1616), approved by Paul V, which declared the Copernican doctrine "false and altogether opposed to Holy Scripture" and suspended Copernicus "until they be corrected".

**Body:** the Congregation of the **Index**, not the Holy Office; the syllabus row's "disciplinary decree of the Holy Office" is wrong on both counts. The Holy Office's acts were the consultors' unpublished advice (24 February 1616) and the 1633 sentence.

**Two parts, two weights:** a doctrinal censure, a congregation's non-definitive judgment with ordinary papal approval (**rung 3 at its lightest while in force**, withdrawn in stages 1758–1835), joined to book bans (**discipline, no rung**). It is **not a definition**: no pope spoke *ex cathedra* ([infallibility](#infallibility)), and "until they be corrected" is not a definition's form; it announces reformability.

**Trap on the Catholic side:** calling it "only disciplinary" to escape the doctrinal charge (some sibling texts call it a "disciplinary decree"); see [disciplinary and doctrinal](#disciplinary-and-doctrinal). The honest answer concedes that a Roman congregation, with a pope's approval, censured a truth as contrary to Scripture and bound consciences for two centuries.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Argument from institutional error

The argument that a Church whose tribunals taught error and killed dissenters cannot be the Church Christ keeps from error.

**Its confessional premises, as their texts state them** (Boss 7(a) close-reads these; the card only names them):

- *Anglican*, Article XXI (English of 1571; omitted in the American revision of 1801): general councils "may erre, and sometyme haue erred, euen in thinges parteynyng vnto God".
- *Reformed*, Westminster XXXI.4 (XXXI.3 in the American numbering): all synods since the apostles "may err, and many have erred", a help in faith and practice, never their rule. XXV.5: the purest churches are subject "both to mixture and error", yet "there shall be always a Church on earth to worship God according to his will".
- *Calvin* (*Institutes*, a theologian's book): the Church cannot err in things necessary to salvation only so far as she submits to the Spirit through the word (IV.8.13); Rome places that authority "without the word of God" and maintains its doctrine of unerring councils "first, in writing, and then, by fire and sword" (IV.8.10).
- *Orthodox*: the [1895 encyclical](#patriarchal-encyclical-of-1895) of Anthimos VII, §XVIII: apart from Christ the Orthodox Church "knows no one that was infallible upon earth" (Liberius, Zosimus, Vigilius, Honorius); §XIX: the Orthodox churches "remain, and will always remain, faithful".

**At full strength** (7.1's assembly from the record, no single author's words): Rome claims the Spirit keeps her from error in faith and morals; in 1616 a Roman congregation, with papal approval, censured a truth as contrary to Scripture, and a 1633 court called it "declared and defined"; a pope licensed torture, and another condemned the denial that heretics may be burned; these were the Church teaching, not private sins; so the claim is false. The second-order charge is the [retreat objection](#retreat-objection).

**The Catholic answer is a distinction, not an excuse** (7.1 P1–P4): [infallibility](#infallibility) attaches to definitions; [indefectibility](#indefectibility) promises that the Church lasts in faith and sacraments, not impeccability; no Galileo act and no coercion act was a definition. It answers the doctrinal charge only, **never the moral one**.

**Crux (7.1):** can "the Church" of the promise err in a judgment of this kind (a congregation's censure, an [*in globo*](#in-globo-censure) papal condemnation) without the promise failing?

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Dignitatis Humanae

Vatican II's Declaration on Religious Freedom (7 December 1965): the human person has a right to immunity from coercion in religious matters.

**By section (paraphrased):** §1 leaves untouched the traditional teaching on the moral duty toward the true religion; §2 grounds the right in the dignity of the person, known from revelation and from reason, and asks for it in constitutional law; §9 says the doctrine has roots in revelation; §10 says the act of faith must be free; §12 admits that the Church's people at times acted in ways hardly in accord with the Gospel, while holding that the teaching that no one is to be coerced into faith has always stood. **Rung 3**, defining nothing (some theologians read its core as definitive). The doctrine is owned by [`catholic-social-teaching`](../catholic-social-teaching/syllabus.md).

**Against *Exsurge Domine* prop. 33 (7.1 Example 2).** Leo X (15 June 1520) condemned, among 41 propositions and *in globo*, "that heretics be burned is against the will of the Spirit" (lesson's translation). The Catholic reply: the censure was no definition, and the constant teaching concerned forcing faith on the unbaptized, whereas heretics were punished as baptized subjects. The second move strains, because the council's ground, every person's dignity, covers the baptized dissenter too. Development or reversal: **rung 5** ([6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md) supplies the tests). **Where it stops:** it shows that no definition was reversed, not that no pope taught, at rung 3, a moral proposition the Church now rejects; this is where "your own documents contradict each other" ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)) lands hardest.

**Traps:** "*Dignitatis Humanae* defined religious liberty"; "*Exsurge* defined that heretics may be burned".

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Retreat objection

The charge that whenever the Church errs, the erring act is reclassified as not having met infallibility's conditions, or filed under "discipline", so that the claim can never be falsified: the ladder as "a device for retreating".

Sent to this course by [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md); the crux of 7.1. A claim no record can falsify claims nothing; it is ad hoc protection of a theory's core in Popper's and Lakatos's sense ([`philosophy-of-science`](../philosophy-of-science/syllabus.md)).

**What a Catholic must show (7.1):** that the conditions were fixed independently of the failures, and what would falsify the claim, named in advance: a definition meeting *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 4's conditions, shown false. The critic's best evidence: the 1633 court called the 1616 judgment "declared and defined to be contrary to Holy Scripture".

**In 7.2, as "too convenient":** the [disciplinary/doctrinal](#disciplinary-and-doctrinal) line is drawn after the event by the party it protects (Calvin, *Institutes* IV.5.7). 7.2's partial answer: the reformers draw the same kind of line (Augsburg Part II preface; Calvin IV.2.1), and Trent's own acts sorted law from doctrine at the time (reform decrees against canons).

**In 7.3:** the critic presses *Dei Filius* ch. 3: a Church that offered her holiness as a sign cannot retreat to the deposit when the sign fails. Any reply has to name what would refute it (7.3 Flashback).

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Reformation grievances

The sixteenth-century complaints against the late medieval Church, of two kinds: conduct and law, and doctrine.

| Kind | Grievances |
|---|---|
| Conduct and law | simony; [pluralism and *commendam*](#pluralism-and-commendam); absentee bishops; sees handed on like estates; untrained priests; the Renaissance papacy; the money behind the St Peter's indulgence |
| Doctrine | the Mass for the living and the dead; the power to grant indulgences |

**As the reformers made them.** Luther, *Address to the Christian Nobility* (1520; Buchheim's translation, 1883; a pamphlet with no confessional standing): annates kept as a tax, convents handed to cardinals in *commendam*, incompatible benefices held together, and "I complain that they do not observe their own fabricated canon law". Calvin (*Institutes*, a theologian's book): "youths who are thrice abbots, twice bishops, once archbishops" (IV.5.7), churches treated "merely in the light of farms" (IV.5.11). The Augsburg Confession, Part II, articles XXII–XXVIII (Triglot numbering; Schaff numbers them I–VII): both kinds, the marriage of priests, the Mass, confession, meats, monastic vows, the power of bishops.

**Not Donatist:** none of these texts argues that bad clergy unchurched Rome ([Donatism](#donatism)); the case is doctrinal, and the grievances are its symptoms.

**Verdict and concession (7.2):** most of the disciplinary grievances were justified, by Catholic testimony ([*Consilium*](#consilium-de-emendanda-ecclesia); Trent's own reform decree of 1547 calls discipline "exceedingly relaxed"). Trent legislated against nearly every item, a concession in the form of law, and the abuse reached papal acts: the 1517 campaign ran on a papal grant ([`church-history` 6.1](../church-history/lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)). The honest Catholic argument is not that the Church was healthy but that a corrupt Church can still be the Church.

**Crux (7.2):** was the doctrine of indulgences a cause of the abuse, or only its occasion? Entailment fails (no fees for four centuries, [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)); tendency is a historical judgment the [distinction](#disciplinary-and-doctrinal) cannot make.

**The counter-question** (Brandenburg's sees appropriated by Hohenzollern princes; Zurich drowning Felix Manz; Geneva burning Servetus) tests only a claim to be purer and never exculpates Rome. The decay debate (Dickens against Duffy) is graded in `church-history` 6.1–6.4.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Pluralism and commendam

Pluralism: one cleric holding several benefices or sees at once, usually with absenteeism. *Commendam*: a benefice "commended" to someone, often a cardinal, who drew its revenue without doing its duties.

**Three witnesses, one verdict (7.2 Example 1).** Luther (1520) complains that convents go to cardinals in *commendam*. The *Consilium* (1537) objects to cardinals given several bishoprics "conferred or commended", since a bishop must live with his sheep (abuse 8), and to incompatible benefices (abuse 7). Trent forbids anyone, "by whatsoever dignity" distinguished, to hold several cathedral churches "by title, or in commendam" (Session VII, 3 March 1547, reform decree ch. 2), and restricts plural benefices (Session XXIV, 1563, reform decree ch. 17). All three say the practice broke the Church's own canons: the law was right and the prelates broke it. Nothing follows against the Church's doctrine.

**Level:** the reform decrees are **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Donatism

The fourth- and fifth-century African position that the Church's holiness and the sacraments' validity depend on the ministers' holiness, so that a corrupt clergy unchurches a body.

**Every party in this course rejects it.** Augsburg VIII (Lutheran): sacraments administered by evil men may be used, and "They condemn the Donatists". Calvin, *Institutes* IV.1.13 (no withdrawal from a church for imperfection of conduct; the Cathari and the Donatists; the net and the tares) and IV.2.1 ("no errors of conduct, no defects" take away the name of Church; it dies when the sum of necessary doctrine is "inverted"). The Anglican [Article XXVI](#article-xxvi): the evil sometimes "have chief authority", and their ministry may still be used. Westminster XXVII.3 (Reformed): a sacrament's efficacy does not depend on "the piety or intention of him that doth administer it". Catholic: a minister in mortal sin still confers the sacrament (Trent VII can. 12, **rung 1**). Augustine's answer is [`patristics` 4.4](../patristics/lessons/04-04-augustine-against-the-donatists.md); holiness and the mixed body, [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-02-holy.md).

**So** "a corrupt Church can still be the Church" is common ground (7.2 P4). The reformers' case against Rome is doctrinal, with the abuses as symptoms, and the [argument from scandal](#argument-from-scandal) is not Donatist either: stating it that way fails it (7.3).

**Trap:** "bad popes don't falsify doctrine" answers an argument no confession makes.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Consilium de emendanda ecclesia

The confidential report on the Church's abuses that a commission of nine named by Paul III gave him in 1537, before Trent.

**Who wrote it** (as signed; B. J. Kidd, *Documents Illustrative of the Continental Reformation*, 1911, No. 126): four signed as cardinals (Gasparo Contarini, the chair; Gian Pietro Carafa, later Paul IV; Jacopo Sadoleto; Reginald Pole) and five as prelates (Federico Fregoso, Girolamo Aleandro, Gian Matteo Giberti, Gregorio Cortese, Tommaso Badia). Not "the cardinals".

**What became of it:** the members were sworn to secrecy and reported in 1537; the text leaked and was printed in 1538, and Luther issued it in German that year with a preface and mocking notes.

**Shape:** a preface, then twenty-six numbered abuses with remedies; [Boss 7(c)](syllabus.md) close-reads the preface and the indulgence clause of item 21. 7.2's Source is abuse 5, in the lesson's translation of Kidd's Latin: bishoprics resigned with a right of return and reservations of revenues, which amounts to "appointing oneself an heir to the benefice", and the Church's revenues diverted "from common to private use", which kindled hatred of the clergy.

**Level:** **no magisterial level**. A commission's report: evidence, not teaching.

**Trap:** calling it papal teaching, or "the cardinals' verdict".

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Corpus permixtum

Augustine's "mixed body": the visible Church holds good and bad together until the harvest, and the Lord, not anyone before the end, sorts them.

Augustine calls it "the true and the mixed body of the Lord" (*De doctrina christiana* III.32.45, quoted in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-02-holy.md)). His formula has **no magisterial level**; that sinners are inside the Church is **at least rung 3**. The reformers' confessions share it: Augsburg VIII (hypocrites and evil persons are mingled with the Church); Article XXVI ("the evil be ever mingled with the good"); the Belgic Confession 29 (hypocrites mixed in the Church with the good); Calvin IV.1.13 (the net and the tares).

**Its use (7.2 P4):** the ground of "a corrupt Church can still be the Church". **Where it stops:** it explains why wicked men can hold office in a true Church; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-04-a-church-of-sinners.md) marks its limit at policy, and 7.3 takes that as given when the charge is failed discipline.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Argument from scandal

The argument that an institution which concealed the abuse of children for decades cannot be the Church Christ founded: its claim to teaching authority is discredited by its exercise of authority.

**What it is not:** [Donatism](#donatism). Augsburg VIII condemns the Donatists; [Article XXVI](#article-xxvi) keeps evil ministers' sacraments; Calvin calls the offence at a church's poor fruit "well founded" and still refuses to say no church exists there (*Institutes* IV.1.13).

**What it is, in four steps** (7.3's reconstruction from confessional clauses, no confession's article):

1. Rome claims more than any Reformation church: bishops who teach faith and morals with Christ's authority (Lumen Gentium 25, paraphrased), and a Church whose "eminent holiness" is "a great and perpetual motive of credibility" (*Dei Filius* ch. 3, Schaff).
2. The crisis was that hierarchy's failure, through its offices, in the moral matter it teaches most confidently, across countries and decades, corrected chiefly from outside: a failed [mark](#discipline-as-a-mark), not a few bad ministers.
3. "By their fruits you shall know them" (Matthew 7:16, DR).
4. So either the claim is false, or its guarantee never reaches the office's conduct, and then it gives no reason to prefer Rome to a church that confesses it can err (Article XXI; Westminster XXV.5).

It needs no clean Reformed record: its premise is Rome's claim, which the Reformed never made.

**The Catholic reply (7.3 P1–P4):** the promises as defined cover the deposit, the sacraments and definitions (*Pastor Aeternus* chs. 2 and 4), not conduct; no act claims anyone in the Church cannot sin (Trent VI can. 23; VII can. 12; **rung 1**). So the argument does not refute the claim as defined. It refutes any version that promised good governance, and it is real evidence against one [motive of credibility](#motive-of-credibility) the Church herself offered.

**Verdict:** survived, not answered. **Crux:** is the Church's claim about holiness of conduct, or about the deposit and sacraments she is given, and can that distinction survive this case? The worst available answer is the [comparative move](#comparative-move).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Abuse crisis

The sexual abuse of minors by Catholic clergy and religious, and its concealment by bishops who moved offending priests, settled quietly and kept the police out, brought to light by journalists, grand juries and commissions rather than by the Church's own processes.

**The record** is [`church-history` 9.3](../church-history/lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)'s, taken as given. 7.3 adds one official report: France's independent commission (CIASE), set up at the request of the French bishops' and religious conferences, reported on 5 October 2021. It estimated some 216,000 people in France (95 percent interval 165,000–270,000) abused as minors by clergy and religious since 1950, put the abusers at no fewer than 2,900–3,200 (about 3 percent of clergy and religious, a minimum), and found that for a very long time the Church's first reaction was to protect itself as an institution. **Weight:** an independent commission's report, **no magisterial level**; its figures are estimates.

**The structures since 2014 are acts, not arguments:** the Pontifical Commission for the Protection of Minors (2014), the February 2019 meeting, [*Vos estis lux mundi*](#vos-estis-lux-mundi), the December 2019 rescript and the revised Book VI (2021).

**Concession (7.3):** on this question the Catholic case does not win. For decades bishops failed the standard of discipline that [Article XXVI](#article-xxvi) and the Belgic Confession state, outsiders brought the facts out, and the sign Vatican I offered has been visibly damaged by the Church's own governors. If the Church's claim is true, it is true of a Church that did this. The argument the record grounds is the [argument from scandal](#argument-from-scandal).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Article XXVI

The Anglican article "Of the Unworthiness of the Ministers, which hinders not the effect of the Sacraments".

**Two halves** (English of 1571 in Schaff vol. 3, spelling modernized; cite from the Articles' own section, not the Methodist renumbering later in the volume):

1. *Anti-Donatist:* in the visible Church "the evil be ever mingled with the good, and sometime the evil have chief authority" in the ministry of word and sacraments; their ministry may still be used, and the sacraments are effectual because of Christ's institution and promise. Common ground with Augsburg VIII and Trent VII can. 12.
2. *Discipline:* "it appertaineth to the discipline of the Church, that inquiry be made of evil ministers", that they be accused by those who know their offences, and, found guilty "by just judgment, be deposed". For 7.3, the standard of discipline the bishops failed ([discipline as a mark](#discipline-as-a-mark)).

**Weight:** an Anglican formulary whose subscription varies across the Communion; **no Catholic level**.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Discipline as a mark

The Reformed teaching that church discipline against sin is one of the marks by which the true Church is known; where the argument from scandal actually bites.

The Belgic Confession art. 29 (1561; revised at Dort, 1619; Schaff's English) adds to pure preaching and pure sacraments a third mark: "if church discipline is exercised in punishing of sin". Westminster XXX.3 makes censures necessary for reclaiming offenders, deterring others, purging the leaven, and "for vindicating the honor of Christ". The Anglican [Article XXVI](#article-xxvi) closes with the duty to inquire, accuse and depose. The Lutheran Augsburg VII names only word and sacraments ([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)). The Catholic [marks](#marks-of-the-church) are one, holy, catholic, apostolic.

**Weight:** the Belgic is a doctrinal standard of the continental Reformed churches (not Lutheran); Westminster is the Presbyterian subordinate standard; **no Catholic level**.

**Why it matters (7.3):** at its strongest the argument from scandal charges not sinful ministers (that would be Donatism) but a failed mark: the hierarchy's discipline failed, through its offices, for decades.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Comparative move

The answer "it happened everywhere": that abuse elsewhere excuses, offsets or relativizes the Church's. 7.3 calls it the single worst answer available and refuses it outright.

**Four reasons:**

1. **True only as a report:** abuse is documented in families, schools, sports and other churches.
2. **Unestablished as a comparison of rates** almost everywhere ([`church-history` 9.3](../church-history/lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md): no comparable data). Where a general-population survey did compare settings, in France (CIASE, 2021), more adults had been abused as children by people connected with the Church (1.16 percent; 0.82 by clergy and religious) than in any setting outside family and friends (state schools 0.34, sports clubs 0.28). The CIASE's "just under 4 percent" of all child sexual abuse in France is a share, not a rate.
3. **Irrelevant:** the argument's premise is Rome's claim, which no club or school made.
4. **A moral failure:** it ranks the institution before the children, the ordering the CIASE condemned.

Francis, closing the February 2019 meeting, cited worldwide data and then said the evil is no less monstrous inside the Church. 7.2's counter-question (the reformers' own record) likewise tests only a claim to purity and never exculpates.

**Trap:** offering "it happened everywhere" as an established comparative fact.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Clericalism

The *Letter to the People of God*'s name for a distorted understanding of authority in the Church, fostered by priests or by laity, which splits the ecclesial body.

**What the Letter says** (Francis, 20 August 2018): clericalism, whether fostered by priests or by lay persons, causes a split in the ecclesial body that "supports and helps to perpetuate" many of the evils being condemned. **It does not call clericalism "the root"**: the Letter's one "root" is said of the thirst for power and possessions.

**Level:** the Letter carries what it restates; its judgment on clericalism is pastoral; the causes of the crisis are disputed ([`church-history` 9.3](../church-history/lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)).

**Trap:** "Francis called clericalism the root of abuse" (some sibling texts say so; 7.3 corrects it).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

### Vos estis lux mundi

Francis's motu proprio obliging clerics and religious to report sexual abuse and its cover-up.

Issued 7 May 2019 (in force 1 June 2019, *ad experimentum* for three years); definitive text 25 March 2023 (in force 30 April 2023). **Law, no doctrinal rung.**

**The other acts beside it**, all acts with no doctrinal rung: the Pontifical Commission for the Protection of Minors (chirograph, 22 March 2014); the meeting "The Protection of Minors in the Church" (21–24 February 2019); a rescript dated 6 December 2019 (published 17 December) lifting the pontifical secret from these cases (**law, no rung**); the revised Book VI of the Code (*Pascite gregem Dei*, 23 May 2021, in force 8 December 2021), placing the offence among offences against human life, dignity and freedom (can. 1398; **law, no rung**).

**How to use them:** as acts, judged by what changes, never as arguments for the Church's holiness.

**Trap:** treating *Vos estis* or the 2019 rescript as doctrine, or as proof of holiness.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)


## Arguments

The skeletons the lessons build, one per lesson's `## The argument` section, compressed to one line a premise. Each lesson's own **Mark the levels.** paragraph grades its premises; the levels are collected under [Levels of authority used in this course](#levels-of-authority-used-in-this-course). Where a Boss part asks you to produce something, the entry names the parts and stops: the reconstruction is yours.

**Arguing among Christians (Module 1)**

### Argument: Locate the dispute, then carry the larger burden

Most of what Christians confess is shared; argue where it leaks, and admit whose claim is bigger.

1. **P1.** Every tradition in the course confesses the [common inheritance](#common-inheritance), much of it in shared words.
2. **P2.** Where it leaks, the leak sits on one of the [two axes](#two-axes): authority, or interpretation.
3. **P3.** When exegesis and history do not settle a downstream dispute, each side's last appeal is its answer on an axis (canon and papacy: axis 1; justification once Trent's canons are invoked; the filioque: axis 2 as doctrine, axis 1 as the lawfulness of the addition).
4. **C1.** Locate a dispute on its axis before arguing it, and never argue the inheritance.
5. **P4.** The Catholic answer is the larger one on both axes: one deposit with a living authentic interpreter (*Dei Verbum* 10), papal definitions irreformable "of themselves" and universal ordinary jurisdiction (*Pastor Aeternus* chs. 4 and 3), Marian definitions only Rome has made.
6. **C2.** The Catholic carries the [larger burden](#burden-of-the-catholic-claim); conceding it gives up the possession ground.

*Crux:* whether the Church needs, somewhere, an organ that cannot err when it settles what belongs to the faith.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-shared-ground-and-where-the-argument-is.md)

### Argument: The terms of argument with other Christians

The doctrine permits argument with other Christians and fixes how it is done.

1. **P1.** A validly baptized Christian is incorporated into Christ and in real, [imperfect communion](#imperfect-communion) with the Catholic Church (Trent VII, *On Baptism*, can. 4; UR 3, 22).
2. **P2.** Those born into the separated communities cannot be charged with the sin of separation (UR 3, *quandoque*).
3. **P3.** The doctrine must be presented whole: no [false irenicism](#false-irenicism) (UR 11); all dogmas believed with the same faith (*Mysterium Ecclesiae* 4).
4. **P4.** Truth and fairness (UR 4), *par cum pari* (UR 9), not polemically (UR 10), in terms the other can understand (UR 11).
5. **P5.** Ecumenism removes neither the right nor the duty to propose the faith whole to those who freely wish it; it excludes [proselytism](#proselytism-and-witness), a matter of means and motives (CDF *Doctrinal Note*, 2007, §12).
6. **C.** Argument is lawful on fixed terms: a brother, never charged with the separation; his view stated as he states it; the Catholic view whole; assent sought only by truth fairly put. "Outside Christ, so win" denies P1 and P4; "a brother, so keep quiet" denies P3 and P5.

*Left open:* whether arguing is a duty, a permission or a distraction is Boss 1(d)'s evaluative question.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-what-the-church-says-about-other-christians.md)

### Argument: What history can and cannot show

The archive answers "who held what, when", never "is it revealed?", and its rules bind the Catholic first.

1. **P1.** Historical evidence is testimony to what was said, done and believed.
2. **P2.** Silence is evidence only where a record was expected and would survive, and then it shows absence from the record, not denial (the two-sided rule; see [argument from silence](#argument-from-silence)).
3. **P3.** A text is evidence for its own sense ("catholic", "papa", "sacrifice" mean what they meant then; see [anachronism](#anachronism)).
4. **P4.** One Father is a witness; a consensus must be defined, then shown across the spread (see [cherry-picked Father](#cherry-picked-father)).
5. **P5.** A historical premise is locus 10, extrinsic, unless it reports the Fathers teaching (locus 6) or the Church's practice (locus 3); a conclusion is capped by its weakest load-bearing premise ([loci and historical premises](#loci-and-historical-premises)).
6. **C.** History can refute a claim that a doctrine was always explicit, show which way a development ran, and break a factual premise; it cannot by itself prove a doctrine revealed, nor by silence disprove one.

*Crux:* can a doctrine be apostolic and not yet explicit in the first centuries' record? Jewel's challenge needs "no"; development needs "yes".

*Boss 1(a)-(b), outline only:* the de Sales and Campion passage is reconstructed in numbered premises by you; name whose argument it is, the premise that does the work, which of the four abuses it commits and avoids, each premise's locus, and the note the conclusion can carry. The four abuses and the weakest-premise rule are this lesson's; the reconstruction is not on this card.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-arguing-from-history-without-abusing-it.md)

**Against sola scriptura (Module 2)**

### Argument: What a reply to sola scriptura must answer

2.1 builds no syllogism; it lists what any Catholic reply must meet, so that the reply is aimed at the confessions and not at [solo scriptura](#solo-scriptura).

1. **Authority's source is shared.** Westminster I.4, Calvin I.7.2 and *Dei Filius* ch. 2 all deny that the Church confers it; the dispute is who identifies Scripture and settles its reading.
2. **The [canon](#canon-of-scripture).** The Formula names its rule without listing it; Westminster and Calvin identify it by the Spirit's witness (2.2).
3. **[Formal sufficiency](#material-and-formal-sufficiency).** Not that everything is explicit (I.6 allows consequence), but that Scripture interprets itself and, by the Spirit, judges (I.9-10). Material sufficiency is a Catholic option; aim at the formal claim (2.3).
4. **[Clarity](#perspicuity) as I.7 states it,** qualifiers included (2.4).
5. **Real, fallible authority.** Show why it is not enough; never pretend it is absent (4.4).

*Crux:* whether a rule that fallible bodies must identify and read can be the sole infallible rule, its certainty given by the Spirit's witness through it.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-sola-scriptura-at-its-best.md)

### Argument: The canon needs a recognizer that cannot fail

Not "who made the list?" but how the edge of the list can be held with the certainty of faith.

1. **P1.** Neither side says the Church confers canonicity (*Dei Filius* ch. 2; Westminster I.4; Calvin I.7.2).
2. **P2.** No book of Scripture lists the books of Scripture; every side knows the edge by something beyond the text: reception, Tradition, the Spirit's witness, or several.
3. **P3.** Believers who had the Spirit's witness disagreed about the edge for centuries (Hebrews, James, Revelation; the [deuterocanon](#deuterocanon); Luther's 1522 ranking against Belgic IV).
4. **P4.** Where the edge was settled, reception settled it, and reception is a church's act; the question becomes whose.
5. **P5.** Trent IV defines the books under anathema "entire with all their parts"; DV 8 teaches the canon is known through Tradition.
6. **C.** An edge held with the certainty of faith needs a recognizer that cannot fail when it defines; at the disputed edge the Reformed account rests on reception, the point at issue.

*Crux:* can a recognition carry more certainty than the body that recognizes? The Catholic's own recognizer then needs a warrant that does not presuppose it (the [circularity objection](#circularity-objection)).

*Boss 2(b), outline only:* the [self-authenticating canon](#self-authenticating-canon) is stated as its defenders state it, with what it explains that a bare appeal to the Church does not; the lesson's "The other side" gives the parts.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-the-canon-question.md)

### Argument: Materially sufficient, not formally sufficient

The Bible may hold everything; it does not settle its own reading.

1. **P1.** The New Testament hands on the apostolic teaching in two modes: word and letter (2 Thess 2:15; 1 Cor 11:2).
2. **P2.** It treats that teaching as a deposit (*parathēkē*) entrusted to teachers who will teach others (1 Tim 6:20; 2 Tim 1:14; 2:2).
3. **P3.** The Reformation's strongest text, 2 Tim 3:16-17, does not state formal sufficiency: "profitable" is not "alone", and the "holy scriptures" of 3:15 are the Old Testament.
4. **P4.** When heretics argued from the same books, the second-century Church settled readings by the [rule of faith](#rule-of-faith); Vincent holds the canon sufficient yet needing the Church's interpretation.
5. **P5.** No text of Scripture or the early Church says that after the apostles only the text is infallible.
6. **C.** Scripture may be materially sufficient; it is not formally sufficient (DV 10).

Each of P1-P3 is weak alone; the case is cumulative, rests on no claim about Pauline authorship, and shows only that formal sufficiency is unsupported. Who holds the interpreting office is Module 4's question.

*Crux:* can a post-apostolic act of interpretation share the gospel's infallibility without being inspired? The argument fails if the second-century rule never decided between rival readings, or if apostolic churches taught contrary doctrines as apostolic.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-tradition-and-what-sufficiency-means.md)

### Argument: A clear text still needs a judge

Not that Scripture is obscure, but that clarity did not settle the disputes the parties themselves called necessary.

1. **P1.** A rule of faith settles, for those who hold it, what must be believed (Westminster I.10; Luther's external clarity).
2. **P2.** By its own scope clause, the clarity thesis is tested only by disputes about what is necessary.
3. **P3.** The parties disagreed about what is necessary (Augsburg IX on baptism against Westminster XXVIII.5).
4. **P4.** They disagreed about which texts are plain (Formula, Epitome VII, against the Reformed method Westminster I.9 generalized).
5. **P5.** They used every ordinary means; inside each party a subscribed confession settled such disputes.
6. **P6.** Rome claims that office as a judge (Trent IV's second decree; DV 10).
7. **C.** Clarity about necessary things, by the ordinary means, settled neither the disputes held necessary nor what is necessary; inside each tradition a confession settled them, between traditions nothing did.

*Crux:* is a rule of faith that needs an authority to apply it still a rule of faith *alone*? Against the Catholic: can his infallible judge be identified without the circle of 2.3?

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-perspicuity-tested.md)

**Against sola fide (Module 3)**

### Argument: What a reply to sola fide must answer

3.1 states the confessions and builds no syllogism; it lists what any Catholic reply must meet.

1. **The [formal cause](#formal-cause).** The confessions deny that the righteousness that counts is infused (Westminster XI.1; Epitome III neg. 3) and refuse [double justice](#double-justice) (neg. 9), as Trent's "alone" does. Show why a righteousness God makes inherent is the ground; do not claim the confessions deny renewal.
2. **Faith.** Trust that receives, against faith as "the beginning ... the foundation, and the root" of a justice including hope and charity (Trent VI ch. 8). "Faith" and "justify" may not mean the same on both sides (3.2).
3. **[Assurance](#assurance).** The Lutheran ban on doubt and Westminster's later assurance, met separately.
4. **[Falling away](#perseverance).** The Lutherans side with Trent (VI can. 23); only Westminster contradicts it.
5. **[Merit](#merit).** The confessions require works and deny that they merit justification; state Catholic merit so the objection misses (3.4).

*Ruled out:* "Lutherans say works don't matter"; "faith alone is bare assent"; "Calvin and Luther taught the same thing"; "Protestants teach once saved, always saved".

*Crux:* whether the righteousness for which God accepts a sinner can remain Christ's, counted to him, or must be God's gift made his own.

*Boss 3(a), outline only:* the Lutheran doctrine is stated from Augsburg IV and the Formula in words a Lutheran would sign: what is imputed (and how Augsburg IV's "faith imputed" fits the Formula's "righteousness of Christ's obedience"), what faith does, what "alien" righteousness means and which Formula words carry it, what is denied about infusion and indwelling. See [sola fide](#sola-fide), [imputation](#imputation), [alien righteousness](#alien-righteousness), [Osiander controversy](#osiander-controversy).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-justification-as-the-confessions-state-it.md)

### Argument: Where Trent VI and the confessions collide, and where they pass

Four real contradictions, three verbal passings; defend the four and do not count the passings as wins.

1. **P1.** A canon condemns a proposition in its stated sense and names no one (see [contradiction and passing by](#contradiction-and-passing-by)).
2. **P2.** Inherent justice: ch. 7's single [formal cause](#formal-cause) and can. 11 against Westminster XI.1 and Epitome III neg. 3; both sides closed the middle road ([double justice](#double-justice); Epitome III neg. 9).
3. **P3.** Falling away: can. 23 against Westminster XVII.1 and XI.5, with the Lutherans on Trent's side (Augsburg XII; Smalcald III.3).
4. **P4.** [Assurance](#assurance): ch. 9's denial of a certainty of faith against Westminster XVIII.2's "infallible assurance"; cans. 13-14 condemn only requiring certainty, which XVIII.3 also denies.
5. **P5.** [Merit](#merit): can. 32 against the Apology and Westminster XVI.5, narrowly, over grace and eternal life, not reward.
6. **P6.** Passings: "justify" (absolve vs translate into grace), "faith" (assent living by charity vs [fiducial faith](#fiducial-faith)), "instrument" (baptism vs receiving faith; [faith as instrument](#faith-as-instrument)).
7. **C.** Session VI contradicts the confessions on inherent justice, assurance, merit of grace and eternal life, and (with the Reformed only) falling away; it passes them where the key word shifts.

*Crux:* beneath "inherent or imputed?", the premise Chemnitz named: can a righteousness real but imperfect in degree stand at God's judgment? (Westminster XVI.5 against Trent chs. 11 and 16.)

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-trent-session-vi-against-the-confessions.md)

### Argument: James 2 as the verdict of God on a completed faith

Every party reads James of the already justified; the live question is before God or before men.

1. **P1.** Every party reads [James 2:21-24](#readings-of-james-2) of people already justified (the Apology §125; Calvin on 2:23; Trent ch. 10 under the increase of justification).
2. **P2.** On first justification the Catholic signs Paul's exclusion: nothing preceding it, "whether faith or works", merits its grace (Trent VI ch. 8).
3. **P3.** James's verbs (*synērgei*, *eteleiōthē*, 2:22) describe something that happens to the faith, inside a frame of judgment (2:12-14); "before men" is brought to the text.
4. **P4.** Paul also knows a verdict that weighs works (Rom 2:6, 2:13, which the Apology reads of believers).
5. **P5.** Trent needs only that works done in grace preserve and increase justice and are not "merely the fruits and signs" of it (can. 24).
6. **C.** James 2:21-24 is best read as God's approving verdict on a faith completed in works; the premise to deny is the Reformed "before men". That the verdict is also an increase of justice is a further step the text supports less well.

*Crux:* is James's "justified" a verdict before God that weighs works done in faith, or a showing before men of a verdict already given? Against the Lutherans the live question moves to can. 24 and merit (3.4).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-paul-and-james.md)

### Argument: Merit inside grace misses the earning objection

One work is wholly gift and truly merit; what survives is the inherence question.

1. **P1.** Trent condemns setting gift against merit: the works of the justified are God's gifts and also true merits (VI can. 32).
2. **P2.** Merit lives inside grace and Christ's mediation: nothing before justification merits it (ch. 8); Christ's virtue precedes, accompanies and follows every good work (ch. 16); can. 33.
3. **P3.** The reward is owed to God's promise, not the work's weight (ch. 16; Aquinas, ST I-II q.114 a.1 and ad 3, a.3 ad 2).
4. **P4.** In 1999 the Church confirmed a common confession of acceptance "not because of any merit on our part" (JDDJ §15) and explained "meritorious" as a promised reward for works that remain gifts (§38).
5. **C.** The earning objection misses Trent, and both partners said so in 1999. What survives is whether the gift God crowns inheres in the justified.

*Crux:* Westminster XVI.5 and Aquinas q.114 a.3 share a premise (the good in the work is the Spirit's) and draw opposite conclusions; the premise one side must deny: a gift of God can become the recipient's own without ceasing to be God's.

*Boss 3(c), outline only:* Newman's Lecture 6 is close-read and tested against the Formula (Epitome III neg. 4; SD III.54) and Trent ch. 7 by you; the parts are named in [Lectures on Justification](#lectures-on-justification) and [created and uncreated grace](#created-and-uncreated-grace).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-joint-declaration-merit-and-reward.md)

**The Church, the papacy, and the Reformation's alternatives (Module 4)**

### Argument: The Petrine texts and the perpetuity step

The texts get you Peter singled out for a lasting need; the step from function to office is taken with tradition.

1. **P1.** The texts set singular gifts beside shared ones: binding and loosing, forgiving and retaining, the thrones go to the Twelve; the name and the keys (Matt 16:18-19), the charge to strengthen (Luke 22:32), the flock (John 21:15-17) go to Peter ([Petrine texts](#petrine-texts)).
2. **P2.** The exegetical step is widely conceded outside Catholicism (Cullmann reads the rock as Peter; Calvin IV.6.5 grants "the first among the faithful").
3. **P3.** The singular gifts answer needs that outlast Peter (the Church Hades will not overpower; brethren to strengthen; a flock to feed).
4. **P4.** Both sides grant leadership under Christ (Luke 22:26; Second Helvetic XVIII.14, 16); the key is a steward's ([key of David](#key-of-david), Isa 22:20-22; Rev 3:7). The dispute is whether Christ set one steward over the house.
5. **P5.** A charge given for a lasting need is presumptively given for as long as the need lasts: the [perpetuity argument](#perpetuity-argument), *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 2's own reasoning.
6. **C.** The texts make it reasonable to read Peter's charge as meant to continue, and strain "the same and alike in all"; they name no successor and no Rome. The step from function to office ([Petrine function and Petrine office](#petrine-function-and-petrine-office)) is 4.2's evidence.

*Crux:* can a personal commission in the Gospels found an office that continues? The Catholic owes a principled way to tell the unrepeatable parts of an apostle's commission from the permanent ones.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-petrine-texts-argued.md)

### Argument: The patristic record reaches the door of development

The first millennium proves a primacy of appeal and communion on Peter's warrant, not by itself universal ordinary jurisdiction.

1. **P1.** Rome grounded its role in Peter, never in the city (Philip at Ephesus, 431; Leo, Letter CIV.3, refusing [canon 28](#canon-28-of-chalcedon)).
2. **P2.** At decisive moments others conceded more than precedence: [Sardica](#sardica) can. 3 on retrial; "Peter has spoken through Leo" (Chalcedon); "Peter spoke through Agatho" (681).
3. **P3.** The capital explains Constantinople's rise (canons 28 and 9), not Rome's role.
4. **P4.** By 1.3's rule, early silence about jurisdiction neither denies nor attests it; Irenaeus did rebuke Victor, Cyprian defy Stephen. "Honour only" fails P1-P3; "1870 already" fails P4.
5. **P5.** The Church's own account is development (CDF 1998 commentary §11, paraphrased); Newman's notes assign burdens rather than prove.
6. **C.** The record proves a [primacy of appeal and communion](#primacy-of-appeal-and-communion) with a Petrine warrant; it does not by itself prove [universal ordinary jurisdiction](#universal-ordinary-jurisdiction). That step needs [development](#development-of-doctrine).

*Crux:* is a primacy of appeal and communion universal jurisdiction developed, or a different thing? Gregory's Letter IX.59 states the hinge.

*Boss 4(a), outline only:* the *Treatise* §12 is stated as a Lutheran would sign it (what it infers about the origin of Rome's authority, from which premise), and [Nicaea canon 6](#nicaea-canon-6) close-read (what it grants Alexandria and on what ground; what the Roman clause does and does not say; what Percival's Latin versions add). Which of the *Treatise*'s claims the canon supports is yours to say; see [Treatise on the Power and Primacy of the Pope](#treatise-on-the-power-and-primacy-of-the-pope).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-what-the-patristic-record-proves.md)

### Argument: Apostolicae Curae on the Anglican Ordinal

The lesson reconstructs Leo XIII's case (1896) and the archbishops' reply (1897); this entry names its parts only, because Boss 4(b) asks you to produce them.

1. **P1.** Orders is a sacrament conferring a character and a priesthood with power to consecrate and offer (Trent XXIII cans. 1, 3, 4): there is a definite thing to hand on.
2. **P2.** The form must signify the order conferred, since the laying on of hands signifies nothing definite by itself (*Apostolicae Curae* §24); the archbishops agree (IX).
3. **P3.** How a form's signification is fixed (§31): the premise on which the two documents divide. State it yourself (Boss 4(b)).
4. **P4.** The [Edwardine Ordinal](#edwardine-ordinal) was made by striking what spoke of sacrifice (§30); the archbishops record the removals (XVIII).
5. **P5.** The ancient forms' silence and the Ordinal's are said not to be alike (the reply to the [proves-too-much](#proves-too-much) argument, which is the lesson's concession).
6. **C.** The [defect of form](#defect-of-form) and the [defect of intention](#defect-of-intention); the Holy See's prior practice (§§7-22) confirms. Three supports, not "two grounds".

*Boss 4(b), outline only:* the three supports, the two points *Saepius Officio* ch. V says the judgment hinges on, the ch. XX argument, the unshared premise, and the genre and level of [Apostolicae Curae](#apostolicae-curae).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-apostolic-succession-and-the-validity-of-orders.md)

### Argument: Authority came back, finality did not

The Reformation replaced the papacy not with Scripture alone but with visible authorities that disclaim finality in faith.

1. **P1.** The New Testament addresses a Church that acts as one visible body: it receives letters, hears cases, expels, issues decisions "to keep" (1 Cor 1:2; Matt 18:17; 1 Cor 5:13; Acts 16:4).
2. **P2.** The company of the elect, as such, can do none of this (Calvin IV.1.2; [visible and invisible Church](#visible-and-invisible-church)).
3. **P3.** So the confessions give the visible Church real authority: keys (Westminster XXX), synods (XXXI.3), controversies (Article XX), discipline (Belgic 28-29). This is the strongest reply, and the argument grants it ([ministerial authority](#ministerial-authority)).
4. **P4.** That authority acted as authority: subscription to the Formula, deposition at [Dort](#synod-of-dort), consistories, magistrates ([royal supremacy](#royal-supremacy)).
5. **P5.** At Jerusalem (Acts 15) the apostles and elders ended a dispute about the gospel for all the churches; an authority that may err in faith can bind an office, not end such a dispute for one who reads Scripture otherwise.
6. **P6.** The [fragmentation argument](#fragmentation-argument), stated properly: when two churches bearing the marks divide over what each treats as necessary, no judgment exists that both must accept. Structure, not a head count.
7. **C.** Authority came back in confessional, synodal and royal form; finality did not.

*Crux:* can an openly fallible authority do the settling the New Testament church needed done? The Reformed deny P5 (Acts 15 was apostolic and is now Scripture); the Catholic denies that the function ended with the apostles.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-visible-church-and-what-replaced-the-papacy.md)

**Against the Orthodox case (Module 5)**

### Argument: Two filioque questions kept apart

Whether the doctrine is true and whether the addition was lawful are independent; the Catholic owes an argument for each.

1. **P1.** The two questions are independent: truth does not license an insertion; an unlawful insertion does not falsify a clause ([lawfulness of the addition](#lawfulness-of-the-addition)).
2. **P2.** [Lyons II](#lyons-ii) (*Fideli ac devota*, 1274) defines procession "as from one principle ... by a single spiration" and condemns two principles: the West condemns the two causes Photius attacks.
3. **P3.** Florence (DS 1301) teaches the Son has from the Father that the Spirit proceeds from him; *Cantate Domino* calls the Father alone "principle without principle". The [monarchy of the Father](#monarchy-of-the-father) stands.
4. **P4.** The one principle is Father and Son, not the essence ([1995 clarification](#clarification-1995), reading Lyons II through Lateran IV).
5. **P5.** Maximus ([Letter to Marinus](#letter-to-marinus)) reported that the Romans do not make the Son the Spirit's *aitia*.
6. **P6.** Ephesus forbids a *different* faith ([Ephesus canon 7](#ephesus-canon-7)); the creed of 381 had enlarged Nicaea's, and Cyril held that making one's faith plain "innovates in nothing".
7. **P7.** Florence defined the addition lawful (DS 1302) on two grounds: declaring the truth, and necessity at the time.
8. **C.** The Catholic owes the Orthodox two arguments: that the Latin doctrine is not the two-cause doctrine (P2-P5, strong), and that Florence's grounds meet Ephesus (P6-P7, weaker).

*Boss 5(a), outline only:* Lossky's charge (what the clause does to the Spirit's personal distinction and to the Church) and the Orthodox lawfulness case (the Ephesian decree, no council, Rome's long refusal) are stated by you; then the single premise a Catholic must deny to hold doctrine and lawfulness together, and the premise he may concede. The lesson's crux names both; this card does not. See [filioque](#filioque).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-filioque-two-questions-kept-apart.md)

### Argument: A universal primacy inside eucharistic ecclesiology

A universal first with real power is no category error in the East's own categories.

1. **P1.** Catholic teaching accepts the eucharistic premise: the Church is truly present in every legitimate local congregation (LG 26; UR 15) ([eucharistic ecclesiology](#eucharistic-ecclesiology)).
2. **P2.** Eucharistic ecclesiology needs a test of which altars are the one Church, and the test is communion among the churches (Zizioulas against Afanasiev).
3. **P3.** Communion has a first at every level, the universal included (Ravenna §43; Zizioulas: a universal primus is necessary).
4. **P4.** A first who serves communion must be able to act for it between councils; the first millennium's appeals were such acts (UR 14) ([primacy of appeal and communion](#primacy-of-appeal-and-communion)).
5. **P5.** A ministry whose object is each church's communion with the rest reaches into every church, as a service interior to each (*Communionis Notio* 13).
6. **C.** A universal primacy with real power is no category error; the categories exclude a branch-office primacy, and so does Catholic doctrine (LG 27). Whether "ordinary and immediate" fits P5 is 5.3's question.

*Crux:* the communion of the churches is itself one Church whose first holds, by Christ's institution, a power reaching into every church. The Catholic affirms it; the Orthodox answer that the communion is a relation among churches each whole without it.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-papal-primacy-as-the-east-reads-it.md)

### Argument: Vatican I makes no bishop a papal vicar

Read by its text and its approved reading, chapter 3 claims real power in every church without making bishops delegates.

1. **P1.** Ch. 3's canon defines a jurisdiction "ordinary and immediate" over each and all the churches ([universal ordinary jurisdiction](#universal-ordinary-jurisdiction)).
2. **P2.** The same chapter calls the bishops' power ordinary and immediate too and says the primacy protects it; LG 27: bishops are not vicars of the Roman pontiffs.
3. **P3.** The [German bishops' declaration](#german-bishops-1875) (1875) refused Bismarck's reading that bishops were the pope's instruments; Pius IX approved it (DS 3117).
4. **P4.** [Ex sese](#ex-sese-non-ex-consensu) and [reception](#reception): what the clause excludes and what reception does (Newman's 1875 postscript; Alexandria §3.7). Outline only: Boss 5(b).
5. **P5.** *Ut Unum Sint* 95 seeks a way of exercising the primacy that renounces nothing essential: the power is fixed, its form is not.
6. **C.** Vatican I makes no bishop a papal vicar; it claims a real power to act in any church, which the Catholic must defend as such, arguing that its exercise can take a first-millennium form. Not [minimalism](#minimalist-temptation): the 1875 bishops affirmed papal power "at every time and in every place" (DS 3113).

*Crux:* can one bishop hold, by divine right, ordinary power inside another bishop's church without making it less than the whole Church in its place?

*What reunion would require (the lesson's statement, compressed):* of the Orthodox, accepting a universal primate by Christ's institution with power reaching into their churches by right; of the Catholic, a first-millennium form of that power, synodal self-government untouched, while saying plainly that the definition is not negotiable.

*Boss 5(b), outline only:* the Orthodox objection from reception (what reception is, why it rather than convocation or papal confirmation makes a council [ecumenical](#ecumenical-council), why 1870 does not bind on that account), the Catholic account with each claim's level (LG 22; the Code), and the premise on which they divide.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-vatican-i-read-by-orthodox-theologians.md)

### Argument: The energies, the metaphor charge and the simplicity charge

Lossky's charge that Latin grace is created misses Catholic doctrine; the simplicity charge against Palamas is a live question no act has answered.

*First, the Latin objection* from simplicity, stated from *Firmiter* and *Cantate Domino*'s rule of relation (DS 1330). Outline only: Boss 5(c).

1. **P1.** The datum is shared: "partakers of the divine nature" (2 Pet 1:4; CCC 460), by grace, never by nature ([deification](#deification)).
2. **P2.** In Catholic doctrine the term of that share is uncreated: the Persons dwell (John 14:23), the Spirit gives himself, the blessed see the essence ([Benedictus Deus](#benedictus-deus)).
3. **P3.** [Created grace](#created-and-uncreated-grace) is the other end of the union, the change that makes the creature able to receive God; it does not stand in for him.
4. **P4.** What the Latin acts define about [divine simplicity](#divine-simplicity), and whom they name. Outline only: Boss 5(c).
5. **P5.** What Palamas's defenders deny about composition ([essence and energies](#essence-and-energies)). Outline only: Boss 5(c).
6. **C.** The metaphor charge misses Catholic doctrine (P1-P3); the simplicity charge is a live question no act has answered (P4-P5): a Catholic may press it, not announce the Church's answer.

*Crux:* the premise one side must deny, *whatever in God is uncreated and is God is the essence*; it turns on whether the Palamite "real" is the scholastic [real distinction](#real-distinction) or nearer Scotus's [formal distinction](#formal-distinction).

*Boss 5(c), outline only:* the distinction as its defenders state it, the Latin objection from simplicity, and what the magisterium has and has not said about Palamas, *Benedictus Deus* and Florence included.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-essence-and-energies.md)

### Argument: Indissolubility against oikonomia; the dead without the furniture

Where the marriage practices truly differ Rome keeps the unqualified saying; on the dead, the definitions are thinner than the dispute.

1. **P1.** Mark 10:11-12, Luke 16:18 and 1 Cor 7:10-11 forbid remarriage after separation: three witnesses of four carry no exception.
2. **P2.** Matthew 19:9's [exception clause](#exception-clause) has several readings, none defined; Trent XXIV can. 7 forbids saying the Church errs in holding the bond intact after adultery.
3. **P3.** [Nullity](#nullity-and-dissolution) finds that no bond arose; dissolution reaches only bonds not both sacramental and consummated: no power is claimed over the bond Christ called indissoluble.
4. **P4.** [Oikonomia](#oikonomia) requires the first marriage "dissolved or annulled" (Crete); Moscow grants "a church divorce": it ends, or declares ended, a real bond while both spouses live.
5. **C1.** Where the practices truly differ, a valid marriage broken afterwards, Rome keeps the unqualified saying and the East does not ([indissolubility](#indissolubility)).
6. **P5.** Both churches pray for the dead, and Mark admits a cleansing; Florence defines purgation "by purgatorial pains", naming no fire, place or treasury; Trent VI can. 30 adds the remaining debt ([state of the dead](#state-of-the-dead); [Ferrara purgatory debate](#ferrara-purgatory-debate)).
7. **P6.** The Church reads that debt as following from the nature of sin, not vengeance from without (CCC 1472), and defines no mechanism.
8. **C2.** Fire, place and treasury are not in the definition; what stands is whether something owed for forgiven sin is purged after death, and Florence's authority to say so.

*Crux:* marriage: can a consummated sacramental bond cease before death? The dead: must Trent's remaining debt be a penalty paid to justice, or can it be CCC 1472's unhealthy attachment?

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-marriage-divorce-and-the-state-of-the-dead.md)

**The contested distinctives (Module 6)**

### Argument: The Marian objections reach practice and warrant, not content

The Reformation objections hit devotion and the authority to define; the Orthodox objection hits 1854 only through inherited guilt.

1. **P1.** *Latria* is fixed by its object (God as Creator and last end), not its intensity; *hyperdulia* is the highest honour for a creature ([latria, dulia, hyperdulia](#latria-dulia-hyperdulia); ST II-II q.103 a.3-4).
2. **P2.** The line is drawn in acts: the sacrifice is offered to God alone (Trent XXII ch. 3); Nicaea II reserves *latreia* to God; "Co-redemptrix" judged always inappropriate (2025).
3. **P3.** The [one mediator](#one-mediator) of 1 Tim 2:5 is the one who gave himself a ransom (2:6), and the same paragraph commands intercessions (2:1); asking a prayer is not a second ransom.
4. **P4.** The warrant for 1854 and 1950 is Scripture read in Tradition by the Church, the authority Article VI denies: Mary is a test case of [sola scriptura](#sola-scriptura); "invented in 1950" mistakes the definition for the belief.
5. **P5.** Trent V defines that sin, not death only, passes to all, not how; [Ineffabilis Deus](#immaculate-conception) defines Mary preserved "in view of the merits of Christ" ([praeredemptio](#praeredemptio)). The dogma needs a lack she was spared, not Augustine's account of transmission.
6. **C.** The Reformation objections reach practice and the authority to define, not the [dogmas](#marian-dogmas)' content; the Orthodox objection reaches 1854 only if nothing inherited from Adam is a lack of grace, and then its quarrel is with Trent V can. 2 ([ancestral sin](#ancestral-sin)).

*Crux (Orthodox):* does the Immaculate Conception depend on the Augustinian account of original guilt?

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-mary.md)

### Argument: Invocation and veneration give no creature latria

Asking a saint's prayer is asking a friend's prayer, the friend being in heaven; honour to an image passes to the person.

1. **P1.** Asking another Christian's prayer is good, and every side grants it (Calvin III.20.19; Smalcald II.ii).
2. **P2.** The saints are alive in God (Luke 20:38); Jerome, *Against Vigilantius* 6: how much more do they pray once crowned.
3. **P3.** An act of religion is fixed by what is asked and of whom as source (Aquinas, ST II-II q.83 a.4 ad 1-2); how the saints hear is not taught ([invocation of saints](#invocation-of-saints)).
4. **P4.** Honour to an image is relative: [Nicaea II](#nicaea-ii) gives images reverence, not *latreia*; the commandment forbids images made to be adored as gods; the Incarnation gave God a face ([veneration of images](#veneration-of-images); [second commandment](#second-commandment)).
5. **P5.** [Relics](#relics) are honoured as members of Christ and temples of the Spirit (Trent XXV); Smyrna already drew Nicaea's line (*Martyrdom of Polycarp* 17-18).
6. **P6.** On images the Catholic is not alone: the Orthodox receive Nicaea II and defend icons; the charge falls on the seventh council.
7. **C.** Invocation and veneration, rightly done, give no creature *latreia*; they are the [communion of saints](#communion-of-saints) in act.

*Crux:* is invoking a saint an act of religion of a different kind from asking a friend's prayer, or the same kind toward a friend in heaven? The Reformed answer returns the dispute to the [regulative principle](#regulative-principle) and so to sola scriptura.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-saints-the-images-the-relics.md)

### Argument: The one offering applied to members still being sanctified

Hebrews proves the price complete, not the patient well.

1. **P1.** Christ's one offering is the whole ground of forgiveness; our [satisfaction](#satisfaction) is made through him (Trent XIV ch. 8; Heb 10:14).
2. **P2.** The forgiven still suffer because of their sins: Westminster XVII.3 and Calvin III.4.31 grant it; Trent XIV can. 12 asserts it.
3. **P3.** Scripture's paradigm case ties that suffering to the past sin (2 Sam 12:14): "because of", not only "in order to" ([guilt and punishment](#guilt-and-punishment)).
4. **P4.** Hebrews joins a finished offering to an unfinished sanctification (*tous hagiazomenous*, 10:14; 12:6, 10).
5. **P5.** The [keys](#power-of-the-keys) bind as well as loose (John 20:23; Trent XIV can. 15); an [indulgence](#indulgences) looses what the Church can bind. The dispute is whether binding includes imposing satisfactions on the forgiven.
6. **P6.** What is unfinished at death is finished after it, as the Church's prayer for the dead presupposes; 2 Macc 12:46 counts only for a reader who receives the [deuterocanon](#deuterocanon); Augustine allows the after-death fire as "possibly" true.
7. **C.** Satisfaction, indulgences and [purgatory](#purgatory) apply the one offering to members still being sanctified; they are not a rival payment.

*Crux:* what the suffering of the forgiven is: satisfaction owed to justice (Trent XIV ch. 8) or correction only (Calvin III.4.31); see [chastisement and satisfaction](#chastisement-and-satisfaction).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-penance-purgatory-and-indulgences.md)

### Argument: Conversion reads the words as identity

"Is" means *is*, and the ascended body stays one.

1. **P1.** The words of institution predicate identity (*touto estin to sōma mou*): "is", not "signifies" or "here is".
2. **P2.** A sentence of identity is false of two substances that both remain (Aquinas, ST III q.75 a.2): union makes "is" mean "is with", [metonymy](#sacramental-metonymy) "is called".
3. **P3.** The New Testament speaks of the body itself as given (1 Cor 11:27; John 6:54-58, *trōgō*).
4. **P4.** The earliest witness is realist and theory-free (Ignatius, *Smyrnaeans* 7; Justin, *1 Apology* 66); at Corbie [Paschasius and Ratramnus](#paschasius-and-ratramnus) both said "truly" and "figure".
5. **P5.** The body is present [by way of substance](#presence-by-way-of-substance), not in a place (Trent XIII ch. 1; Aquinas q.76 a.5).
6. **C.** [Transubstantiation](#transubstantiation) reads the words as identity, fits the realist witness, and answers the Reformed objection on its own terms.

*Crux (against the Lutherans):* can "this is my body" be true of bread without a change in what the bread is? The [sacramental union](#sacramental-union) owes an account of what single thing "this" names. *Against the Reformed:* whether a body can be present in its substance without being in a place.

*Boss 6(a), outline only:* Calvin's two restrictions and the Formula's four reasons, each as its tradition would sign it; the single premise on which Reformed and Lutheran divide over the ascended body (not what "the right hand" means); whether Catholic doctrine needs either premise. See [ubiquity](#ubiquity), [spiritual presence](#spiritual-presence), [modes of presence](#modes-of-presence).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-the-real-presence.md)

### Argument: One sacrifice made present, not repeated

"Once" excludes another offering, not the one offering made present.

1. **P1.** Trent begins from Christ offering himself once on the cross: no Catholic argument may make the cross incomplete ([once for all](#once-for-all)).
2. **P2.** Hebrews' priest always lives to intercede (7:25), appears for us now (9:24), and must "have some thing to offer" (8:3): Hebrews forbids a second sacrifice, not a present priestly act. Argued, not given: Reformed exegetes read 8:3 of the cross.
3. **P3.** The Church offered the Eucharist early, everywhere, undisputed (1 Cor 10:16-21; Heb 13:10; *Didache* 14; Justin, *Dialogue* 41; Irenaeus IV.17.5; Cyprian Ep. 63.17). By 1.3's rules this attests an offering, not Trent's doctrine in Trent's terms ([anachronism](#anachronism)).
4. **P4.** Trent: the same victim, the same offerer by the ministry of priests, "the manner alone of offering being different"; the one sacrifice [made present](#re-presentation) (*repraesentaretur*).
5. **P5.** "[Propitiatory](#propitiatory)" names what the offering obtains for the contrite (ch. 2), which meets the Apology's charge about [ex opere operato](#ex-opere-operato) without faith.
6. **C.** The Mass is a true and propitiatory [sacrifice](#sacrifice-of-the-mass), neither a second sacrifice nor a repetition.

*Crux:* is a re-presentation a distinct offering? Count by acts of offering and every Mass is a new oblation; count by victim, principal offerer and continuing self-offering and it is the one offering made present.

*Boss 6(b), outline only:* Article XXXI is close-read beside Trent XXII ch. 2 with can. 3 and [Tract 90](#tract-90) §9: what the Article condemns (weigh "sacrifices", "in the which it was commonly said", "remission of pain or guilt" against its first sentence), Newman's reading and its words, and whether Trent's offering falls under the Article on each reading. See [Thirty-Nine Articles](#thirty-nine-articles).

*Lessons:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Argument: Development has falsifiers

Development is not unfalsifiable in principle; its falsifiers are narrow, and excess is assigned to devotion.

1. **P1.** Every party accepts some later formulation (Mozley's explanation; Westminster I.6's "good and necessary consequence"; the Orthodox Nicaea II and the Palamite councils): the dispute is which later statements are the same faith.
2. **P2.** Newman names his own refutation: a teaching that contradicts and reverses is "certainly corrupt" (*Essay* ch. 5 §6), and a Church that contradicted her own enunciations would shatter the hypothesis (ch. 3 §2) ([Newman notes](#newman-notes)).
3. **P3.** The Church binds herself to the same falsifier: *Dei Filius* ch. 4 with can. IV.3, "one and the same sense".
4. **P4.** Excess is real and judged in devotion and practice (LG 67; Newman to Pusey; Trent abolishing the alms-questors).
5. **P5.** The [argument from silence](#argument-from-silence) proves less than either side wants (1.3).
6. **C.** [Development](#development-of-doctrine) is falsifiable in principle: contradiction and reversal in doctrine; excess belongs to devotion.

*Crux:* can any possible evidence count, on Newman's account, as corruption in a defined doctrine? Against the critic, the "same sense" rule applied where it cost something (1950 declined to define Mary's death). See [Mozley objection](#mozley-objection), [deep in history](#deep-in-history).

*Boss 6(c), outline only:* Newman's own argument from silence (*Essay* ch. 4 §3) is reconstructed by you in numbered premises, with the premise that carries it, what it shows and cannot show, an application to the least-attested distinctive, and the strongest Protestant reply on the Trinity analogy.

*Lessons:* [6.6](lessons/06-06-development-or-corruption.md)

**The hard history (Module 7)**

### Argument: Institutional error falsifies no definition

The distinction answers "your dogma is false"; it does not answer "your Church did evil", and never could.

1. **P1.** [Infallibility](#infallibility) attaches to a kind of act: *Pastor Aeternus* ch. 4's conditions; councils and the ordinary universal magisterium share it; can. 749 §3, nothing is defined unless manifestly so.
2. **P2.** [Indefectibility](#indefectibility) promises that the Church lasts holding faith and sacraments and cannot err *in credendo*; it promises no one impeccability.
3. **P3.** No Galileo act was a definition: the consultors advised; the [1616 Index decree](#index-decree-of-1616) joined a non-definitive doctrinal censure to book bans; the 1633 sentence was a court's judgment ([Galileo affair](#galileo-affair)).
4. **P4.** Coercion of heretics was law (*Ad extirpanda*), theology (Aquinas, ST II-II q.11 a.3) and once a papal censure ([Exsurge Domine](#exsurge-domine) prop. 33); no act defined it, and [Dignitatis Humanae](#dignitatis-humanae) teaches the contrary principle ([Inquisition](#inquisition)).
5. **C.** The record convicts the Church's tribunals of doctrinal error and grave wrong, but falsifies no definition, and the Church never ceased to hold the faith ([argument from institutional error](#argument-from-institutional-error)).

*Crux:* can "the Church" of the promise err in a judgment of this kind without the promise failing? The [retreat objection](#retreat-objection) denies that the conditions were fixed independently of the failures; the 1633 court's word "defined" is the critic's best evidence.

*Boss 7(a), outline only:* Article XXI and Westminster XXV.5 and XXXI.4 are close-read by you (what each says about the capacity of councils and churches to err, in what matters, on what ground, with what consequence), with what XXV.5's last sentence affirms, and which claims the Catholic distinction contradicts or can grant, each Catholic claim with its level.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-inquisition-and-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Argument: The grievances justified reform, not separation

A corrupt Church can still be the Church; whether Rome ceased to be the Church turns on the doctrinal charges.

1. **P1.** Most disciplinary grievances were true, by Catholic testimony: the 1537 commission's report and Trent's own reform decrees ([Reformation grievances](#reformation-grievances); [pluralism and commendam](#pluralism-and-commendam)).
2. **P2.** Trent answered with law: residence, no plural sees or benefices, seminaries, no alms-collectors and no gain from indulgences. Changeable rules, changed.
3. **P3.** The doctrines under attack were defined separately and kept: the Mass propitiatory (XXII can. 3), the power to grant indulgences (XXV), no divine precept of both kinds (XXI can. 1) ([disciplinary and doctrinal](#disciplinary-and-doctrinal)).
4. **P4.** Conduct does not unchurch: a minister in mortal sin still confers the sacrament (VII can. 12); the Church is holy from Christ's gifts, a [mixed body](#corpus-permixtum); the confessions agree, rejecting [Donatism](#donatism).
5. **C.** The justified grievances justified reform, not separation; the doctrinal charges (Modules 3 and 6) decide the rest, including the charge that doctrine bred the abuse.

*The counter-question* (Brandenburg's sees, Manz, Servetus) tests only a claim to be purer and never exculpates Rome.

*Crux:* was the doctrine of indulgences a cause of the abuse, or only its occasion?

*Boss 7(c), outline only:* the [Consilium de emendanda ecclesia](#consilium-de-emendanda-ecclesia) is close-read by you: the root it names, the words that make it a charge against Roman practice, which grievances it concedes and which doctrine the indulgence clause leaves untouched, its authorship (the commission's membership) and kind, the distinction in one sentence, and the objection that it is too convenient.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-reformations-grievances.md)

### Argument: The argument from scandal refutes no defined claim, and is survived, not answered

The record refutes any version of the claim that promised good governance, and counts as real evidence against one motive of credibility the Church herself offered.

*First, the answer refused.* The [comparative move](#comparative-move) ("it happened everywhere") is refused outright: unestablished as a comparison of rates almost everywhere, irrelevant to an argument whose premise is Rome's claim, and a moral failure that ranks the institution before the children.

1. **P1.** As defined, the Church claims the deposit, the sacraments and the apostolic ministry, that she will last holding them, and that under stated conditions her definitions cannot err (*Pastor Aeternus* chs. 2, 4).
2. **P2.** No act claims that anyone in her cannot sin (Trent VI can. 23; VII can. 12).
3. **P3.** The holiness *Dei Filius* ch. 3 offers as a sign is the Church's, given by Christ and read off her whole history; it was never "our bishops are good men" ([motive of credibility](#motive-of-credibility)).
4. **P4.** The record shows grave sin committed through the offices for decades, but no definition made in error and no sacrament voided; the teaching the bishops broke was the Church's own.
5. **C.** The [argument from scandal](#argument-from-scandal) does not refute the Catholic claim as defined; it refutes any version that promised good governance, and is real evidence against one motive of credibility ([abuse crisis](#abuse-crisis)).

*Crux:* is the Church's claim about her holiness of conduct, or about the deposit and sacraments she is given, and can that distinction survive this case? The critic presses *Dei Filius* ch. 3.

*Capstone:* Boss 7(d) is evaluative and any verdict passes; an answer that does not engage the abuse crisis on its own terms fails.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-abuse-crisis-and-the-argument-from-scandal.md)

## Concessions

Every lesson names one concession before it argues: the thing the other side has right, stated flatly. Compressed here from each lesson's **Concession.** paragraph; the lesson has the full wording and sources.

| Module | What the Catholic case concedes | Lessons |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The Catholic claim is the larger one and carries the [larger burden](#burden-of-the-catholic-claim); on the common inheritance the other traditions hold it as fully as Catholics, often in the same words. | [1.1](lessons/01-01-shared-ground-and-where-the-argument-is.md) |
